"Then that belongs in our working relationship. Not as evidence. As a possible distortion of your threshold."

Daniel nodded.

The private line did not suddenly become part of the case.

But the way he was handling it was no longer purely private.

"Then separate it cleanly," she said. "Case and source."

Daniel opened two new entries in the case file.

For the first, he set the status to **substantiated lead for review**.

For the second he wrote only:

**Source unknown. Proximity of access unclear. Separate risk.**

He did not quote the private line there. He noted only that there was a personal reference involving his private life and that it had been preserved separately.

Then he saved the two entries separately.

Lena's three-column sheet remained on the table.

Claim. Verification. Result.

For the source itself, there was still nothing that justified trust.

Only truth.

## 10

Markus Heller arrived four minutes late and did not apologize.

He set a thin folder on the table, shook Berg's hand, and took the empty chair beside Lena.

"You're Reuter?"

"Yes."

"Heller."

Nothing more.

Daniel knew the name. Coordination, special analysis, interagency threat assessments. One of those people whose name appeared on distribution lists before you ever met them in person.

Before Heller arrived, Berg had asked the same question twice.

"What exactly do you want?"

Daniel had given the same answer both times.

"If the courier passes the item on, I don't want to wait until tomorrow to find out where it went."

Lena had folded her hands on the table.

"That's an objective. Not a measure."

"Access to the service provider's premises and vehicles if the case ends up there."

"On what basis?"

"The source predicted the pickup correctly. Billing party, wrong unit, time window."

"And the service provider?"

"That's where the route leads."

"That makes it the next location. Not the offender."

Berg had said nothing.

Now he slid the documents toward Heller.

"Daniel wants to move faster at the second location."

Heller skimmed the first page.

"What second location?"

"The courier from this afternoon," Daniel said. "The pickup from Warehouse C went to a technical service provider in the south of the city. They work several major events and have temporary access permissions for delivery areas."

"Legitimate permissions?"

"Yes."

"And the contents?"

"Unknown."

"Packaging?"

"Black hard-shell case."

"Size?"

Daniel indicated it roughly with his hands.

Heller did not look impressed.

Tools, measuring equipment, replacement parts—any of it was possible. So was something dangerous.

Heller turned the page.

"Direct link to a threat?"

Daniel pointed to the page with the anonymous message.

"The source predicted the pickup."

"That wasn't my question."

His tone was not sharp. Almost friendly.

Daniel leaned back.

"The pickup was booked under the wrong unit and paid for by the tenant in Unit 17."

"That explains the pickup. Not a planned attack."

"Correct."

Heller looked at Lena.

"What do you want exactly?"

Daniel answered himself.

"Search the service provider's premises and vehicles before anything moves on tonight."

Lena shook her head slightly.

"We don't have enough for that."

Berg looked at her. "Even with the time window?"

"Time pressure doesn't replace the missing link."

"If the tip turns out to be right tomorrow, that won't help us either."

"If it's wrong, we've just searched an ordinary service provider because an anonymous sender told us a story."

Berg said nothing.

Heller turned back to the delivery address.

"Who saw the case?"

"Colleagues on site. Black hard-shell case. Normal size."

"What was inside?"

"Unknown."

"Who accepted it?"

"An employee of the service provider."

"Did he do anything that doesn't fit his job?"

Daniel was silent for a moment.

"No. The case hasn't been opened or passed on."

"Did the source claim the case was dangerous?"

Daniel looked at the message.

"No."

Heller waited a second.

Daniel understood.

He had made the case the center of the story himself because it was the most visible movement out of the warehouse.

"The message only says the delivery is the beginning," he said.

"Exactly."

Daniel liked that Heller did not correct him. He simply left the gap in place.

Heller nodded.

"Then we have an odd booking, an anonymous tip containing genuine advance knowledge, and a legitimate service provider accepting a case."

"Plus the manipulated exterior door at the warehouse."

"Or a door someone propped open."

Daniel looked at him.

"Yes."

Heller put the folder down.

"What is the harmless version if everything you know for certain is still true?"

Daniel did not answer immediately.

Berg looked at him.

Lena did not. She knew the question.

"A retailer orders ordinary goods," Daniel said. "The courier job is assigned incorrectly or routed through the neighboring unit for convenience. Someone props an exterior door open because they don't want to use the access fob every time. A technical service provider accepts a hard-shell case because that's part of its business."

"And the source?" Heller asked.

"Knows internal or semi-internal details."

"Which is a separate problem."

"Yes."

"But not evidence for the story the source builds from them."

Daniel looked at him.

"No."

Heller let the harmless version sit for a moment.

Then he asked, "What would have to happen for you to discard it?"

This time Daniel answered faster.

"A second point that doesn't come from the same chain."

"More specific."

"A vehicle from the service provider's environment appearing somewhere ordinary business doesn't explain. Access that doesn't fit normal activity. A person or place that independently already matters in a relevant case."

"And if the case turns out to contain nothing but equipment?"

"Then the harmless version gets stronger."

"Even though the source knew about the pickup?"

Daniel looked at him.

"Yes."

Heller nodded.

"Good. Then you have a threshold, not just impatience."

Berg leaned back.

"The threshold costs time."

"Of course," Heller said. "Every threshold costs time. Otherwise it wouldn't be one."

He turned the folder back toward Daniel.

"The question isn't whether you want to move quickly. Everyone here does. The question is which false explanation you still have to take seriously before you interfere with someone's rights."

Daniel did not like the sentence.

It was not wrong. What bothered him was that it sounded like something Daniel himself would have said.

The alternative explanation was not comfortable. But it held up.

That bothered Daniel more than a formal refusal would have.

Heller set the folder down.

"I'm not trying to minimize this. Quite the opposite. If the source genuinely has advance access to details like these, that may be a larger problem than the individual tip."

Berg folded his arms.

"But?"

"No but." Heller looked at Daniel. "The gap is real. If we pretend it isn't, tomorrow we'll have two problems instead of one."

Daniel knew what he meant.

He just did not like that Heller was right.

"What can you run in parallel?" Heller asked.

Daniel named the open points. Company connections. Vehicles. Personnel, as far as legally and operationally justified. Comparison with existing threat information. Queries to the offices responsible for the service provider's event access.

Heller let him finish.

"Which of those requires the source as its basis?"

Daniel went through the list again.

"None of them. Once the service provider is established from the actual courier route."

"Good."

"It only makes things slower."

"Yes."

Heller said it without consolation.

Then he tapped the company connection.

"That can run in parallel. Vehicles too, as far as they follow from the real service-provider link. Event access through the responsible office. Don't wait for one question to be answered before you even ask the next."

Daniel looked at Lena.

She gave a slight nod.

It was not a special route.

Just better organization.

Heller continued.

"I can speed up the cross-state checks. No special measure. Just see whether the same company, address, or vehicle has already appeared somewhere else on a clean basis."

"How fast?" Berg asked.

"If the systems and the people cooperate: today."

"And if they don't?"

"Then tomorrow. As always."

For the first time Heller smiled briefly.

It was neither a likable nor an unlikable smile. Just tired.

Daniel took the folder back.

"If we go into those premises on the current facts," Heller said, "and all we find is ordinary event equipment, what do we explain afterward?"

"That we acted on a specific threat tip."

"From whom?"

Daniel was silent.

"Exactly."

Heller did not stand up to make the point bigger. He remained seated.

"And if the tip is right and we don't go in tonight?" Berg asked.

Heller looked at him.

"Then later we'll ask ourselves whether we could have moved faster."

"That's not an answer."

"It is. Just not a comfortable one."

Daniel looked at the clock.

Heller noticed.

"Time is a risk," he said. "So is a thin basis. You can't pretend one of them can be argued away."

Daniel asked, "What if we simply keep the case under observation?"

Heller nodded. "Then you may gain a connection. Or lose it. Both belong to the decision."

"So your recommendation?"

"I'm not recommending that you turn my role into an authorization."

Daniel held his gaze.

Heller continued calmly.

"I would run everything that follows independently from the real route in parallel. I would not turn the case into evidence. And if a second solid anchor appears, reassess."

It was almost exactly the answer Daniel himself would have given a younger investigator.

He no longer liked it as much as he would have a week earlier.

Daniel stated the alternative explanation again, more briefly this time.

"A retailer orders normal goods. A courier job is assigned incorrectly. An exterior door is propped open for convenience. A service provider accepts a hard-shell case because that's what the business does."

Berg looked at him.

"And a source knows all of it in advance."

"That's why we keep checking."

Heller stood.

"Exactly."

At the door, he stopped once more.

"Reuter."

Daniel looked up.

"If you get a second solid point, call me directly. Not through three distribution lists."

"Why?"

"Because we just spent this meeting talking about time being a problem."

Then he left.

Berg watched him go.

"Unpleasantly reasonable."

Lena gathered her documents.

"The worst kind."

Daniel did not answer.

He was not angry about the decision.

That almost bothered him more.

Heller had not denied him anything Daniel himself could have cleanly defended on the current facts.

He had only named the same boundary Daniel had understood as one of his own strengths a few days earlier.

This time it felt slower.

After Heller left, Daniel opened a new note.

Lena saw him.

"What are you writing?"

"What he actually offered."

"Why?"

Daniel entered three lines.

**Parallel regular checks. Direct coordination route. Reassessment on second anchor.**

Underneath he wrote:

**No additional authority. No authorization to search premises. No claim that the source is trustworthy.**

Lena read over his shoulder.

"You're protecting Heller against your own memory."

"I'm preserving the record."

"Of course."

Daniel let the objection stand.

He knew how quickly later harm could change the memory of an earlier conversation. A man who had named a boundary could later become someone who had forced it on them. A recommendation to parallelize the work could become a refusal to act faster.

Neither would be true.

Jonas came back into the room.

"What's the working rule now?"

Daniel turned the screen toward him.

"Everything supported by the real service-provider link runs in parallel. No measure based on the hard-shell case alone. If a second independent point appears, we decide again."

"And Heller?"

"Coordinates if we need him."

"You trust him?"

Daniel shook his head.

"I have no reason to distrust him."

Lena looked at him.

"Also not the same thing."

"No."

Daniel saved the note.

That did not remove Heller from the story. But it did not make him larger simply because Daniel was angry about lost time.

For Daniel, that mattered more at the moment than it did for Heller.

Daniel drew another line beneath the note.

"What would Heller have had to say for me to treat him as an obstacle?"

Jonas thought about it.

"That we shouldn't run anything in parallel at all."

"He didn't."

"Or that the source is worthless."

"He didn't say that either."

Lena said, "Or that the missing basis wouldn't matter as long as the risk sounded serious enough."

Daniel nodded.

Heller had not said that either.

He had placed two risks beside each other and left Daniel with the decision.

Daniel saved the record with a timestamp.

If his assessment changed later, at least it would remain clear what it had been based on at the time.

## 11

At 7:06 p.m., Daniel still did not have a second solid point.

Instead, six unanswered requests were sitting on his screen.

One company connection was clean and ordinary.

A vehicle check had produced nothing.

The query concerning a former employee of the service provider was still open.

At the event coordination office, the responsible person had called back only to say that she did not manage the access permissions herself.

A second office had taken over.

Answer: pending.

Daniel looked at the time and hated that he did.

6:42 p.m.

6:51 p.m.

7:02 p.m.

The same open list every time.

Jonas had checked two more publicly available company connections. Nothing.

The black hard-shell case had arrived at the technical service provider and, according to the report, remained there.

No unusual contact.

No rushed handoff.

No person suddenly disappearing.

It almost looked as if Heller's harmless version intended to win.

Daniel called the office managing event access once more. An employee searched for the service provider in the system and found it immediately.

"Registered normally," she said. "Several vehicles, several people. Nothing blocked."

"Any changes today?"

"I'd have to check. I'll get back to you tonight."

Daniel thanked her. He knew explaining his impatience would not make her work faster.

At 7:12 p.m., Jonas called.

"We have a problem."

Daniel heard voices in the background.

"Where are you?"

"Operations room. Report from Neukölln. The technical service provider."

Daniel stood.

"What happened?"

"A vehicle drove through the entrance to one of their depots. The security guard tried to stop it."

"And?"

Jonas was silent one second too long.

"He's in the hospital."

Daniel was already moving down the corridor.

"Driver?"

"Gone. Van abandoned on the grounds."

"Plate?"

"Not the gray van from the warehouse."

Daniel stopped for a moment.

"Then what?"

"Blue van. Rental vehicle."

That did not fit.

The sentence changed everything and nothing at the same time.

If the gray van was not involved, Daniel's most visible connection had been wrong.

If the depot still belonged to the service provider's environment, the warning had not simply disappeared.

Five people stood in front of two monitors in the operations room. One colleague was on the phone with the depot. Someone else was pulling vehicle records. A fragmented follow-up to the initial report came over a speaker.

A colleague read out the first note: barrier damaged, security employee injured, vehicle abandoned, driver fled.

Daniel did not let anyone summarize the initial report for him.

"Who is on the phone with the depot right now?"

The colleague pointed at her handset. "Shift supervisor."

"Speaker."

She switched it over.

A man's voice came through, too fast while still trying to sound factual.

He had not seen the impact. Only heard it. When he came out of the building, the van was standing at an angle behind the damaged barrier. The security guard was on the ground. The driver was already gone.

"Did anyone see whether he was hit or fell while trying to get out of the way?" Daniel asked.

"No. Not clearly."

"Did the driver speak to him first?"

"I don't know."

"Did the security guard say anything afterward?"

A short pause.

"Not to me."

Daniel stopped the questions there.

More pressure would not turn the absence of an eyewitness into a better one.

A second phone brought the first update from the hospital. Multiple injuries, including fractures. At present, no life-threatening condition.

Daniel did not copy the sentence down.

He did not need a detailed medical file to understand that the until-now abstract cost of waiting had become a specific person.

At the same time, he still did not know whether faster action would have prevented this exact harm.

Both had to remain true at once.

Jonas pulled the first still image of the abandoned vehicle onto the monitor.

Blue.

Daniel looked at the front first, then the plate, then the color again.

Not gray.

The story he had built around the hard-shell case and the gray van did not collapse because of some major piece of evidence.

Only because the vehicle was different.

That was enough.

"Take the gray van out of the operational chain," Daniel said.

Jonas looked at him. "Completely?"

"As the main lead. If it comes back independently later, it comes back."

Daniel said the sentence and felt how much easier it would have been to keep the gray van somewhere in the picture anyway.

Just in case.

That was exactly what he did not do.

Daniel had them show him the origin of the new report.

"The depot itself?"

"Yes. Initial report from the shift supervisor. Patrol officers are there."

"Not the source?"

"Not the source."

He did not really need the sentence. It still mattered that the new anchor had emerged outside the anonymous message.

Jonas immediately pushed a printout toward him.

"The van first appeared at the service provider today at 4:41 p.m. From everything we have so far, it was never at Warehouse C."

"Certain?"

"Front cameras, known access points, the yard footage. Can't prove a complete negative, but no hit."

"The black case?"

"Still there."

Daniel looked at him.

"Where?"

"In the service provider's office. Unopened, according to an employee. The officers are securing it."

"Who confirmed that?"

"Employee on site and the first patrol."

That broke apart the simplest story.

Case from the warehouse. Service provider. Vehicle. Threat.

Too clean.

And wrong.

Lena came in, still holding her phone.

"The security guard is responsive. Multiple fractures, not life-threatening."

Daniel looked at her.

"How did it happen?"

"He approached the driver's side when the van stopped at the barrier. The van suddenly accelerated through. He was hit from the side and pushed against the entrance structure."

No dramatic description.

Only enough to give Daniel an image he did not need.

"Intentional?"

"Unclear. Driver's gone."

"Name?"

Lena gave it to him.

Daniel did not write it down. It was already in the case file.

"Family notified?"

"As far as I know, yes."

He asked nothing more.

The man was not there to illustrate Daniel's decision morally. He was injured. That was enough.

Berg followed Lena in.

"What do we have in the van?"

"No complete search result yet," Jonas said. "But two access badges that don't belong to the vehicle. One has been reported lost. The second appears to be forged."

"Appears?" Daniel asked.

"Number doesn't match the issue sequence. Final confirmation is running."

Daniel looked at the screen.

A photo of the blue van. Front-end damage. The barrier bent beside it.

The vehicle looked ordinary. Blue paint, light scratches, nothing matching the weight it had acquired over the last three minutes.

"Event?"

Lena nodded. "Both badges belong to the service-provider environment for a major event this weekend."

"Which one?"

She named it.

Daniel knew the name. Everyone in Berlin did.

He felt his mind start filling in the missing pieces immediately.

Warehouse. Courier. Service provider. Badges. Van. Major event.

A line.

Maybe.

He moved to the second monitor.

"What connects the driver to Unit 17?" he asked.

Jonas shook his head. "Nothing yet. Nothing to the courier either. The van was at the service provider for just under twenty minutes today. Rental agreement under false contact details, identity of the renter still open."

"So not the employee who accepted the case."

"Not on what we know now."

Daniel looked at the two vehicle images.

Gray from the warehouse environment.

Blue from the depot.

He had made the gray vehicle larger because it was the first thing he could see.

The source had never claimed it was the decisive vehicle.

The reply from event coordination appeared on Daniel's screen.

**No unusual changes to regular service-provider access permissions.**

Ten minutes earlier, that sentence would have reassured him.

Now he knew that two badges from exactly that environment had been found in an abandoned vehicle.

Clean data could be reassuring in the wrong context too.

Berg looked at Daniel.

"The warning?"

Daniel took out his phone and opened the message.

**The target is not at the warehouse. This is about the logistics of a public event in Berlin.**

That was true.

Or true enough.

The part Daniel had focused on was not.

The hard-shell case apparently was not the decisive object. The gray van was not the vehicle involved in the incident. The company in Unit 17 was still only a connection, not an established offender.

"We read the source wrong," he said.

"Or the source was imprecise," Lena said.

"Both are possible."

"Or deliberately broad," Berg said.

Daniel looked at him.

"Also possible."

Jonas looked up from the screen.

"Does that matter right now?"

"Yes."

"A man is in the hospital."

"That's why it matters."

Jonas started to say something, then stopped.

Daniel stepped closer to him.

"If we now start treating every part of the warning as confirmed because one part was real, tomorrow we'll be worse than we are today."

Jonas looked at the photo of the van.

"And if we separate things for too long again?"

Daniel did not answer immediately.

That was the new sentence in the room.

A colleague at the other end called for Berg.

Confirmation on the second badge came in.

Forged.

It was not enough for the whole chain. It was enough for more than a traffic accident.

Daniel sat at an open workstation and pulled up the timeline.

2:20 p.m. Pickup.

4:41 p.m. Blue van at service provider.

6:53 p.m. Van enters depot.

7:01 p.m. Security guard injured.

Between them he added their inquiries.

3:03 p.m. Request to event-access office.

3:26 p.m. Forwarded to second office.

4:12 p.m. First partial response.

4:41 p.m. Blue van at service provider.

Part of the lost time had not been a legal problem.

Only sequence.

They had had almost five hours between the pickup and the incident.

Five hours with indications, not proof.

That was a difference.

The difference was now lying in a hospital with multiple fractures.

Daniel looked at the 3:03 line.

That did not suddenly make the decision from the meeting with Heller wrong.

On the facts they had then, Daniel could still have defended it.

That did not help the security guard.

For a moment he imagined the man arriving for work that afternoon without being part of any threat situation. No longer than that. Just long enough to understand what the word consequence meant here.

Then Daniel pushed the image away.

He did not need guilt larger than the facts.

He needed a better process.

Daniel closed the timeline again.

"Everything we can legally pull on the blue van, run it in parallel now," he told Jonas. "Not one after another."

"Registered owner, rental, contacts?"

"Everything the current vehicle itself supports. No expansion just because of the message."

Jonas nodded.

"On it."

Daniel reached for his phone.

Heller had given him a direct number.

He called.

While it rang, Daniel looked once more at the front-end damage on the van.

The warning had not been entirely right.

Only right enough that someone was now in the hospital.

## 12

"If you stare at the clock long enough, it won't slow down."

Jonas stood in Daniel's doorway.

It was after midnight.

Daniel had opened the timeline again.

This time he was using a different view than earlier that evening. On the left were the facts they had actually had at each point in time.

On the right, separated from them, everything they had learned only later.

"I'm not looking at the clock."

"You're looking at times."

"That's different."

Jonas came in and set two cups on the desk.

Daniel took one.

The coffee was too hot and too weak.

"What do we have?" Jonas asked.

Daniel turned the monitor toward him.

"Two places where we lost time without the legal situation forcing us to."

Jonas leaned forward.

Daniel pointed to the first.

"We waited for the landlord's response before checking the service provider in parallel."

"Because we didn't know whether the tip was real."

"We could still have checked the public registry and contract information."

"Would that have given us the blue van?"

"Probably not."

Jonas looked at him.

"Then why mark it?"

"Because it was still avoidable delay."

Daniel pointed to the second.

"The query on event access went through two offices one after the other."

"Because the first office was responsible."

"And the second had the data."

"Would parallel requests have prevented what happened at the depot?"

Daniel looked at the timestamps.

"I don't know."

"So maybe nothing would have changed."

"Correct."

Jonas took a sip and grimaced.

"This coffee should be illegal."

"Almost."

Daniel marked both points with a different symbol from the actual legal limits.

Not *mistake*.

**Friction.**

Beside **Friction**, Daniel added a second column.

**Visible at the time?**

Jonas read the heading.

"Now we're really having fun."

Daniel ignored him.

For the first point, he wrote **yes**. The public company check could have run in parallel with the landlord inquiry without requiring any new assumption.

For the second, he also wrote **yes**. They had already known the event data sat with another office; they had simply followed the chain sequentially.

Then Daniel came to a point he had marked in red earlier that evening: their failure to focus sooner on the technical service provider.

He stopped.

"What?" Jonas asked.

"We didn't know that then."

"We knew the courier route."

"We knew there was a legitimate recipient and a case."

Daniel removed the red marker.

"Knowing what happened at the depot makes it look more inevitable in hindsight than it was."

Jonas nodded slowly.

Daniel wrote **no** in the second column.

That made the review less satisfying.

Two organizational delays remained. Other supposed failures disappeared as soon as he took away the knowledge they had gained afterward.

That was exactly how Daniel wanted it.

If he took a bad outcome and built a past in which the right decision had supposedly been obvious all along, he would learn nothing.

At the bottom of the page, he wrote:

**Change only what could already have been organized differently with the information available at the time.**

"What's the difference?" Jonas asked.

"A mistake means we made the wrong decision then."

"And friction?"

"That we could have organized the same defensible path faster."

Jonas nodded.

Daniel opened a new list.

Event-coordination contact.

Direct landlord contact.

Readiness for rapid vehicle and company checks within the permitted scope.

No special authority. No shortcuts that required a different legal basis.

Only routes they would not have to rediscover every time.

Beside each item, he added an owner.

None of them belonged to Daniel alone. That mattered to him.

Then he took the previous day's sequence and ran it again with the new order.

Pickup confirmed.

In parallel: check service provider through open sources.

In parallel: contact event coordination.

In parallel: check known vehicles arising from the real service-provider connection.

"How much do we gain?" Jonas asked.

Daniel shifted the timestamps.

"If everyone responds the way they did yesterday? Maybe forty minutes."

"Not five hours."

"No."

"Forty minutes might not have protected the security guard either."

"Correct."

Daniel did not delete the simulation.

"Then the result is still forty minutes."

Jonas looked at him.

"You're not looking for blame."

"No."

"Then what?"

"Time we don't have to waste again."

"And the next tip?"

"What about it?"

"How certain does it have to be?"

Daniel held the cup in both hands.

A week earlier, he would have had an answer.

Not a percentage. Not a formula. But at least a standard.

Now he thought of Ahrens from the first case. Event technician. Photographs at the loading door. Harmless.

Then of the security guard in the hospital.

Both stories were true.

"As certain as it needs to be," he said.

Jonas looked at him.

"That's not an answer."

"I know."

"Yesterday you would've given me one."

Daniel looked at the screen.

"Yesterday I would've said: first enough basis, then act."

"And today?"

"Today I say we need to start building that basis earlier."

"That's still not the same question."

Daniel knew.

"No."

Jonas sat down across from him.

"What if the next source gets one thing right and another thing wrong again?"

Daniel pulled up the three-column structure from Lena's sheet as a digital template.

Confirmed.

Open.

Refuted.

"Then we split it apart immediately."

"We do that now too."

"Too late. First we built a story, then separated the pieces."

Jonas looked at the line about the gray van.

"True."

Daniel added a fourth field beside it.

**What can we verify without already believing the claim?**

Jonas read it.

"That's going to get long."

"It shouldn't. One sentence."

"And who fills it in?"

"Whoever takes the tip."

"So usually me."

"Then write clearly."

Jonas shook his head, but he smiled.

Lena was suddenly standing behind him.

"Good."

Jonas turned around.

"How long have you been standing there?"

"Long enough to smell the coffee."

She came in and set a printout on the desk.

"The blue van was rented properly. False contact details, genuine payment. Still no solid link to Unit 17."

Daniel took the sheet.

"Security guard?"

"Staying overnight. Prognosis is good."

Daniel nodded.

Lena looked at the timeline.

"You're already building the next case."

"I'm removing avoidable delay."

"That's not the same thing."

"I didn't say it was."

She pointed to the two columns separating what had been known then from what had become known later.

"You know what hindsight does."

"Yes."

"Then tell me what you would have decided differently this afternoon if all you had was what you actually knew then."

Daniel looked at the left-hand column.

Pickup confirmed.

Service provider real.

Case contents unknown.

Source informed, motivation open.

No direct link to the claimed attack scenario.

He did not need a long answer.

"I still wouldn't have supported entering the premises."

Jonas looked at him.

Lena nodded.

"Good."

"But I would've started the parallel checks earlier."

"That's legitimate."

"And with a new comparable tip, I'd prepare faster even while its significance was still open."

Lena looked at him for a moment longer.

"Preparing isn't intervening."

"I didn't say it was."

"Not yet."

Daniel leaned back.

"The decision this afternoon was defensible on the information we had then."

"Yes," Lena said.

"The outcome doesn't make it retroactively bad. And I still change the process."

"I don't object to that."

"You sound like there's more."

"There is."

Lena tapped the new three-column template.

"If next time you start weighting uncertainty toward action more quickly, don't call it process improvement."

Daniel looked at her.

"What should I call it?"

"A decision."

She said it without accusation.

That was exactly why it stayed with him.

Jonas picked up his cup.

"Reassuring."

"I wasn't talking to you."

"Even more reassuring."

Lena left.

Jonas remained in the doorway.

"And if the next source is only half right again?"

Daniel looked at him.

"Then we need to find out which half faster."

Jonas nodded and left too.

Daniel stayed alone.

He moved two review paths into parallel.

Then he created a simple rule for new source tips:

**Immediately separate operational claims into: confirmed / open / refuted.**

Under it, he wrote:

**Do not confuse the source with the claim.**

He read both lines.

They looked like order.

In reality, they also meant he wanted to become capable of acting earlier next time.

Daniel put the cursor beneath them again.

Capable of acting earlier could not mean convinced earlier.

He added three limits.

**No intervention based solely on a source claim.**

**No expansion of data access merely because time is short.**

**Refuted parts remain refuted even if another part later proves true.**

He read the sentences.

This was not new authority. Not even a new threshold. It was an attempt to shorten the time between receiving a tip and testing it properly without prejudging the result.

Daniel turned the three fields into a small template. Beside every open point, there now had to be one sentence stating what could already be checked and what explicitly must not follow from it yet.

The template would detain no one, authorize no access, and shift no purpose limitation.

It would only prevent a testable question from sitting idle until some other question had been answered.

Daniel looked at the depot timeline.

That was exactly the kind of lost time he did not want to give away again.

For that night, he had allowed himself no more than that.

He was about to lock the computer when the private phone beside him vibrated.

This time he looked at it immediately.

## 13

The second message was shorter.

**B-QV 4172. Today, 8:20 to 9:00 p.m. Parking level 3, south. The vehicle belongs to the next step.**

**Don't check only the registered keeper.**

Then a blank line.

**5:46 p.m. Your sister left your mother's apartment alone.**

Daniel looked at the time.

6:38 p.m.

Less than an hour until the start of the window.

The first message had given him almost a day.

This one had not.

He did not call Jana.

He texted her.

**Were you at the apartment today?**

The reply came two minutes later.

**Yes. Realtor. Why?**

Daniel typed nothing.

Then:

**What time did you leave?**

Three dots appeared.

**No idea. Just before six. Daniel, what is this?**

He put the phone down.

5:46 p.m. fit.

Not exactly provable. Jana normally did not check the time as she left the apartment. But *just before six* was close enough to take the line seriously.

Daniel opened the realtor's appointment history Jana had sent him. Today's viewing was listed there. End: 5:30 p.m. Jana could easily have spent another few minutes gathering papers afterward.

That made 5:46 plausible.

It did not explain who had seen her.

The message could still have been constructed. Someone could have watched Jana. Someone could have watched Daniel. Both were bad. Neither confirmed the operational threat claim.

Daniel wrote the sentence down before his mind could merge the two.

**Observation of Jana does not confirm the operational claim.**

He picked up the work phone.

"Jonas."

"I'm not asleep, if that's what you're calling to ask."

"Plate B-QV 4172. Regular channels only. Registered keeper, current assignment, anything we can properly check under the current case."

"From where?"

"Source."

A short pause.

"The source?"

"Yes."

"On it."

Daniel opened the map.

Parking level 3, south.

Several garages could fit that description. The message also named an address Daniel had not read aloud. A few streets from a logistics area used for the event.

No proof.

But not random either.

He put three open routes side by side.

Check the plate through regular channels.

Check the courier company through open sources.

Cross-check the event connection through the contacts they had already established.

All legal.

All immediate.

Exactly what he had spent the past few hours preparing for.

At 6:46 p.m. the first vehicle response came back.

Plate valid.

Rental vehicle.

No current wanted hit.

At 6:51 p.m. Jonas called back.

"Plate exists. Rental vehicle. Rented yesterday to a small courier company."

Jonas went straight through the company data as well. Real company, seven years in the commercial register, three drivers, eight vehicles, ordinary website. Contract and payment ran through the business account; the driver was not clearly assigned.

"Current movement?"

"Not without more."

The proper routes gave him a real vehicle and a real company, but no significance.

"What about the old records?" Jonas asked.

Daniel did not answer immediately.

"Which old records?"

"Earlier you said that with the blue van we should check whether the same vehicles appear in other threat streams. Heller pulled contacts for that."

"Through the relevant offices, properly."

"Yes."

"Then that's how it stays."

Jonas heard the tone.

"Okay."

Daniel ended the call.

He knew there was a faster route.

The faster route would not give him a live location or some secret movement map. It would answer only one question inside protected data from a different case: had this plate already appeared there together with a relevant vehicle or at a relevant location?

Technically, the search would take seconds.

The cross-case basis was the problem.

Daniel did not open the system.

Not yet.

He called Lena.

"I need the fastest clean route for a cross-case plate check."

"What's the current basis?"

He gave her only the operational chain.

New source warning. Specific plate. Rental vehicle. Real courier company. Near the event-logistics area. No independent link to a threat.

Lena was silent for a moment.

"And the private line?"

Daniel looked at Jana's message.

"It has another accurate detail."

"Relevant to the plate?"

"Not demonstrably."

"Then it stays out. I'll check the authorization side."

Daniel hung up.

6:57 p.m.

He did not look at the clock. He looked at the line with the time window.

8:20 p.m.

Eighty-three minutes left.

He called the contact they already knew from event coordination.

The plate was not registered there. Neither was the courier company listed as a direct service provider.

No exoneration.

No incriminating point.

"The garage at that address?" Daniel asked.

"Not part of our site. Several companies in the area use it."

Another ordinary fact.

The source had chosen an address that could fit.

Still no proof why.

Daniel called the rental company through its public switchboard. The contract existed, the payment was unremarkable, and the vehicle had been picked up the day before. The employee could not tell him more without additional authority.

"Was there a return time?"

"Tomorrow morning. No planned mileage. I can't give you any additional drivers without further authorization."

Daniel thanked her.

Another proper route taken all the way to its limit.

Still no answer to the question the source had put in front of him.

At 7:03 p.m. Lena sent back the result on the last clean option.

**The current basis is not enough for cross-case use of the protected legacy records. Urgency is understood. We won't get a defensible authorization tonight.**

Daniel read the sentence.

Then again.

He called Berg.

No answer.

Again.

Voicemail.

He texted only: **Urgent authorization question. Call me.**

Then Heller.

Heller answered.

Daniel gave him only the new operational information.

"Can you check through your side whether the plate connects anywhere through regular channels?"

"I can ask."

"By when?"

"Not before eight. Maybe later."

Daniel looked at the time.

7:08 p.m.

"Window starts at 8:20."

"I know."

"The last warning was essentially right."

"I know that too."

Heller was quiet for a moment.

"Daniel, if I tell you this is enough now, I'd be saying something I can't substantiate."

It was the first time Daniel had heard Heller use his first name.

"Understood. Can you prioritize it?"

"Already have. I'm pulling what I can pull. Anything more would mean pretending we have something we don't."

Daniel said nothing.

Neither did Heller.

That was almost worse than being told to slow down.

"Call me," Daniel said.

"I will."

The call ended.

Daniel sat for a moment with both phones in front of him.

Private: Jana.

Work: open plate.

The source had put them in the same message.

Daniel separated them again.

He texted Jana:

**Everything okay with you?**

The reply came immediately.

**Yes. What's going on?**

He typed:

**I'll explain later. Please stay alert tonight. No specific indication of danger.**

He read it twice before sending.

No instruction to leave the apartment.

No claim that she was a target.

Only what he could honestly support.

7:10 p.m.

Daniel opened the list of regular review paths again.

Plate: exhausted.

Courier company: exhausted.

Event connection: exhausted.

Legacy-record authorization: denied on the current basis.

Heller: regular inquiry running.

Berg: unreachable.

There were still things he could do. He could wait. He could call again. He could hope one of the open offices responded before 8:20.

Those were real alternatives.

Just not alternatives whose outcome he controlled.

Daniel wrote the remaining options on a sheet of paper even though he knew them by heart.

**Heller – regular inquiry running.**

**Berg – callback pending.**

**Lena – authorization checked; current basis insufficient.**

**Jonas – regular vehicle/company checks exhausted.**

He left one line blank beneath them.

The blank line was for waiting, not for the protected records.

Waiting was no longer a non-decision. It was an option with its own consequence: the warning window could begin before any of the open offices responded.

Daniel wrote beside it:

**No new harm established.**

Then:

**No independent confirmation that the plate has threat significance.**

And underneath:

**Observation of Jana remains separate.**

He looked at the last sentence longer than the others.

It would have been easy to read it the other way. Someone was watching his sister, therefore the warning mattered more. Emotionally, that was instantly true.

Professionally, it was not.

Daniel did not cross the line out.

He simply drew a line between it and the operational list.

Then he picked up the work phone and called Berg a third time.

No answer.

Heller had no new update either.

Daniel did not make Jonas check the same public registers for a fourth time. Repetition was no longer diligence.

He stayed in front of the empty search field.

The clean alternatives still existed.

They were simply no longer in his hands.

That was exactly the point at which the faster, improperly authorized route began to feel like action and waiting like omission.

Daniel knew that feeling was not, by itself, a legal basis.

He also knew it would not disappear this time merely because he named it correctly.

Then he placed the private phone face down.

Daniel opened the internal system, passed the normal case view, and entered the protected legacy records he knew because he had worked another matter there months earlier.

The search field was empty.

At the top was the notice explaining permitted use.

Daniel put his hands on the desk.

He knew why this route was not clean.

He also knew why he had opened it at all.

Two days earlier, he would have closed it at this point and waited.

Now he thought of the security guard.

This time he thought of him as a fact, not an image.

Multiple fractures. No danger to life. A real cost in a situation where everyone had had defensible reasons not to move earlier.

That did not make the legacy records permissible.

It only meant waiting no longer felt neutral.

On the private phone, Jana's next message was waiting.

**Daniel?**

The work phone showed 7:11 p.m.

He did not type in the plate.

Not yet.

## 14

At 7:24 p.m., Heller's update came in.

**Still no solid connection. Two offices outstanding.**

Daniel read it on the work phone.

The search field on his monitor was still empty.

It had been empty for thirteen minutes.

During that time he had done nothing new. Only waited, opened updates, tried not to look at the time, and looked anyway.

7:12 p.m.

7:17 p.m.

7:21 p.m.

Berg had not called back.

Lena had sent no new authorization.

And now this from Heller.

Two offices outstanding.

Outstanding was not the same as negative. Daniel knew that.

At the moment, it was equally unusable.

He could have kept waiting.

That was not a theoretical option. He did not need to pretend otherwise.

The regular confirmation could arrive at 7:40, at 8:05, or tomorrow. The plate might turn out to mean nothing.

A rental vehicle belonging to a courier company in Berlin was about as unusual as rain.

Daniel took his hands off the desk and stood.

He had nowhere to go. Even so, he walked to the door, opened it, and looked into the corridor.

At the far end, someone walked past carrying two binders. Jonas was three rooms away on the phone. Nothing in the building had changed simply because Daniel had spent thirteen minutes staring at a search field.

He closed the door again.

Jana's message was still on the private phone.

**Daniel?**

He did not open it.

Not because it was unimportant.

Because he knew what would happen if he connected it to the plate now.

Jana had been watched. Probably.

That did not make B-QV 4172 more dangerous.

Daniel said the sentence quietly, just to hear whether he still believed it.

"Jana doesn't make the plate more dangerous."

The room gave no answer.

He thought of Tobias Ahrens.

Photographs of the loading door. A question about delivery times. A van nearby.

They had waited then.

They had been right.

Ahrens had been harmless.

Daniel remembered Jonas standing in front of the closed case afterward. The man had never known about them. That had been the success.

Then he thought of the security guard from the night before.

Multiple fractures. No danger to life.

That was true too.

Daniel did not reopen the depot timeline. He no longer needed it.

They had found forty minutes of avoidable friction there.

Not five hours.

Not proof that the security guard would have remained unhurt.

Only forty minutes that could have been theirs if they had worked in parallel earlier.

That did not help him now.

Maybe the source had engineered this exact moment. The danger could still be real independently of that.

A real injured victim.

A new warning.

A narrow time window.

A proper review that might not finish before the window began.

And a technically trivial route sitting directly in front of him.

Daniel knew it.

He sat down again.

The permitted-use notice was still at the top of the system.

He did not dismiss it.

He read it.

Not word for word. He knew notices like this too well for that. The records belonged to another case. Access and use were logged. The fact that Daniel could technically view them was not permission to use every hit in a new matter.

He could have built himself a justification out of nothing but true statements: urgent situation, threat to a major public event, two precise source messages, an injured security guard.

None of those statements changed the fact that this specific cross-case use had not been properly authorized.

Daniel opened the current case and looked at the field for case nexus.

If he ran the search, that field would record why he had entered the legacy records.

This was not an invisible step and not a hack. The access would run under his name, his credentials, his timestamp. The visibility of it was almost reassuring.

He picked up the work phone again.

No new message from Berg.

None from Lena.

Heller's last update was four minutes old. Daniel opened it as if the words might have changed.

**Two offices outstanding.**

He could have called Heller again. The answer would have been the same: review in progress. He could have asked Jonas to start the keeper check over from the beginning. The clean routes had not vanished; they were simply exhausted or still open.

Daniel wrote three times on a sheet of paper:

**7:24 – now**

**8:20 – warning window begins**

**9:00 – warning window ends**

Underneath, he wrote:

**No certainty that the window is real.**

That mattered. He was not acting against a clock he knew to be running. He was acting against a deadline set by a manipulative source whose previous warning had been substantially correct.

That was the trap.

If he waited and nothing happened, waiting would be easy to defend afterward.

If he waited and something happened, every minute would suddenly look like a decision.

If he searched and found nothing, the rule violation would still exist.

If he searched and found something, success would not retroactively authorize it.

Daniel turned the paper over.

The calculation did not become cleaner the longer he looked at it.

He could only decide which risk he was going to carry now.

He clicked into the case-nexus field.

The cursor blinked.

Daniel entered the current case number.

Then he stopped.

He could also claim an authorization. A verbal consultation. Some exceptional urgency worded in a way that made it sound as though the problem had disappeared.

He did not.

He did not write that authorization existed.

He did not invent a new justification that made the situation larger than it was.

He linked the current case.

That did not make the use proper.

It only made it visible.

Daniel took his hands off the keyboard again.

Not because he had changed his mind.

Because he wanted to prevent the first boundary crossing from quietly becoming a second one.

He opened the current working note and recorded the status before the search.

**Regular plate review produced no solid connection. Two responses outstanding. Cross-case use currently not authorized.**

Beneath it, he added the time.

7:24 p.m.

The sentence was ugly. That was exactly why Daniel left it there.

If he later had to explain why he searched, no one should be able to think he had discovered the missing case nexus only afterward.

He returned to the search screen. The fields for person, address, and phone remained empty.

The observation of Jana stayed in another note and in his head.

Daniel looked at the empty fields.

Technically, he could have retrieved more.

Precisely for that reason, limiting the search was not some virtue he could use to make the decision feel better. It was merely the smallest form of the violation that could answer his specific question.

Nothing more.

Daniel opened the search form.

Beside the plate field were fields for person, address, phone number, and additional associations.

He looked at them.

Then he hid the expanded fields.

Not necessary.

Not now.

He wanted to know whether this one plate had already appeared in a relevant context.

He did not pull drivers, contacts, households, or movement histories. He did not search for Jana's name either, though the thought appeared briefly.

He pushed it away before it took shape.

Then he entered:

**B-QV 4172**

He limited the query to plate hits and existing vehicle associations.

A selector offered a broader time range. Daniel left it on the narrow default set relevant to the known prior case.

He used only the slice needed to answer his one question instead of opening everything the system could technically provide.

His finger rested on the Enter key for a moment.

He did not think about statutes.

He thought about the question he had asked Jonas in the first case.

What would we need to find if it's harmless?

A rental vehicle that connects to nothing.

Then Heller.

*Still no solid connection. Two offices outstanding.*

Those offices could call back in ten minutes.

Daniel knew that.

He also knew this was not his only option.

That was exactly why it was a decision.

Daniel pressed Enter.

The search took less than a second.

Three hits.

He felt no relief.

Only the immediate loosening in his body that came from suddenly having something in front of him he could test.

The first hit was worthless. Parking violation, more than a year old.

Daniel closed it immediately.

The second came from a protected case unrelated to the current matter. The plate had been captured in the edge of a surveillance photograph.

Daniel opened only the metadata.

Six weeks earlier.

Industrial area in Reinickendorf.

B-QV 4172 had been parked four meters behind a white delivery van.

In that earlier case, the white van had been associated with a person from an Islamist support network.

Daniel stopped.

Not a suspect in the current case.

No proof the drivers knew each other.

No proof the same drivers had even been inside the two vehicles.

Only two vehicles at the same place at the same time.

He did not click through to the person associated with the white van.

The option was there; he left it alone. Daniel opened the third hit. Two days later, on a different street, both vehicles again appeared within the same time window, this time nine minutes apart.

He leaned back.

Coincidence was possible.

The same coincidence twice was worse.

Daniel looked at the time.

7:26 p.m.

He could now have expanded into drivers, contacts, other courier-company vehicles, and people from the old case.

That would have been the logical next search if he treated his first decision as authorization for the second.

He did not.

He took no screenshot of the result list.

He exported nothing.

He wrote down the case references, timestamps, and the association of the white delivery van.

That was all he needed for a regular follow-up check.

Then he closed the legacy records.

A notice appeared in the upper right confirming that his access had been logged.

Of course it had.

Daniel looked at his note.

Three hits: one worthless, two open.

He had not crossed the line to obtain certainty.

Only to turn an open plate into two testable points.

That made the step smaller.

It did not make it proper.

Daniel looked at the time.

7:27 p.m.

Three minutes.

That was how long it had taken to cross a boundary they had been discussing for hours.

His phone rang.

Jonas.

Daniel answered.

"I have a lead," he said.

"From where?"

Daniel looked at the black monitor, where his face reflected faintly back at him.

For the first time since the search, he noticed how tightly he had been clenching his jaw.

He relaxed it.

"I'll explain," he said. "First we find out whether it's worth anything at all."

## 15

The first hit was bad.

Jonas put it on the table anyway.

"The courier company made a delivery to the same industrial area eight months ago."

Daniel shook his head.

"Eight months. Industrial area. Courier company."

"I know."

"Then why bring it?"

"Because you said everything."

"Everything doesn't mean we make something out of everything."

Jonas pushed the printout aside.

"Next point."

They were working in a small room beside the operations area now. On the table were Daniel's handwritten notes from the protected legacy records, not the legacy records themselves.

That was deliberate.

Daniel did not want his one problematic access to turn into a shared workspace where everyone simply continued using the same data as though it were normal.

Lena sat at the far end of the table on the phone with a Berlin office. Daniel had told her what the problematic cross-check had produced, not exactly how he had obtained it.

Not yet.

He did not like that either.

Jonas pointed to Daniel's note.

"Again. B-QV 4172 twice in temporal proximity to a white delivery van from the old case."

"The old van was associated with a man from a support network—associated, not automatically a perpetrator."

"And we can't just start pulling the person's entire network now."

"Correct."

Jonas looked at the time.

"Then we search publicly."

"And in everything the current case legitimately gives us."

"Garage?"

"Where did you get a garage from?"

Jonas pushed aside the first registry printout.

"The white van from the old case," he said. "It's been off the road for three weeks."

"So no current movement."

"No."

"What remains?"

"The person it was associated with back then."

Daniel looked up.

"No people spiral."

"Didn't do one." Jonas tapped a line. "The man hasn't worked for the old company for two months. But a company his brother has an interest in rents a garage."

Daniel waited.

"Source?"

"Commercial-register link. Company address. Then a publicly listed rental property through the business entry."

"Where?"

Jonas gave him the address.

Four hundred meters from the parking garage named in the anonymous message.

Daniel felt the impulse to make the point larger immediately.

Two vehicles in the legacy records.

Brother's company.

Garage.

Four hundred meters.

He could tell the whole line in one sentence.

That was exactly why he said nothing.

"What's the harmless version?"

Jonas rolled his eyes, but only briefly.

"A company rents a garage. Someone's brother isn't that someone. Four hundred meters in Berlin isn't a conspiracy."

"Better."

"And the rental van could still have been there by coincidence."

"It hasn't even been there yet."

Jonas nodded.

"Right."

Daniel pointed to the address.

"Publicly traceable?"

"Register and rental property through the company address. Yes."

"That's better."

"Still no vehicle at the garage."

"Correct."

Lena ended her call.

"Colleagues can keep the public approach under discreet observation. No technical tracking. No search. First we see whether the vehicle appears at all."

Daniel looked at her.

"On what basis?"

"Current plate from the source warning plus a publicly confirmed garage in the vicinity of the current event-logistics area. Narrow observation of a public area. Nothing more."

Daniel nodded.

"How fast?"

"They're on the way."

"Time?"

7:46 p.m.

Thirty-four minutes until the warning window began.

Jonas stood.

"If the van shows up, is that enough?"

Lena looked at Daniel.

"Depends what it does."

"That seems to be the answer to everything today."

"Don't get used to it."

Daniel looked at the garage again.

The information from his rule violation had led them there.

But from this point on, every new step had to stand on something else.

He said it aloud.

"From here on, nothing solely because of my hit."

Daniel stayed with the sentence.

**From here on, nothing solely because of my hit.**

He wrote it across the top of Jonas's working sheet.

Jonas looked at it.

"So we forget where the garage came from?"

"So we don't forget."

"Difference?"

"If the current connection can't support itself, then the garage is only a lead produced by a problematic access."

Daniel drew a line beneath the legacy-record note.

Below it, he started a new section.

**Independently confirmed in the current case.**

There was nothing beneath it yet.

Deliberately.

"And if nothing happens in twenty minutes?" Jonas asked.

"Then we don't have a solid new connection."

"Despite the two old hits?"

"Despite them."

Jonas looked at him briefly, then at the time.

Daniel realized how strongly he hoped B-QV 4172 would appear in front of the garage.

Not only because of the situation. If it did, his hit would no longer stand quite so alone in retrospect.

He recognized the desire and disliked it.

"If I start needing the outcome, tell me," he said.

Jonas raised an eyebrow.

"How am I supposed to know?"

"If I turn a maybe into a therefore."

Jonas nodded.

"I can do that."

Then he looked at him.

"Your hit?"

Daniel knew the moment had come.

"Later. Situation first."

Lena looked over.

She had heard the tone.

Daniel did not avoid her eyes.

At 8:07 p.m. the first report came in.

**B-QV 4172 on site.**

Daniel read it aloud.

Jonas sat down again.

"At the parking garage?"

"No. At the garage."

That was better than the source.

Or worse.

Because the source was no longer the only reason the location mattered.

Daniel asked where the report had come from.

Observation from a public area.

Plate visible.

No technique he could not support.

"Driver?"

"Still inside the vehicle."

"Contact?"

"None."

"Then wait."

Jonas looked at the time.

"Warning window's running."

"It's not an order."

Twelve minutes later, the second report came in.

The driver got out. No visible contact. Went into the garage.

Daniel looked at Lena.

"Not yet."

"I didn't say anything."

At 8:26 p.m. the garage door opened.

A second vehicle drove out.

A dark minibus, not the white delivery van from the old case.

Jonas leaned over the phone receiving the field updates.

"Plate's coming."

It came.

Lena wrote it down and passed it on.

The response took four minutes.

Four minutes in which Daniel noticed how easily he was already turning the minibus into proof.

Garage from the legacy records.

Rental van from the source.

Second vehicle.

He pushed back against it.

"If the plate gives us nothing, it's still just a vehicle leaving a garage."

Jonas nodded.

"Yes."

The response came.

The minibus was registered to a subcontractor of the technical service provider linked to the two access badges recovered the night before.

Now there was a current connection.

Without the anonymous source.

Without Daniel's legacy-record hit as the sole basis.

Daniel had the association confirmed a second time through a separate current system. Registered keeper, company address, subcontractor relationship. Same subcontractor. No stale record, no echo from his own access.

"Now," he said.

Jonas understood. "Now the garage isn't only yours anymore."

Daniel disliked the wording, but it was accurate.

The problematic lead had produced a location. The current minibus gave that location its first independently supportable significance.

Lena stood.

"Now we can move forward."

Jonas looked at Daniel.

"That's it?"

"No," Daniel said. "Now the defensible part begins."

The words sounded almost like relief.

Daniel disliked that.

He was relieved because his rule violation had produced a place that actually mattered.

The danger itself had not become any smaller.

Lena stopped at the door.

"Daniel."

He knew what was coming.

"Later."

"No."

Jonas looked from one to the other.

Daniel checked the time.

8:31 p.m.

The minibus had not moved yet. Operational command was reviewing the new basis.

There were a few seconds now in which telling the truth would not delay anything operationally.

Daniel said, "I checked the plate from the warning against protected legacy records. Different case. No proper cross-case basis."

Jonas did not move.

Neither did Lena.

"When?"

"7:24 to 7:27."

"Who authorized it?"

"No one."

"What exactly did you pull?"

"Plate hits and existing vehicle associations. Three hits. One parking violation. Two shared time-and-location appearances with a vehicle from an old case."

"People, contacts, movement data?"

"Didn't expand. No contacts, no movement data."

Lena looked at him for a long time.

"And you're telling me now?"

Daniel could have said: because the lead has now been independently confirmed.

That was exactly the truth.

And exactly why it sounded like a justification.

"Yes."

"Why not immediately?"

"Because I wanted to know first whether the hit was worth anything."

"So you tested the usefulness first and disclosed the rule violation afterward."

Daniel was silent.

Jonas looked down at his notes.

The answer was there.

"Yes," Daniel said.

For a few seconds, the ventilation was the only sound in the room.

Lena picked up her phone.

"We move the current situation forward on a clean basis. After that, we talk about your access."

Daniel nodded.

"Document it now," she said.

"I will."

"Not later."

Daniel was already opening the note.

Time. Data set. Purpose. Scope. Result.

No softened language.

Lena left.

Jonas remained seated.

"You just did it?"

Daniel kept typing.

"Yes."

"Even though Lena said the basis wasn't enough?"

"Yes."

"And Heller was still checking?"

"Yes."

Jonas looked at the new vehicle connection.

"And now there really is something."

Daniel stopped with his hands over the keyboard.

"That doesn't make the access proper."

"I didn't say it did."

Daniel looked at him.

Jonas had learned that sentence from them.

Suddenly that was not reassuring.

The minibus began to move.

Daniel saved the note.

Then he picked up the new vehicle association.

"Now we work with what we have independently."

Jonas nodded.

He said nothing more.

## 16

Daniel did not see the operation.

He heard it.

Short updates over the operations channel. Location. Vehicle. Movement. Confirmation.

Tactical command was somewhere else. That was how it should be.

Daniel sat with Jonas in front of two monitors, keeping the intelligence picture together while people outside in protective equipment made decisions he could not make and should not make.

8:48 p.m.

The dark minibus left the garage.

B-QV 4172 stayed behind.

"That doesn't fit the warning," Jonas said.

"Which part?"

"The source called the rental van the next step. It's not moving."

Daniel looked at the updates.

The source had not said B-QV 4172 itself would reach the target.

*The vehicle belongs to the next step.*

Broad enough to fit many things afterward.

"Then we don't work the source's script."

He passed on the current assessment.

The minibus was now heading toward a delivery area scheduled to open for the event the following morning. Still no offense. Still no immediate attack.

But the connection between the garage address, stolen access credentials, the service-provider network, and the current vehicle now supported measures that would not have been defensible that afternoon.

Operational command asked for the basis.

Daniel gave them only the independently confirmed chain.

Garage.

Current minibus.

Subcontractor of the service provider.

Access badges from the previous day's incident.

His legacy-record search and the claim that the warning window must be real stayed out of it.

"Assessment?" came the question.

Daniel looked at Lena, who was now standing in the room.

She did not nod. She only said, "The current connection supports further operational review."

Daniel passed that on.

8:54 p.m.

The minibus turned off.

Jonas enlarged the map.

"Not a direct route."

"To where?"

"Still unclear."

They waited.

A position update from the ongoing observation arrived later than the previous one. Daniel knew that six minutes on a map said nothing about six minutes outside.

8:57 p.m.

The minibus continued west.

Berg came into the room.

"Status?"

Daniel gave him three sentences.

Berg looked at the time.

"Warning window has been running thirty-seven minutes."

"It's not our basis."

"Understood."

He stayed anyway.

9:02 p.m.

The minibus stopped.

It stopped one hundred sixty meters away at a side entrance instead of at the parking garage named in the warning.

Jonas pointed at the map.

"If they want to get in, they won't use the official delivery entrance."

Daniel looked at him.

"Why?"

"Because that side entrance opens for setup crews tomorrow. Today it's closed. Public event plan."

"Current status of the door?"

Jonas pulled up the site plan. "Normally closed today. Internal access only."

"Pass it on."

Jonas did.

A few seconds later, operational command changed the intervention point.

Daniel noticed he was gripping the edge of the table.

He let go.

A new update came in.

One person got out of the minibus, ignored the side entrance, and walked toward an outbuilding.

"Doesn't fit," Berg said.

Daniel did not answer immediately.

"Fit what?" Lena asked.

Berg looked at her.

"The assumption that they want to get inside."

"That was Jonas's hypothesis," Daniel said. "Not the situation."

He removed it from the working picture.

The person disappeared from view for two minutes.

Then returned.

Carrying a bag.

Nothing dramatic in size. No visible contents.

Daniel felt the room begin to focus on the object again.

"Not the case," he said.

Jonas looked at him.

"I didn't say it was."

"I was telling myself."

9:06 p.m.

Operational command reported that there were now enough current anchors for intervention. Not because of the bag alone. Because of the vehicle, the access environment, the false credentials, and the current movement.

Daniel heard the wording twice.

Enough current anchors.

His legacy-record search had opened the route that led here.

The intervention itself now stood on different ground.

9:07 p.m.

**Intervention. Two people stopped. Vehicle secured.**

Then nothing.

One minute.

Two.

Jonas stood, then sat down again.

"That's taking too long."

"No."

"How do you know?"

"I don't."

Daniel took off the headset for a moment and put it back on.

The ventilation in the room was louder than the channel.

Lena did not look at the time. Berg did.

9:09 p.m.

One person from the minibus had been identified. No name Daniel knew.

No immediate connection to the man from the old legacy records.

That mattered.

The lead had held, but not in the simple way.

9:10 p.m.

Second person still being identified.

Vehicle opened.

Specialists requested.

Jonas stopped talking.

At 9:11 p.m. the next update came.

Inside the vehicle were several falsified or improperly used access documents, clothing from a technical service provider, and material the specialists on site immediately isolated for separate assessment.

No details over the operations channel.

Only one sentence:

**Actual security relevance confirmed.**

Jonas read it aloud.

Daniel said nothing.

Berg exhaled audibly.

Lena did not.

Only then did Daniel notice the tension in his shoulders. He let them drop.

It was not triumph.

It was relief.

That made it more dangerous, not less.

They still did not know what the security relevance specifically meant. That forced Daniel to reduce the relief again.

"What's confirmed?" he asked over the channel.

The answer came in fragments. Access documents: at least one obtained through misuse. Service-provider clothing: genuine, but not assigned to the people stopped. Technical material: assessment ongoing. No final statement on intended end use.

Daniel wrote each element down separately.

He forced himself not to accelerate simply because someone had used the phrase **actual security relevance**.

The operations channel stayed open.

The material had not yet been classified precisely. No one in the room knew whether the people stopped understood the entire plan, were carrying out only one part, or even knew what individual items were intended for.

Berg asked, "Is that enough to tie it to the event?"

Daniel shook his head.

"It's enough to show the situation is real. Not enough to say we know its full scope."

Jonas looked at the time.

"But we stopped something."

"Probably."

"Daniel."

"I want to hear that sentence from the people assessing the material."

Over the next few minutes, only partial updates came in.

One document matched an actual delivery area.

One access credential was relevant for the following morning.

Its connection to the people stopped remained open.

Then operational command reported that the prepared access route could no longer be used with the seized credentials. The affected delivery route was closed and reorganized.

Only then did Daniel write beneath his notes:

**Concrete preparatory step interrupted.**

Not *attack prevented*.

Not *source confirmed*.

The smaller sentence was defensible enough.

Daniel thought of the security guard from the day before.

Multiple fractures. No danger to life.

The man had been injured while Daniel and the others were still hesitating for defensible reasons.

Today, a problematic step had bought real time.

Daniel did not want to balance one fact against the other.

That would have been the most convenient story of all.

"So no finished attack plan in the vehicle," Jonas said.

"No one said there was."

"Security relevance sounds bigger."

"Which is why we ask."

Another interim assessment followed. One of the documents carried markings corresponding to a delivery area for the event. Still no proof that the two men had actually intended to enter there. But the combination of false credentials, vehicle connection, and marked access point was no longer a neutral coincidence.

Berg leaned over Daniel's notes.

"Enough for you?"

Daniel looked at him.

"For what?"

"To say your cross-check was right."

Daniel did not need a second.

"No."

Berg raised his eyebrows slightly.

"It was useful. That's not the same thing."

Lena glanced at Daniel but said nothing.

9:14 p.m.

The second person was identified. Again, not a name from the legacy records. A current link to the service provider could be established through a legitimate employment trail only a few weeks old.

Another point that had nothing to do with Daniel's old hit.

The picture became broader and more stable at the same time.

The legacy records had led them to the garage.

What they were finding now belonged to the present.

The second person from the vehicle was not the man they knew from the old case. The driver of B-QV 4172 was still unaccounted for.

The picture remained incomplete.

The success did not.

9:15 p.m.

The specialists separated several items for further assessment. Some were legal and ordinary in isolation. Clothing. Documents. Technical equipment.

Only the combination with misused access data and the current location made the matter larger.

Daniel listened to every interim step.

There was no single object he could point to and say: because of this.

The danger emerged from the connection between things.

Just as the suspicion had before.

Only now the connection consisted of independent current points.

At 9:26 p.m. the preliminary technical assessment came in: had the vehicle entered the delivery area the following morning using the available credentials, it could have created a concrete danger to the event. Exactly what stage of a larger plan they had interrupted remained open.

Daniel asked for precision.

"Concrete danger meaning what?"

The answer remained professionally narrow.

The materials and access documentation were consistent with a preparatory step that would have made uncontrolled entry or the introduction of additional items substantially easier. The precise ultimate objective had not yet been established.

Daniel accepted the limit of the statement.

No proven final attack plan.

But a real dangerous preparatory step.

Stopped.

Berg stepped behind Daniel.

"That was close."

Daniel looked at the times.

"Yes."

"Without the vehicle from the warning?"

Daniel understood the question.

"Without the first hit, we probably wouldn't have had the garage on our radar in time."

"And without your cross-check?"

Daniel did not answer immediately.

He looked at 7:27 p.m.

Then at 8:07.

"Probably not in time."

"Probably?"

"The regular inquiries were still open. The connection might have arrived later."

"But for tonight?"

Daniel looked at him.

"Tonight, the cross-check bought time."

Berg nodded.

No congratulations.

Good.

Lena came in from the doorway.

"The security guard from yesterday is having surgery tomorrow. Prognosis unchanged and good."

Daniel looked at her.

She knew exactly why she said it now.

The old harm remained.

The new one had been prevented.

Both facts could stand beside each other.

Daniel took the current operational assessment and placed it beside the note documenting his access.

On one side: concrete security relevance confirmed.

On the other: no proper cross-case basis for the initial search.

Two sentences that did not cancel each other out.

Jonas removed his headset.

"What happens to the two of them?"

"First, what always happens," Daniel said. "We find out who they are and what they actually did."

"And the rest?"

Daniel looked at the map.

The rest had become larger.

The people in the vehicle were not the center merely because they had now been caught. The driver of B-QV 4172 was still open. The source was open. The connection to the warehouse was open. The larger structure was open.

But for that evening, something else was settled.

The step Daniel should not have taken had bought them time.

And the time had been enough.

When Berg left the room, Daniel remained seated.

He did not open his access note to change it.

Only to make sure it still contained the same sentence it had before the successful outcome.

**No proper cross-case basis. Check limited to plate.**

It was still there.

Daniel left it there.

## 17

Berg did not ask whether Daniel had known the access was problematic.

He asked when Daniel had known.

"Before I ran the search."

Daniel sat opposite him. Lena was to his right, a closed laptop in front of her.

Berg had shut the door.

Three printouts lay on the table.

Daniel's note.

The current operational assessment.

The short technical assessment of the interrupted preparatory step.

Daniel had expected a fourth sheet.

A preliminary decision about him.

It never appeared.

"Not while you were doing it?" Berg asked.

"No."

"Not only after Lena asked?"

"No."

"Before you pressed Enter, you knew the use wasn't properly covered."

"Yes."

Berg leaned back.

"Good."

Lena looked at him.

"What's good about that?"

"That we don't also have to talk about self-deception."

"It doesn't make the access better."

"I didn't say it did."

Daniel slid his note closer to Berg.

He had written it while the operation was still unfolding. Time. Plate. Data set. Purpose of the check. Result. No wording that turned *not properly covered* into *unclear*.

Berg read the whole thing.

He did not limit himself to the highlighted parts.

Daniel heard footsteps in the corridor outside.

Someone stopped briefly in front of the door, then moved on.

"You limited it to the plate. You didn't expand to people or pull any additional data?"

"Only the hit associations and the two timestamps."

"No screenshot, no export?"

"No."

"Why keep it that narrow?"

Daniel looked at him.

"Because I knew the plate check itself was already too far."

Berg put the paper down.

Lena said, "The limitation matters. It isn't authorization."

"I understand that too."

"Then say it once without using the word success."

Berg looked at her.

"What?"

"Evaluate his access as if we'd found nothing afterward."

The room went quiet.

Daniel looked at Berg's hand. Two fingers rested on his note.

Berg thought.

"Then Daniel would have used data from a different purpose context for this case without a proper basis."

"Thank you."

"And I would have initiated an internal review."

"Now too?"

"Yes."

Lena nodded.

Daniel realized he was waiting for a harder continuation.

*And until then, you're off the case.*

It did not come.

Berg looked at Daniel.

"If you hadn't run the check?"

Lena answered first.

"Then we don't know what would have happened."

"I'm asking Daniel."

Daniel looked at the table.

"The current link to the garage probably would have come later."

"How much later?"

"I can't answer that seriously."

"Would we have stopped the minibus?"

"Maybe."

"In time?"

"Maybe not."

Berg pulled the operational assessment toward him.

"The assessment says the preparatory step was interrupted in time."

"Yes."

"And the regular inquiries?"

"Two were still outstanding when I searched."

"When did the first come back?"

Daniel did not know from memory.

Lena did.

"8:18 p.m. No solid vehicle connection."

Berg looked at the time of the first independent garage hit.

8:07 p.m.

Eleven minutes earlier.

"And the second?"
