"8:34 p.m. Partial connection through the same company network, but still no garage."

Berg leaned back.

"So the rule violation bought real time."

Lena folded her hands.

"And that's exactly why the outcome can't become retroactive authorization."

"I'm not doing that."

"Then what are you doing?"

Berg looked at Daniel.

"I'm not removing him from the case."

Daniel looked at Berg.

"If the hit had produced nothing, would you leave me on the case?"

Lena looked up from the laptop.

Berg did not answer immediately.

"Maybe," he said at last.

"That's thin."

"It's the honest answer."

Berg pushed the operational assessment aside.

"The operational question would have been the same: what do I lose by removing you from the case immediately, and what additional risk do I create by keeping you?"

"And without the success?"

"The second risk would have been harder to justify."

Lena said, "So the success does matter."

"Of course it matters to the operational decision," Berg said. "It just can't replace the rule assessment."

Daniel leaned back.

It was exactly the kind of distinction that sounded reasonable and still had an effect.

The successful rule violation made him more operationally valuable in the same situation in which that rule violation was supposed to be reviewed.

No acquittal.

But no neutral starting point either.

Daniel looked again at his service ID.

"Then the absence of a sanction is itself a consequence of the success."

Berg tightened his mouth briefly.

"The absence of an immediate sanction. Don't make it more than that."

Daniel nodded.

That was enough to make the distinction uncomfortable.

He had expected the sentence.

It still felt wrong.

He had prepared himself for the consequence without consciously deciding to. Since the start of the meeting, both his feet had been planted firmly on the floor as if he might have to stand up at any moment. His service ID lay on the table. Usually it stayed in his jacket pocket. He had taken it out before Berg arrived.

Now he looked at it.

So did Lena.

Berg followed their eyes.

"What?"

Daniel slid the ID one centimeter back toward himself.

"Nothing."

"You expected me to take it?"

"At least to remove me from the case."

Berg said nothing.

Daniel did not take the ID off the table.

"Why not?" he asked.

Berg exhaled.

"Because right now I have to separate two decisions you'd rather turn into one."

"Which ones?"

"Whether your access was acceptable. And whether taking you out of the current operation now is smart."

Lena said, "That's an operational decision."

"Yes."

"Not an assessment of the access."

"Yes."

"Then I want it documented exactly that way."

"Do it."

Daniel looked at her.

Lena opened her laptop.

"I will."

Berg continued.

"We have an ongoing cross-state operation. Daniel knows the sequence, the source, and the open connections. The intervention exposed a real danger. If I remove him on principle now, I lose operational continuity in a situation where time is exactly our problem."

"You also lose a signal," Lena said.

Berg looked at her.

"Which one?"

"That a boundary remains a boundary when breaking it works."

"I know."

"Then don't pretend continuity is free."

Berg was silent.

Daniel stayed out of it.

It was not his argument to win.

Berg eventually looked at him.

"I can take you off the case now. Then in an hour we hand it to someone who knows the source line, the warehouse, the service provider, and the two vehicle strands from the files. That's possible."

Daniel nodded.

"And maybe right," Berg said.

"Yes."

"Or I keep you on the operation, document the violation, run the review separately, and put you under an explicit reporting requirement for any further exceptional decisions, as far as time allows."

Lena said, "As far as time allows is a very large door."

"Do you want me to require absolute prior authorization in an acute situation?"

"No."

"Then the wording stays uncomfortable."

Lena did not answer.

Berg pulled Daniel's note back toward himself and wrote a short file note in the upper right.

"Continues on operation for reasons of operational continuity," Lena read.

Berg put down the pen.

"And underneath: separate review of data use."

"Good," Lena said.

Daniel stared at the two sentences.

That was how dry a moment could look when, for the past hour, it had meant the question of whether he would still be part of the case tomorrow.

"Who reviews it?" he asked.

Berg looked at Lena, then back at Daniel.

"Not you. Not me alone. That's enough for now."

"When?"

"As soon as the situation allows."

A laugh rose in Daniel and died somewhere before reaching his mouth. The same phrase as the reporting requirement. Everything depended on time now.

"And until then?"

"Until then you keep working. But today's success doesn't give you a silent blank check."

"I don't want one."

Lena looked at him.

"The problem isn't whether you want it. The problem is whether you start assuming you have it."

Daniel did not answer.

The sentence sat where a sanction might have sat.

Maybe that was the more uncomfortable consequence.

Berg leaned forward.

"Daniel. This is not an invitation."

"I didn't take it as one."

"Good. If you see another boundary, you tell me beforehand whenever time allows it at all."

Daniel thought of 7:24 p.m.

Three minutes to the hit.

He could have called Berg first.

He had.

Berg had not answered.

That did not make the decision cleaner.

It only made Berg's new rule less clear than it sounded.

"If time allows," Daniel said.

Berg heard the qualification.

He left it there.

"Operational briefing tomorrow, seven-thirty."

That should have ended the meeting.

Daniel stayed seated.

Lena kept typing.

Berg stood, noticed Daniel was not moving, and stopped.

"What?"

"That's it?" Daniel asked.

Berg looked at him.

"What were you expecting?"

"A consequence."

"There may still be one."

"May?"

"There will be a review. I don't know what consequence it will have."

"But not now."

"Not now."

Daniel looked at his service ID again.

He had put it there ready to hand over.

Now that felt almost embarrassing.

He picked it up and slipped it into his jacket pocket.

The movement felt larger than it was.

Berg took Daniel's note.

"If it makes you feel any better: I don't like this decision."

Daniel stood.

"Neither do I."

Berg opened the door.

"Unfortunately, it worked."

No one said anything.

Lena closed her laptop.

Daniel did not see the file on her screen. He only noticed that she skipped the normal shared case area and moved her notes into a different workspace before locking the computer.

"What was that?" he asked.

Lena looked at him.

"Documentation?"

"Separate. Berg just said the review is being handled separately."

Daniel held her gaze.

"Who has access?"

"Not the whole operational team."

She said nothing more.

It was professionally plausible.

And still a new information path Daniel could not fully see.

Lena passed him on the way to the door.

"You wanted a consequence," she said.

"Yes."

"Documentation is one."

Then she left.

Lena had barely left the room when Daniel's work phone vibrated.

Jonas.

"Can you still release the current vehicle status?"

Daniel looked at the screen.

His access still worked.

No lock. No warning. No role switched to **temporarily revoked**.

Only the normal case, the same button as an hour earlier.

"Yes," Daniel said.

"You're still on the case?"

Daniel looked at the door Lena had gone through.

"Apparently."

He did not like the word.

He opened the vehicle status, checked source and timestamp, and released only the current, independently confirmed portion.

Routine.

That was what made it strange.

After the conversation with Berg, Daniel had expected some visible interruption. A different room. A formal handover. At least one moment in which his rule violation stopped the work.

Instead, Jonas asked him about a vehicle.

Daniel completed the task.

Then he looked at the access list. His name was still everywhere it had been before.

The consequence existed as a note.

Operationally, that evening, it was almost invisible.

Daniel wrote down the time at which Berg had decided to keep him on the operation.

Distrust of Berg was not the reason. Daniel did not want to tell himself later that the lack of an immediate sanction had somehow simply happened.

That too had been a decision.

Then Daniel opened his own note once more.

At the top was the rule violation.

Beneath it, Berg's decision to keep him on the operation.

Two separate paragraphs. Two separate responsibilities.

Daniel added no sentence between them that turned the second into retroactive approval of the first.

He left the gap visible.

That was where the discomfort lived.

Daniel stayed alone in the room for a moment.

Nothing remained on the table from his access except the faint imprint of paper in the dust and an empty water glass.

He had crossed a boundary.

He had admitted it openly.

He had probably protected people because of it.

And tomorrow at seven-thirty he would walk back into the same operation.

None of those sentences made any of the others untrue.

## 18

The new message came at 11:18 p.m.

Daniel was alone again.

He had already locked the computer and was about to leave when the private phone vibrated.

Two lines.

**B-QV 4172. Three hits. You limited the search to the plate.**

**You decided before it was too late.**

Daniel stopped.

His jacket was already over his arm.

He put it back on the chair and unlocked the computer.

The important part was the limitation, not the plate.

Three hits.

Not two relevant hits.

Not *connection confirmed*.

Three.

The number Daniel had seen immediately after the search.

And *limited the search to the plate*.

The source now seemed as interested in how Daniel had restrained himself as in the outcome.

Daniel read both lines again.

The fact that he had searched only for plate hits and existing associations was in his note. Berg knew it. Lena too.

Jonas now knew the limitation from Daniel's disclosure.

After the operation, the situation summary had gone to a small coordination group that included Heller's area. Daniel did not know how much detail about his access had been included there.

Then there were technical roles that could see the access logs.

So there were no longer three possible paths.

More like five.

Maybe more.

Daniel still did not click on names.

He opened the timeline first.

When had his note been saved?

8:33 p.m.

When had Lena's separate documentation been created?

He could not see that.

When had the operational summary been sent?

10:41 p.m.

When had the message arrived?

11:18 p.m.

Enough time for an ordinary internal information path.

That mattered.

The message did not prove someone had been watching his screen live.

It proved only that someone had obtained a very specific detail of his actions before 11:18.

Daniel opened the access history for his own note.

Berg.

Lena.

Himself.

Two technical roles.

Later, an export into the protected internal review.

Heller's name did not appear.

The relief came too quickly. Daniel stopped it before it became a conclusion.

Heller's area could have learned different details through the operational summary. A name did not need to appear in this one list for someone to know the content.

Nor did Lena's name make her the source.

She had a legitimate reason to read the note.

So did Berg.

So did the technical roles.

The small circle was uncomfortable.

It still was not conclusive.

Daniel opened the distributed version of the operational summary.

His problematic access was not described there in full.

It said only that a cross-case vehicle lead had contributed to further review.

No number of hits.

No limitation.

That removed one convenient path.

Others remained open.

He searched for the internal-review version.

Not visible to him yet.

Lena had said: separate.

Daniel thought about her screen shortly before she left.

A separate workspace.

Not secrecy without purpose.

But an information path he did not control.

He closed the file.

The next easy hypothesis would have been Berg.

Berg had held the note in his hand.

Berg had an interest in making Daniel's success visible.

But interest was not leakage.

Then Heller.

Cross-agency coordinator. Access to parts of the operation. Direct number. He had pushed for faster review without removing boundaries.

Still not a lead.

Daniel did not create a suspect list.

Instead, he opened a new working document and created four fields.

**Operational information from the source.**

**What has been independently confirmed.**

**What the source knows about internal processes.**

**Who could legitimately have known that internal information.**

Better.

No person became the source automatically.

Only information paths.

Under the four fields, Daniel added a working rule.

**A person is not a source hypothesis merely because they sit on a possible information path.**

Then a second.

**Test source knowledge against access opportunity and timing.**

Jonas could not see the rules over the phone, but Daniel gave him the core of it.

"If the next message contains internal knowledge again, first we establish when that detail became available at all."

"And who could see it."

"After that."

"Why after?"

"Because a detail that existed only after the operational summary has a different audience from something known during my access."

Jonas was quiet for a moment.

"You don't want a suspect list."

"Not yet."

Daniel looked at Berg, Lena, the technical roles, Heller's area.

Names were seductive. They turned a messy information path into people with motives immediately.

He did not delete any names.

He simply placed them behind the timing question.

From now on, two reviews would run in parallel: what the source knew about the case would be tested like any other tip. What the source knew about Daniel and internal processes would be tested separately against possible information paths.

Neither track could automatically confirm the other.

He entered the new message.

Operational information: none new.

Internal knowledge: three hits; plate-only limitation.

Possible legitimate knowledge: Daniel, Lena, Berg, technical documentation; later onward-distribution paths open.

Then he stopped at the second line.

**You decided before it was too late.**

The message did not say *you were right* or *you saved people*. Not even *the access worked*.

Decided.

As if that had been the relevant part.

Daniel leaned back.

The source was not commenting on the find.

It was commenting on him.

His reaction to the situation.

That was different.

He opened the first message.

North door.

Pickup.

Event.

Mailbox.

Then the second.

Plate.

Jana.

Now the third.

Three hits.

Limitation.

Decision.

The source had never told him to break a rule.

It had only placed information so that eventually the question was sitting in front of him.

Daniel felt the muscles in his neck tighten.

He stood and went to the window.

Outside, the yard was almost empty. A single car drove through the gate. The guard raised the barrier.

Daniel thought about 7:24 p.m.

He could have waited.

The source did not have to know he would press Enter.

Maybe it had only counted on him doing it.

That was worse.

Predictable was not the same as controlled.

Daniel put the phone down again. First he wanted the information paths separated before drawing Jonas further into the operational work.

He opened two separate working documents.

The first contained only what was operationally needed: no new situation information, no new location, no new person.

The second contained what the source knew about Daniel's conduct.

**Three hits. Plate-only limitation. Timing after internal disclosure.**

He set different access permissions.

Not perfect. Not final. Only enough to keep convenience from pulling both layers back into the same view.

Then he checked what the separation cost him.

The operational document now lacked the sentence that made the source psychologically interesting. The provenance document lacked the live case context that made every internal access path immediately look suspicious.

Both views were worse for building a quick story.

That was deliberate.

Daniel wrote beside them:

**Separation creates follow-up questions. Follow-up questions are a cost, not a mistake.**

He read the sentence and changed **not a mistake** to **a deliberate cost**.

He did not know whether the separation would actually make the leak harder.

But at least it forced him to decide, each time information moved, what he was moving and for what reason.

He picked up the work phone and called Jonas again.

"Again?" Jonas said.

"Yes. The source."

"What does it want?"

Daniel was silent.

Jonas's tone sharpened.

"Daniel?"

"From now on, with every message we separate two things. Operational information, and information about what the source can know about us."

"Aren't we already doing that?"

"Not cleanly enough."

"What did it say?"

Daniel looked at the message.

"A detail about my access."

"Which detail?"

"Number of hits and the limitation."

Jonas was quiet briefly.

"I knew that afterward too."

They went through the obvious recipients: Lena, Berg, and probably the people who could see the access technically.

"Heller?"

Daniel looked at the empty space in his working document.

"Possible through coordination. Not established."

"So everyone."

"No. A small circle with several paths."

Jonas exhaled.

"And what do we do with that?"

"We don't change the operational picture because of a provenance hypothesis. But not everyone automatically gets the same full source file anymore."

"You want to segment it."

"Yes."

The disadvantages were obvious: annoying, error-prone.

During the call, Daniel opened the existing distribution list. Four people currently received the same full source status. He did not simply remove a name. Instead he split the distribution into two groups: operational claims to the operations team; source knowledge only to the small review group, and only as needed for the specific review.

"What if that means you withhold exactly the information someone later needs?" Jonas asked.

"Then that's the cost."

"A bad one."

"And if the source notices we're reacting differently?"

Daniel paused.

"Then we've at least learned that it really is observing our response."

Jonas said nothing.

It was the first time Daniel's source review itself could begin to test the behavior of the other side.

No trick.

Just less uniform distribution.

Jonas remained quiet.

"What else did it say?"

Daniel read the second line.

"That I decided before it was too late."

"That's true."

Daniel looked out the window.

"That's the point."

Jonas said nothing.

"It's interested in how I decide," Daniel said.

The sentence sounded worse aloud than it had on the screen.

"Maybe it's just praising you," Jonas said.

"Maybe."

"You don't believe that."

"I don't know."

"And we still use its next tips?"

Daniel did not answer immediately.

That was the actual decision.

He could cut off the channel.

Block the number. Preserve messages only for technical analysis. Stop giving any operational response the source might read as feedback.

That would be cleaner for the source question.

And perhaps stupider for the case.

"We don't ignore verifiable information just because the source may want something from me," Daniel said.

"So yes."

"Yes. But we review the two layers separately."

"Double game."

Daniel disliked the term.

"Division of work."

Jonas laughed briefly.

"Sure."

"Seven-thirty."

"I know."

Daniel ended the call.

Then he opened the source file.

Until now it contained origin, timestamps, confirmed information, discrepancies.

He added two separate sections.

**A – operational claims**

**B – knowledge of internal processes / Daniel**

The new message went almost entirely into B.

Under A, there was only: no new operational claim.

Daniel added a new point.

**The source may be observing: the situation and decision behavior.**

He read it.

Too abstract.

He deleted it.

Then he wrote:

**The source is interested in how I decide.**

He left that sentence.

He locked the computer.

The jacket was still on the chair.

Before picking it up, he looked at the private phone one last time.

No new message.

For the first time that evening, that was not a relief.

The question was no longer only how the source knew what it knew. Just as important was what it wanted Daniel to do with that knowledge.

## 19

Mika Lorenz wanted to know whether he was allowed to go to work on Monday.

Daniel looked at him.

"That's your question right now?"

"I have a job."

Mika sat with both forearms on the table. Late twenties, narrow shoulders, dark beard thinning under the chin. He had been wearing the same gray hoodie for almost two hours. Someone had brought him water. He had not touched it.

There was nothing on the table except the cup and a notepad in front of Daniel. No photos. No printouts. Daniel wanted to see first what story Mika told without props.

"You were temporarily detained after being found in connection with falsified access documents," Daniel said.

"I wasn't in the vehicle."

The answer came immediately.

Daniel let a moment pass.

"You were in the garage."

"Yes."

"The minibus came out of the garage."

"Yes."

"You had a key."

Mika raised his hands. "For the garage."

"Not for the bus."

"That's what I said."

Lena sat to Daniel's right and wrote nothing down. That made Mika more nervous than a running transcript would have. His eyes kept returning to her closed pen, as if something important would happen the moment she used it.

Before they began, Daniel had made Mika explain what he did for a living. Warehouse logistics at a medium-sized electrical wholesaler. Early shift whenever possible. Four years with the company. Permanent contract for eight months. Daniel had not needed those facts to understand the case.

Now he needed them.

As a baseline for where Mika lied, not as exoneration.

"Who gave you the key?" Daniel asked.

"I told you."

"Again."

"A guy I know as Samir."

"Last name?"

"No idea."

"Phone number?"

"It was in my phone."

"We have the phone."

"Then you have the number."

Daniel looked at him.

"How long have you known Samir?"

"A few months."

"Two?"

"More."

"Six?"

Mika lifted one shoulder. "Maybe four."

"Where did you meet him?"

"Through people."

"Which people?"

"Just people."

Mika was bad at minimizing things. His answers became shorter, not smarter.

Daniel changed direction.

"What was supposed to happen at the garage?"

Mika pressed his lips together.

"Nothing."

"Mika."

"I was supposed to receive some stuff."

"What stuff?"

"Clothes. Badges. Things."

"What kind of things?"

"I didn't look."

"Why not?"

"Because I'm not stupid."

Daniel leaned back.

That was not a good sentence for Mika. He realized it too.

"So you knew it was better not to know what you were receiving."

"That's not what I said."

"It is."

Mika looked toward the door.

"I knew the badges weren't clean," he said finally. "Nothing more."

"Then why did you take them?"

"Money."

"How much?"

Mika looked at the door again.

"Five hundred."

Daniel waited.

"Total?"

"For the stuff."

"How many times?"

"Twice."

"So a thousand."

"No."

"Why no?"

Mika rubbed his thumb along a seam in his sleeve. "The first time was two hundred."

"You just said five hundred."

"This time."

Daniel wrote down both amounts.

Mika watched the pen.

"I'm in debt," he said.

Daniel did not look up. "I didn't ask."

"Yeah, but you're acting like I—"

"I'm not acting like anything right now."

Mika closed his mouth.

That was the edge Daniel needed. Mika was not trying to explain why what he had done was right. He was trying to make his own role small enough that his normal life might continue afterward.

"Who decided you'd use the minibus?"

"No idea."

For the first time, Daniel slid a sheet across the table. It contained no real information. Only a false time reference he had prepared.

"Thursday, 10:40 p.m. Handover at the east gate."

Mika stared at it.

"What?"

"You were there."

"No."

"Sure?"

"I was on late shift."

"That doesn't rule it out."

"Yes, it does, because I was in the warehouse until ten-thirty and then went home with Patrick."

"Patrick who?"

Mika gave a last name and immediately afterward the bus they had taken.

Daniel wrote down both.

Mika looked at him. "You can check that."

"We will."

Daniel set a second false anchor.

"Samir told you Tuesday that the delivery area would be open the next morning."

Mika frowned.

"What delivery area?"

"You know which one."

"No."

"The event."

Mika looked at Lena, then back at Daniel.

"I didn't even know which event."

The answer came too quickly, but not with the practiced defensiveness of a man reciting a prepared lie. Daniel left it there.

Third test.

"Who knew the security guard would be there yesterday?"

Mika looked at him.

"What security guard?"

Daniel held his gaze.

Mika frowned.

Lena moved for the first time.

Only a small glance toward Daniel.

"The one at the depot," Daniel said.

"I wasn't there."

"That wasn't my question."

"I didn't know anything about it."

"Do you know his name?"

"No."

"What happened at the depot?"

"I only heard about it here."

"From whom?"

"One of the officers outside said something. Hospital."

Daniel made a note.

Mika's voice rose immediately. "That doesn't mean I had anything to do with it."

"I didn't say it did."

"But you're writing it down."

"I'm writing down what you say."

"Great."

Mika finally picked up the water and drank. His hand trembled slightly. Not enough to prove anything.

Daniel continued. Other times. Other handovers. A name from a marginal note.

Mika lied.

More than once.

But his lies always clustered around his own role. Who he had met. How often. Whether he had been paid. Whether he knew the badges were fake.

On one real handover, he first claimed he had transported only an envelope. When Daniel told him video also showed a bag, the bag suddenly became "not his." When Daniel asked what color it was, Mika knew anyway.

"Black," he said, and realized what had happened at the same moment.

Daniel said nothing.

Mika swore under his breath.

That was guilt. Real, small, ugly guilt.

But whenever Daniel tried to make him the center of the structure, the clever lies disappeared.

There was only blankness.

Mika knew no names above his two contacts. He did not know why the event mattered. He knew neither the final location nor a time. On deliberately false details, he pushed back hardest where he could safely disprove his own presence. On two genuine connections, he seemed honestly surprised.

That could be acted.

But not all of it felt acted.

After an hour, Jonas came in and placed a note in front of Daniel.

Two of the handovers Mika had described were independently confirmed. A third had demonstrably taken place somewhere else than Daniel had assumed. Mika could not have been there. His late shift on Thursday was also confirmed. The colleague he named had left the site with him.

Daniel read the note twice.

One of his false claims had not merely been denied by Mika.

It was verifiably false.

That did not make Mika credible.

It made Daniel's model smaller.

"Break," he said.

Mika looked up. "Can I call my boss now?"

"No."

"He's going to fire me if I just don't show up."

"You have other problems right now."

"Maybe for you."

Daniel stopped at the door.

Mika did not look at him.

"I've worked there four years," he said. "He's not going to wait two weeks while you decide whether I'm the new Bin Laden."

"You accepted falsified badges."

Mika looked up.

"Yes."

"For money."

"Yes."

"And you deliberately didn't ask what they were for."

Mika pressed his lips together.

"Yes."

The word hung between them for a moment.

No mistake. No innocent mix-up.

Mika looked toward the door again.

"My boss has been calling since this morning," he said. "He probably thinks I'm sick or just gone."

"That's not your biggest problem right now."

"Not for you."

Daniel almost repeated himself, then stopped.

Mika continued. "If he fires me, I can't exactly tell him: sorry, I was briefly at the police station over fake badges, but I'm not the mastermind."

"No."

"Exactly."

The cynicism held only until the last word. After that, Mika looked again like a man who did not know how much of his old life was still there.

Still, he asked again: "Can I at least tell my boss I'm not just skipping work?"

Daniel looked at him.

Mika did not sound like an ideologue trying to explain his cause. He sounded like a man simultaneously realizing he had knowingly made himself guilty and still carrying his shift schedule around in his head.

Lena opened the door.

Daniel did not answer Mika.

Jonas was waiting in the corridor.

"He's not the head," Jonas said.

"No."

"But he's involved."

"Yes."

Lena closed the door behind them.

"One doesn't make the other smaller."

"No."

Daniel looked through the narrow window in the door. Mika had his head in both hands. Then he lifted it and looked at the second untouched cup of water as though he expected to be sitting there for a while longer.

Not broken. Not redeemed. Just a man beginning to understand that his normal Monday was no longer happening.

Daniel took the note from Jonas.

"We check both of his contacts fully within the permitted scope. But not because he makes them the center."

"Then why?"

"Because they're the limit of what he knows."

Jonas looked at the confirmed handovers.

"And everything above that?"

"We don't look for it inside his head."

Lena nodded slightly.

Daniel slipped the note into his pocket.

"We stop looking for what he controls," he said.

Jonas nodded.

"Then what?"

"What he doesn't know."

Daniel remained outside the door for another moment.

"His Monday isn't trivial to him," Lena said.

Daniel looked through the glass at Mika.

"No."

"And that's exactly why he lies at the points where his normal life is attached."

Daniel thought about the amounts. Two hundred. Five hundred. Twice. About the bag whose color Mika knew even though it supposedly had not been his.

"He's not protecting the structure," Daniel said.

"He's protecting the version of himself that somehow goes back to early shift."

Jonas looked at the confirmed late shift.

"That almost makes him more credible."

"No," Daniel said. "It makes his lies more local."

He took the note out again and divided Mika's statements into three groups.

**demonstrably false about his own role**

**confirmed about his own movements**

**no solid knowledge above his contacts**

The third group remained the most important.

Mika's honesty was not decisive for that. Daniel simply no longer had a reason to treat every gap in Mika's knowledge as an especially sophisticated lie.

"If we want to make him the mastermind, we'll find enough ugly sentences," Daniel said.

Jonas nodded.

"And if he was only a paid helper?"

"We'll find the same ugly sentences."

Daniel put the note away.

The job was not to make Mika smaller.

Only not to make him larger than the verifiable facts did.

## 20

The problem was the number of hits, not the plate or the legacy records.

Daniel had recorded it in his note after checking B-QV 4172 against the protected records. Three hits. Limited to plate matches and existing associations. No expansion to persons.

Later the source had written:

**B-QV 4172. Three hits. You limited the search to the plate.**

Jonas sat opposite him with four versions of the same matter open.

"I froze the historical versions," he said. "Not just the current one."

Daniel nodded.

The first screen showed Daniel's original access note.

The second, the short operational version.

The third, the situation summary.

The fourth, a technical access record Daniel himself could read only in part.

"None of this was ever public."

"No."

"On the operations channel?"

"Only the lead we gained. Not how the query was run."

Jonas opened the log.

No number of hits. No limitation.

"In the operational summary?"

Daniel looked at Lena.

She stood at the window with her arms folded.

"Later," she said.

"How much later?"

"After the operation."

"Before 11:18 p.m.?"

Lena did not answer immediately.

Daniel noticed.

"Lena."

"The access was logged. The note existed. So did the technical access record."

"Who had them?"

"More than three people."

"Who exactly?"

"That's not the same question as who could have been the source."

"I didn't say it was."

"It sounds like it right now."

Jonas looked from one to the other and decided to look back at his screen.

Daniel turned his chair toward Lena.

"I don't want a suspect list. I want information paths."

Lena looked at him.

"Then say that."

"I am."

She unfolded her arms.

"Fine. The original note: you, Berg, me. Technical roles for access logging. Later, the separate internal review. The operational short version went to the situation group, but shortened."

Jonas put check marks beside the versions.

"Number of hits and the limitation?"

"In the original note."

"In the situation version?"

"No."

"Cross-agency coordination?"

Lena lifted one shoulder slightly. "Depends which working version was passed on."

"Heller?"

"Possible."

"Possible meaning?"

"That I don't know what was in his summary."

Daniel nodded.

"And what do you know?"

Lena looked at him.

"More about the oversight path than you need to know right now."

Jonas looked up after all.

Daniel said nothing.

Lena held his gaze.

"That wasn't a good answer," he said.

"Maybe not."

"Then give me a better one."

"No."

The room was small enough that the word sounded harder than Lena had said it.

Daniel stood.

"You've been demanding for days that I separate information properly."

"Yes."

"Then separate it."

"That's exactly what I'm doing."

"By refusing to tell me who can access the documentation?"

"By telling you what you can reliably use for this source question and what you can't."

"Why can't you tell me who?"

Lena took one breath.

"Because an independent oversight path stays independent only if the person whose decisions are being reviewed does not control every step of the review."

Daniel stayed standing.

There it was.

Not yet an explanation, but at least the shape of a secret.

"My decisions."

"Among other things."

"Since when?"

"That's exactly the part I'm not opening up here as an informal side process."

"We're not in the hallway."

"You know what I mean."

Daniel went to the window.

Outside, the yard lay gray under low clouds. A delivery van stood half on the sidewalk.

"You have a separate track running."

Lena did not answer.

That was not proof.

But it was new.

And it touched exactly the kind of information Daniel was looking for: a protected path she knew and he did not.

He felt the suspicion gain weight before a single new fact had been added.

That was the dangerous part.

Jonas cleared his throat.

"I've got something else."

Daniel turned.

Jonas had put the document versions side by side.

"The source can't have inferred the three from a later report. The first version with the number of hits and the limitation existed only in the protected case record. The shortened situation version afterward contains neither."

"Sure?"

"Yes."

He showed them both versions. The later one said only **cross-case vehicle lead**. Nothing about three hits. Nothing about limiting the check to the plate.

Daniel checked the modification time.

10:41 p.m.

The source message had arrived at 11:18.

"So someone could have read the early version," Jonas said.

"Or heard about it."

"Or seen the technical record."

Lena said, "Exactly."

"Or through a review office."

Lena looked at him.

"Yes."

Daniel registered the brief hesitation before her answer.

Again, no proof.

Again, something that weighed more heavily with her because she really was withholding something from him now.

"And Berg?" Jonas asked.

Daniel looked at the access list. "Knew the note."

"Heller?"

"Could have received detail through coordination. Not established."

"Technical staff?"

"Can see logs. Motive open."

Daniel did not write names. He wrote paths.

**Original note.**

**Access logging.**

**Separate review.**

**Verbal onward disclosure.**

**Coordination.**

That removed the convenient explanation that someone had simply seen a normal forwarded summary.

The information had come from a protected space.

It did not have to have come directly from the system.

Someone could have read it and told someone else. Someone could have heard it in a meeting. A technical role could have seen more than it should.

But it had not been public.

Daniel took a second notepad.

"From now on, not everyone gets the same information set."

Lena closed her eyes briefly.

"That will make the work worse."

"Maybe."

"No. It will. Two people will know different versions of the same situation. Questions will take longer. Someone will miss a connection because they don't know it exists."

Daniel wrote three workstreams.

**Operational situation. Source file. Internal provenance review.**

"Exactly why we separate by function."

"And when function doesn't separate cleanly?"

"Then we decide."

"Who?"

Daniel looked at her.

"For now, me."

Lena did not laugh. "Of course."

"Have a better proposal?"

"Yes. A documented distribution model with an independent check instead of your personal gut call."

Daniel stopped.

"Build one."

The answer caught her off guard briefly.

"What?"

"You're right. Not just my distribution list. You define the independent check. Jonas maintains the operational picture. Provenance review stays with the smallest group possible."

Lena studied him.

"And you accept that you don't decide everything alone?"

"If it reduces information leakage."

"We don't know that yet."

"Then we measure it."

She stepped away from the window.

"And that's exactly what I mean. Distrust creates work."

"So does trust without verification."

The sentence came out faster than Daniel intended.

Before Lena left, the first question from the operations team came in.

Jonas read it aloud. "Service-provider connection from Mika's handovers. They want to know whether the source ever mentioned anything about it."

Daniel looked at his three workstreams.

There was nothing about it in the operational picture.

The source file contained an old, vague reference that might help.

"Who's asking?"

Jonas named the office.

"They don't have the source file," Daniel said.

"Exactly."

Daniel hesitated.

The separation was working.

And making the answer slower.

He opened the source file himself, checked the old reference, determined that it added nothing defensible to the specific question, and sent back only that conclusion.

Almost four minutes.

Before, Jonas could have searched the shared file in seconds.

Lena looked at the time.

"That's what I mean."

"Four minutes."

"Four today."

"And if it stops us from showing the source what everyone knows?"

"Then it may be worth it. But don't pretend caution is free."

Daniel nodded.

He added a fourth item beside the three workstreams:

**Document delays.**

Then they tested the new distribution model on two more real questions.

The first became faster because the operational picture was enough. The second had to go through Daniel again because it touched source knowledge.

Six minutes.

Jonas wrote the number beside the four from the first round without comment.

Daniel looked at it.

"You're keeping score?"

"You wanted to measure."

"Right."

"Distrust has now cost ten minutes."

Lena said, "And perhaps prevented a leak. We don't know either way."

Daniel nodded.

That was exactly why he left the numbers there. The new caution was not a free security upgrade. It only shifted which risk became visible.

Lena gathered her papers.

"Then I hope you still trust yourself."

She left.

Jonas waited until the door closed.

"That was uncomfortable."

Daniel sat down again.

"Yes."

"Do you think it's her?"

Daniel looked at the path labeled **separate review**.

That would have been the easy answer.

Lena knew a protected channel. Lena was withholding details. Lena had access to the original note.

All true.

"I think she's not telling me something right now."

"Not the same thing."

"No."

Daniel did not put Lena's name into the working document.

He marked only the path she would not disclose.

Less satisfying.

Cleaner.

## 21

Berg did not call it a special route.

He called it a project structure.

"Temporary," he said. "Case-specific. Only the offices we actually need. No more five phone chains and three people checking the same question one after another."

A page with six points was displayed on the screen behind him.

Purpose.

Participating offices.

Data categories.

Access roles.

Logging.

End date.

Daniel liked that the end was there.

Apparently Lena did too.

"Who can extend it?" she asked.

Berg looked at the page. "We're talking about the start."

"I'm talking about the end."

"Of course."

"Who?"

Berg named the responsible level.

Lena wrote it down.

"Automatic?"

"No."

"New justification?"

"Yes."

"Documented?"

Berg pushed the pen away. "Yes."

Jonas sat at the edge of the table trying to look as though he was not the youngest person in the room.

Heller was connected by video. His image froze for half a second every few minutes.

Berg clicked on.

"In practical terms: defined case scope, defined roles, faster combination of information we already have. No new magic query."

"And no new authority to collect data," Lena said.

"Yes."

"If one office isn't allowed to collect something, the project structure can't suddenly produce it for them."

"Yes."

"And that separation has to remain visible in the access model."

"Yes."

"And if the urgent version starts today, the formal steps still have to be completed afterward."

Berg looked at her.

"Lena, if you want to hear yes one more time, just say so."

Jonas became intensely interested in his documents.

Lena did not smile.

Daniel almost did.

"What about access?" he asked.

Berg switched to a role overview.

The views were separated by role.

Operational analysis.

Technical review.

Coordination.

Documentation.

Daniel recognized a cleaner version of what he had improvised himself the previous evening.

"Who sees the source contents?"

"Only the defined group," Berg said.

"Who sees the provenance review?"

This time Lena answered. "Even smaller."

Daniel looked at her.

She held his gaze.

Her secret had not disappeared.

But the new structure made visible that withholding information could also serve a legitimate function.

Heller's video moved again.

"The idea isn't to make Reuter's old access look prettier," he said. "The idea is that no one has to improvise the same mess again."

Daniel looked at him.

"Mess?"

"You know what I mean."

"I think I do."

Heller lifted a hand briefly. "The access worked. It was still a bad process."

"Because it wasn't authorized."

"That too. And because everything depended on one person having three minutes to decide which data environment to open."

Daniel said nothing.

Heller continued.

"If the system is properly prepared beforehand, the next Reuter doesn't have to make the same decision."

"The next Reuter?" Jonas asked.

Heller looked briefly confused, then smiled. "The next person in the same situation."

His image froze. When it resumed, Heller was no longer looking into the camera.

"I was once in a case where every office had a defensible reason to wait," he said. "Afterward, we could explain in great detail why nothing had been possible sooner. People were still dead."

The room grew quieter.

Lena looked at the role overview.

"That's not an argument against purpose limitations."

"It's not meant to be," Heller said.

"Good."

"It's an argument for not making the permitted routes unnecessarily slow."

After a moment, Lena nodded. "I can live with that."

Daniel heard the difference.

Heller wanted speed.

Lena wanted limitation and auditability.

Berg wanted something that worked and could be explained upward.

All three could want the same project structure for entirely different reasons.

Berg slid a second page across the table.

Daniel skimmed it.

Two of his decisions from the previous few days appeared as examples of lost time and successful countermeasures.

"Who wrote this?"

"Working draft," Berg said.

"For whom?"

"Upstairs."

"How far upstairs?"

"Far enough that we're not spending today debating it."

Daniel looked at him.

"You passed the case upward as a reference."

"Of course."

"Without telling me how much of it."

Berg put down the pen.

"Daniel, I'm your supervisor. If a process almost runs too late and an improvised workaround protects people, I don't turn that into a private learning exercise between the two of us."

"I didn't ask you to."

"It sounds like you are now."

"You're not just using the process. You're using my rule violation as justification for a new structure."

Berg looked directly at him.

"I'm using the fact that the old process was too slow and your improvised access bought time. The fact that I dislike the rule violation is in the same record."

"Upstairs too?"

"Yes."

Daniel could not verify whether every version gave both parts equal weight.

Berg noticed the look.

"If you think I'm selling you as a hero, read the draft."

Daniel picked up the second page again.

It did say: **noncompliant cross-case use of existing data; operationally effective; structural action required.**

No hero.

But an argument.

That was almost more uncomfortable.

Daniel thought of the source.

**You decided before it was too late.**

Berg had a real interest in making precisely that decision visible.

And that interest was open.

He was turning it into something the institution could use.

"How long has this been running?"

"Since the access."

"Who has the documents?"

Berg named a small group.

Heller's area was included.

Lena too.

No answer.

Only more paths.

Daniel looked at Heller on the screen. "And your interest?"

Heller raised an eyebrow.

"That the next time we have a credible time window, we don't start by searching for jurisdiction."

"Nothing more?"

"That's enough for me."

The answer was ordinary enough to be believable.

Daniel could still have read almost anything into it.

He did not.

Lena tapped the first page.

"Duration."

Berg gave the date.

"After that?"

"Ends or gets a new justification. Data returns under the defined rules. No silent permanent pool. Logs remain."

Daniel read the six points again.

What convinced him was not greater access.

It was that Jonas would not have to call three offices one after another again.

That vehicle ownership and an access-authorization question could be checked in parallel.

That a legitimate piece of information would not have to travel through five distribution chains before it could sit beside another one.

It did not feel like more power.

It felt like less friction.

Daniel had Jonas run an ordinary matter through the new route.

No urgent situation. No source tip. Just a vehicle question from the existing case complex.

Before, Jonas would have requested the keeper information, waited for the answer, then contacted the authorization office separately. Now both questions went simultaneously to clearly assigned roles. The answers appeared side by side with timestamp and provenance.

Eleven minutes later, the matter was closed.

No dramatic finding.

Just a vehicle belonging to a legitimate subcontractor with valid authorization.

"And now more people have seen the same case context," Lena said.

Jonas nodded. "Yes."

"And we were faster."

"Also yes."

Daniel looked at the closed item.

Cases like this made the structure more convincing than the spectacular success had.

It worked even when they found nothing.

It worked as a better organized space for questions that were already permissible, not as a miracle tool.

The price remained real: more simultaneous visibility, more roles, more logs, more people who could know what they were working on.

So did the benefit: less waiting, less duplicate work, faster rejection of false connections.

Daniel understood why institutions rarely forgot routes like this completely once they had worked.

The reason was ordinary: eleven minutes felt immediately reasonable in daily work.

That was probably why it was so attractive.

"I want the structure," Daniel said.

Lena looked at him.

"Under conditions."

"Which ones?" Berg asked.

"Case-specific. Fully logged. Clear end date. No automatic carryover into the next case complex."

Berg exhaled through his nose.

"It's on the page."

"Then we don't have a problem."

"And independent review of exceptional decisions," Lena said.

Daniel looked at her.

"Even if it slows the process?"

"Yes."

Daniel nodded. "Then that too."

Heller said nothing.

Berg moved to the next point.

"Then we start."

Jonas opened his task list. The old improvised route was still there as a manual note.

He did not delete it. He marked it **do not use** and placed the new project route beneath it.

"Better?" he asked.

Daniel looked at the two lines.

"Cleaner."

"You don't sound excited."

"I'm not supposed to."

No one marked the moment.

No music. No sentence about boundaries.

Just Jonas crossing out an old access route in the task list and replacing it with a new project path.

And Daniel noticing that the new path immediately felt more reasonable than anything they had improvised over the previous few days.

## 22

"Tuesday?" Daniel asked.

Mara said nothing.

He stood in the corridor outside a conference room, holding the phone between his shoulder and ear. In four minutes, the first joint review under the new project structure would begin behind the door.

On the small table beside it lay a printout of the new role model. Daniel's name appeared in three places.

"Tuesday what?" Mara asked finally.

"Our conversation."

"The one that was supposed to be Sunday?"

"Yes."

"And yesterday?"

Daniel closed his eyes briefly.

Sunday, six p.m.

**Hamburg. Not sometime.**

The appointment was still in his calendar. Not moved. Not deleted. Simply overrun by a situation briefing that had started at 5:20 p.m. and was still going at 9:40.

Mara had texted at 6:07.

**I assume tonight isn't happening.**

Daniel had not seen the message until after nine.

"Yesterday wasn't possible."

"I know."

She did not sound angry.

That made it harder.

"Tuesday evening," he said. "I'll try—"

"No."

He heard voices in the room behind him.

"No what?"

"Don't tell me Tuesday if you don't know Tuesday."

Daniel switched the phone to his other hand.

"I can't guarantee you a time right now."

"Okay."

"Okay?"

"What else am I supposed to say?"

"That we'll find another one."

"Daniel, I'm not looking for a free table at a restaurant."

He looked at the time.

Three minutes.

"The situation has become bigger. My role too."

"I understand that."

"Then you know why—"

"Yes."

She did not interrupt loudly. Only early.

Daniel fell silent.

Mara said, "I still have to decide."

"Then ask for another extension."

"I did."

He straightened.

"When?"

"Yesterday."

"What time yesterday?"

"Morning."

"And?"

"Two more days. Not three weeks."

Daniel was annoyed that she had already done it.

The annoyance arrived immediately and was immediately unfair.

"You could have told me you were talking to them."

"When?"

He had no answer that did not sound stupid.

Mara did not help him.

"I also talked to HR about the start date," she said.

"Why?"

"Because they need to know when I can resign."

"That doesn't mean you're accepting."

"No."

"And housing?"

A short pause.

"I asked for two viewings."

Daniel said nothing.

"Haven't signed anything," Mara added. "Just asked."

"That sounds pretty far along."

"Yes."

"So you're already planning the move."

"I'm checking what my life looks like if I take the job."

"Without me."

Mara was quiet briefly.

"No," she said. "Without your decision. That's not the same thing."

Daniel rested his free hand on the small table.

The printout shifted beneath his fingers.

"I wanted us to do this together."

"So did I."

"Then give me a little more time."

"The time isn't mine to give."

"You got two more days."

"Yes. And I'm using them."

Daniel looked through the glass door into the conference room. Berg was already at the screen. Lena was going through documents. Jonas was pulling a cable from his bag.

Inside, there really was a situation waiting for him.

On the phone, Mara was no longer waiting.

Both were reasonable.

"What else have you checked?" Daniel asked.

Mara answered as if the list were already in her head.

Handover at her current office. Notice period. Two possible apartments for the first few months. Commuting time. Whether she could work partly from Berlin during the first weeks. Which client appointments were already fixed for November.

"You did all that since yesterday?"

"Not all since yesterday."

Daniel needed a second.

"Since when, then?"

"Since I knew the offer was serious."

"Why didn't you tell me?"

Mara did not laugh. "I did. Not every spreadsheet row."

He remembered dinner. Team. Reporting line. Start date. Hamburg.

He had asked about salary and work from home.

Mara had already been talking about her life.

Daniel noticed he was once again trying to turn every preparatory step into a decision that had already been made.

"Okay."

"I haven't resigned either."

"I know."

"Doesn't sound like it."

Daniel looked through the glass door again.

"I'm just trying to understand how far along you are."

"Farther than a week ago."

"Because of me?"

"Because of the deadline."

Mara did not say it sharply.

"And because I don't want to lose another five days while we try to find a time to make a joint decision."

Daniel could have objected. The five days had not been only his calendar. There had been a real terrorism case, injuries, an active operation.

All true.

None of it extended Mara's deadline.

The conference-room door opened. Jonas put his head out.

Daniel raised one finger.

"Two minutes," Jonas said quietly.

Mara probably heard the movement.

"You have to go in."

"Not yet."

"Yes."

"Mara."

"What?"

He wanted to say this was not how he wanted it.

That was true and helped no one.

"I don't want you thinking I don't care."

"I don't."

"Then why does it sound like you do?"

"Because that's not the problem."

Daniel waited.

"You just don't have a place for it right now," she said. "That's different from not caring."

The sentence was less accusatory than Daniel had expected.

That made it more accurate.

"I could tonight—"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because you'll be exhausted tonight and I don't want to start talking about the next three years at half past midnight just so we can formally say we talked."

Daniel was silent.

Mara said, "I want a real answer from you when you have one. Until then, I make the decisions that belong only to me."

"And accepting the job?"

"That doesn't belong only to me. But the deadline does."

Behind the door, someone began the meeting even though Daniel was still outside. Muted voices. A screen tone.

Daniel looked at his calendar. The Sunday appointment was still shaded gray. He opened it briefly.

No note explaining why it had failed.

Only **Hamburg. Not sometime.** and a red bar because the time had passed.

He thought back to Sunday evening. At 6:07 Mara had texted. At 6:12 he had seen the message in the operations area and told himself he would answer after the next update. Then a new vehicle hit came in.

At 6:40 he had stopped thinking about it. He had not consciously canceled the appointment; something else had simply outweighed it.

"I didn't even cancel Sunday," Daniel said.

Mara was quiet for a moment.

"No."

"That was shitty."

"Yes."

No apology loop. Just the word that fit.

"I'm sorry."

"Okay."

"Just okay?"

"Daniel, I'm not trying to punish you. I just don't want to pretend the appointment didn't happen simply because we didn't have it."

He closed the calendar entry.

"Understood."

"Good."

"I'll call you as soon as I have something solid to say."

Mara exhaled quietly.

"Good."

"That doesn't sound good."

"It's just the answer."

"And if I don't have anything solid tomorrow?"

"Then I decide with what I have."

Daniel looked at the project-structure printout under his hand.

The same words were standing there in another form. Incomplete situation. Available information. Decision despite open points.

He would rather not have seen the comparison.

Mara had not developed a new personality over the previous few days. She had simply done what a deadline required.

Called HR.

Clarified start options.

Requested two viewings.

Spoken to her deputy about a possible handover.

Every single step was provisional.

Together they still meant her life was moving forward while Daniel waited for the joint decision point he himself had missed.

"If you accept," he said, "I don't want to find out in a message."

Mara was quiet briefly.

"Then answer your phone."

The sentence was not cruel.

Daniel closed his eyes for a second.

"Fair."

"I'll tell you before I decide."

"Thank you."

"But I'm not asking again whether the timing works for you."

Daniel looked through the glass door at Berg, Lena, and Jonas.

"Understood."

This time he meant it.

No threat.

No *then it's over.*

Only the same logic Daniel had been working with for days.

Incomplete information did not remove a deadline.

A voice called Jonas back into the room.

Mara said, "I'm not putting my things on hold anymore until your calendar decides when we can talk about ours."

Daniel grimaced.

"That's not how it is."

"Then tell me when it isn't."

"I will."

"Good."

They ended the call without saying goodbye.

Daniel stayed in the corridor for another moment.

The screen showed the duration of the call. Ten minutes.

The Sunday appointment had been supposed to get two hours.

He put the phone away.

Jonas was back at the door.

"Everything okay?"

Daniel looked at him.

"No."

Jonas nodded as if that were a sufficiently precise professional answer.

Daniel followed him inside.

Berg was pointing at the new project structure.

"We start with the open access points."

Daniel sat down.

His phone stayed in his pocket.

## 23

The man's name was Nils Weber, and at first glance he was interesting only because his name appeared too often.

Three times in access lists.

Twice in phone connections.

Once in a shift overview that placed him at a location where another part of the structure had already appeared.

None of the points was strong on its own.

Together they looked better.

Too good, Daniel thought.

Jonas had opened Weber on the large screen and immediately pulled the defined group around the same access points.

Eighteen people.

Not suspects.

People whose professional roles, access, or communication paths fell within the agreed criteria.

Daniel had the heading changed.

**Relevant persons** became **review group**.

"Better?" Jonas asked.

"Less wrong."

"Work phone," Lena said.

Jonas pushed the assignment across to her.

"Registered to Weber."

"Doesn't mean only Weber uses it. And the access?"

"He coordinates evening shifts for the subcontractor. Legitimate access."

Daniel looked at the timeline.

"But not at 1:14 a.m."

"If the timestamp is correct," Lena said.

"It is."

"Then the question remains why."

Weber got his own line in the working view.

It was not red or highlighted; he sat at the top only because he had the most overlaps.

Jonas opened the first connection.

A call from Weber's work phone to a number that had entered the case through Mika.

Duration: forty-seven seconds.

"Who was on call?" Daniel asked.

"Still open."

"Then leave it open."

Second connection.

A nighttime access event.

Weber was listed as shift supervisor.

"Was he scheduled?"

"No. Off."

Lena looked at Daniel.

"That's better than the call. Not good—better."

On the second screen, the remaining hits from the new project structure were arriving.

Most fell apart quickly: spelling variants, shared on-call devices, delayed synchronization, legitimate shifts, regular vehicles. Jonas crossed them off. The list grew longer before it got shorter.

"Not exactly magic," he said.

Daniel looked at the open hits. "Good."

"You're the first person I've met who's happy about bad data."

"I'm happy about explainable data."

"Almost the same thing."

Daniel stayed with Weber.

"What's the harmless explanation?" he asked.

Lena answered immediately. "Work phone handed off. Shift swap. Error in the access assignment. Private connection to someone who only stands out because of Mika."

"And the dangerous version?"

"That he's using access he legitimately has for something else."

Daniel nodded.

A few weeks earlier, he would have stayed longer with the first list.

Now he thought of the security guard.

The minibus.

Three minutes between the wrong access and the useful one.

"Not just Weber," he said. "Check the defined group around the access points too."

Lena looked at him.

"That brings in people we have less on than Weber."

"I know."

"And that was not the original idea."

"Yes, it was. That's what we built the structure for."

"For faster cooperation. Not so a weak suspicion can strengthen itself simply by widening its reach."

Daniel looked back at Weber.

"We're not deciding guilt. We're looking for connections."

"And every search creates a record about people," Lena said.

"Logged and limited."

"That doesn't make it invisible."

Daniel did not answer.

It was not a victory.

Only a decision.

He authorized the defined review group.

Over the next two hours, almost all the remaining hits were explained. Weber stayed open. Daniel still removed him from the top line: standing out relative to others was not an additional hit.

The same four points applied to everyone in the review group: current timing, functional access, an independent second connection, and whether a harmless explanation remained open or had been refuted. For Weber, two were strong and two remained open.

"So Weber's still ahead," Jonas said.

"Yes. But on the same criteria as everyone else."

Lena reviewed the list. "And what happens when we get a hit?"

"Nothing automatically."

Then Jonas found a residual hit he had initially set aside.

"I marked this one weak."

Daniel stepped behind him.

A temporary driver for another service provider had been registered at two locations within nine days that also appeared in Mika's handovers. The first time was tied to a normal job. The second had no matching assignment.

"Why did you set it aside?"

"Because temporary driver, service provider, two places. That's almost the definition of driving around."

"Correct."

"But the second stay is after the end of his shift."

Daniel read the times.

9:38 to 10:06 p.m.

No assignment.

"Could be coincidence," Jonas said.

"Check it."

The hit did not lead to Weber or to the same subcontractor.

The first response was ordinary.

The driver claimed he had picked up a colleague.

The colleague confirmed it.

Daniel was ready to close the hit.

Then Lena asked, "Which vehicle?"

Jonas pulled the assignment.

It was one of the service provider's vans, not the driver's private car.

"Why use a company van to pick up a colleague privately?" Daniel asked.

"Still possible."

"Yes."

They checked the deployment schedule.

The van should already have been back at the depot by then. It was not returned until the next morning.

Small.

But real.

Daniel left the strand open.

The next response made it smaller again at first. The employer allowed drivers to take vans home privately in exceptional cases. No written approval for that evening, but no clear violation either.

"So almost nothing again," Jonas said.

"Almost," Daniel said.

They needed more.

An hour later, a second independently obtained fact arrived. That morning the temporary driver's vehicle had been stopped near a rented storage unit. Routine traffic stop. No arrest. Nothing unusual about the vehicle.

The storage unit, however, belonged to a company that connected, two steps away, to an already known support-network contact.

Daniel pulled the connection onto the screen.

Not from the new project data alone. Registry information and the current traffic stop confirmed it separately.

"Now?" Jonas asked.

Daniel looked at Lena.

"What's the alternative explanation?"

"The company rents storage space. The driver has a legitimate job or a private reason. The old support connection is historical and has no current relevance."

"And the second anchor?"

"Current vehicle at the current storage unit outside his duty schedule."

Daniel nodded.

So did Lena, once.

"Now."

The measures that followed were targeted.

No broad action against an entire group of people. No screen flashing red.

First, the actual user of the storage unit was confirmed.

Then the current vehicle movement.

Then a second person who had been there repeatedly over the past few days and had already appeared in a separate current review strand.

Only after that did the measure have enough basis.

Weber played no part in it.

Daniel noticed and pushed the thought away. Weber was not cleared yet. He simply was not the reason for this strand.

The first attempt to reach the user of the storage unit went nowhere. The listed business number went to voicemail. The rental company confirmed only that the contract existed and was paid.

For twenty minutes, the connection remained exactly that: an interesting address with a vehicle in front of it.

Then confirmation arrived from a separate current matter that the second person had in fact used the storage unit.

Only then did the colleagues on site request the targeted measure.

Daniel watched a weak residual hit become, step by step, something that could support a decision.

It took longer than a database hit.

And that was exactly why he trusted it more.

The storage unit was checked. Two people were found there. Documents and material inside were consistent with a preparatory step relevant to the event. Specialists took over the assessment.

The first report was cautious.

**Access documentation present. Origin unclear.**

Ten minutes later:

**Material likely connected to event logistics. Technical assessment ongoing.**

Jonas stood behind Daniel's chair.

"That looks good."

Daniel looked at him.

"For whom?"

Jonas sat down again.

"You know what I mean."

Daniel did.

At 6:42 p.m. the response came in:

**Direct relevance to the threat. Preparatory step interrupted in time.**

Jonas read the sentence twice.

"We wouldn't have seen that without the wider review group."

Daniel did not answer immediately.

He traced the path back.

Review group.

Temporary driver.

Second visit.

Company vehicle.

Current traffic stop.

Storage unit.

Independent company connection.

Measure.

No single magic hit.

A series of small things that gained meaning only beside one another.

"Probably not in time," Daniel said.

Lena looked at the chain.

"That's the point in favor of the wider review."

Jonas looked up. "You said that?"

"Yes."

"Should I write it down?"

"Enjoy the moment."

Daniel looked at the hit list.

Weber was still at the top.

His name had not disappeared.

But he had not led them here either. Even so, the success felt like confirmation that widening the review group had been the right decision.

Daniel allowed himself that feeling for a moment.

Outside, people had been prevented from entering a delivery area for which they had prepared material.

Another step had been stopped.

The new project route had made visible something the narrower review probably would have missed.

For a moment, that was enough.

He did not think about Weber.

Not yet.

## 24

"The work phone wasn't with Weber."

Jonas said it as he walked past.

Daniel needed a second to understand.

"When?"

"Both evenings."

"How do you know?"

Jonas set his tablet on Daniel's desk.

An internal handover list from the subcontractor. Old-fashioned. Names, dates, signatures.

The phone was registered to Nils Weber but had been handed off as the on-call phone during night shifts.

Both times to other employees.

Daniel took the tablet.

"Signatures genuine?"

"HR confirmed the shifts. Two employees confirmed them too."

"Could have been filled in afterward."

"Could have. That's why I pulled the device records the employer keeps for internal assignment."

Jonas opened the next view.

At the start of each shift, the device had been signed in under the account of the employee on call. Not proof of every minute. But consistent with the handover list.

"Who had it when the call went to Mika's number?"

Jonas gave him a name. It was not Weber.

"Why did he call?"

"Still open."

Daniel did not want to close the point immediately.

"The 1:14 a.m. access?"

"Not Weber either."

Jonas swiped on.

The access badge was personalized. But the reader at the side door had been running in emergency mode that night. Security had issued several shift badges in a batch.

"Who got Weber's badge?"

"So far all we know is: not Weber."

"Video?"

Jonas opened the clip.

The camera showed the side door at an angle. A man in a cap held the badge to the reader. His face was only partly visible. The man was not Weber.

Daniel now knew Weber from the staff photo.

Height, build, gait did not match.

"Security?"

"Confirmed the batch issue. Documentation's bad. Three badges got mixed up within the same hour."

Daniel looked at the two images.

A man in a cap. Again, not Weber.

"Why was it recorded under him?"

"Because his access was stored as shift supervisor and the badge was issued in his name."

"And the second phone connection?"

"Same on-call phone. Different shift employee."

Daniel leaned back.

The three strongest points against Weber did not collapse all at once.

They became ordinary one by one.

Work phone: shared.

Night access: wrong user.

Phone contacts: on-call duty.

The harmless explanation had not merely existed.

It had been right.

Daniel opened Weber's shift schedule himself.

On one of the evenings, Weber was demonstrably at another branch. Access there at 8:04 p.m., meeting record until 10:15. Reaching the suspicious location afterward would barely have fit the timing.

"Why didn't we have this yesterday?"

Jonas looked at him. "Because we had the hits first."

"That's not an answer."

"Because the other branch wasn't in our first project scope. The HR response only came today."

Daniel nodded.

The new route had been faster, but not complete.

"Where's Weber?"

Jonas hesitated.

"At home."

"Why?"

"His employer removed him from the event project yesterday."

Daniel looked at him.

"Based on whose information?"

"Security review. Our case was part of it."

"Only from the project?"

"Temporarily no night shifts either. Employer decision."

"Paid?"

"Don't know."

Lena was standing in the doorway.

She had apparently heard enough.

Daniel looked at the successful operation from the night before, still open on his second monitor.

"The success still stands," he said.

"Yes."

"The other hit was real."

"Yes."

"The wider review group led us to the storage unit."

"Yes."

He liked that she did not help him.

"And we were wrong about Weber."

"Yes."

Daniel picked up his phone.

"I want to talk to him."

Lena raised an eyebrow. "Why?"

"Because our assessment got him removed from the project."

"Then talk to him. But he doesn't owe you a useful scene."

Daniel looked at her.

"What's that supposed to mean?"

"That he doesn't have to react calmly, reasonably, and gratefully just so you can categorize the damage more comfortably."

Before calling Weber, Daniel spoke to his employer.

The division manager was polite and visibly careful not to say the wrong thing.

"The security assessment has been corrected," Daniel said.

"We received it."

"Then Mr. Weber can return to the project?"

A short pause.

"That's an internal personnel decision."

"Based on what current information?"

"We've already reassigned the shifts."

"That doesn't answer the question."

"Mr. Reuter, I'm not putting someone back into a sensitive area today after a security office told me yesterday to remove him, even if a correction arrives today."

Daniel heard the logic.

It was not absurd.

That was exactly what made it so uncomfortable.

"For how long?"

"Until we've reviewed it internally."

"What are you reviewing if the specific association has fallen away?"

"That we haven't missed anything."

Daniel closed his eyes briefly.

The correction had triggered the same sentence he himself had been working with for days.

Better to check once more.

"And the night shifts?"

"Already assigned for next week."

"Can that be reversed?"

"It can. Whether we do is another question."

Daniel thanked him and ended the call.

The damage was not spectacular.

No one had publicly branded Weber.

It was enough that a correct correction moved through one organization more slowly than the earlier security concern had.

Weber did not come in. They spoke by phone.

His voice was higher than Daniel had expected.

"You told my boss I was a security risk."

"We reported an anomaly that did not hold up."

"Great."

"The correction was sent as well."

"Even better."

"It's already been sent."

"Then I can just go back tomorrow, right?"

Daniel knew the answer now.

"Your employer is still reviewing it internally."

Weber laughed once.

"Of course."

"I spoke to him."

"That makes it much better."

"No."

The answer came out faster than Daniel expected.

Weber was silent for a moment.

Then he became angrier.

Not controlled anger. He jumped between accusations, money, and his work schedule.

"I'm not getting the shifts back. They've already put someone else on next week."

"I can't decide that."

"But you could decide the other thing."

Daniel said nothing.

"You know what my boss said? Until this is cleared internally, he doesn't want me on the site. It's cleared with you now. Not with him."

"The correction says the grounds for suspicion against you were not substantiated."

"Grounds for suspicion." Weber laughed shortly. "Great phrase."

Daniel let him talk.

"My wife thinks I did some kind of shit because my boss isn't allowed to tell me what's going on. So I get to explain that everything's fine while I'm sitting at home."

"Mr. Weber—"

"Don't."

"I only wanted—"

"What? To apologize?"

Daniel was silent.

Weber waited.

"I can tell you that the specific association was wrong," Daniel said. "And that we passed on the correction."

"I know that now too."

"I can't promise you anything more at the moment."

"That's exactly the problem."

The call did not end immediately.

Weber breathed audibly.

Then he said, "My son asked yesterday why I'm not allowed to work. I told him because adults make mistakes sometimes. He thought I meant me."

Daniel closed his eyes briefly.

Weber added at once, "And no, you don't have to feel bad now. That won't get me my shifts back either."

Then he hung up.

Daniel kept the phone at his ear for another moment.

After the call, Daniel opened the correction's distribution chain.

Everything on their side was correct.

**Association with Weber not substantiated. Security assessment corrected.**

Sent.

Received.

Acknowledged.

Three green status markers.

Daniel opened the employer's shift overview to the extent it was available in the case.

Weber was no longer assigned to the event project for the next few days.

No contradiction in the system.

Only two different realities, both correctly documented.

The security office had withdrawn its assessment.

The employer had reacted to the earlier assessment and changed its staffing plan.

Nothing required the second organization to reverse its decision at the same speed.

Daniel looked at the three green checks.

That was what a successful correction looked like technically.

And Weber was still at home.

In the after-action report, Daniel did not write only **misattribution corrected**.

Underneath, he added:

**Downstream effects outside the originating case are not automatically reversible.**

Then he had the time confirmed when the correction reached the employer.

Timestamp: 10:14 a.m.

The new shift plan had been approved at 9:52.

Twenty-two minutes.

No bad intent. No system failure.

Only a decision that had taken effect earlier in another process than the later correction did.

Daniel added both times to the after-action report.

The twenty-two minutes did not explain the harm. But they showed how quickly a provisional assessment could develop a life of its own once another organization acted on it.

After speaking with Weber, Daniel did not open the next case immediately.

He walked through the correction path himself.

Security assessment changed.

Employer informed.

Internal project access: restriction removed.

Shift schedule: outside their control.

Personnel decision: outside their control.

Family: not a system field at all.

On the screen, the correction looked almost complete.

In Weber's life, it was not.

Daniel did not call the employer again. He would only have created the same pressure in the opposite direction that he had criticized before.

Instead, he added an explicit note to the project record that the person-specific grounds for suspicion were **not substantiated** and must not be carried forward as a residual flag in later reviews.

Jonas saw the sentence.

"So he disappears from our group."

"From this suspicion."

"And if something new comes later?"

"Then it's new."

After the call, Daniel pulled the timeline back. Work phone. Night access. Shift overview. At every point, there had been a harmless alternative explanation. He had seen it. He had simply weighted it less heavily as the danger of not acting became more real.

The wider review group had genuinely worked. Weber had still been wrongly implicated. Both remained true.

The problem had not been the absence of a counterhypothesis. It had been what that alternative explanation was still worth in Daniel's calculation.

Beside two early **open** markers, he wrote:

**Alternative explanation existed; its weight declined over time. Why?**

Lena read over his shoulder.

"That's the better question."

Daniel nodded.

## 25

8:11 p.m.

Daniel had seen the time dozens of times.

It was in the source's first message.

**8:11 p.m. Hallway outside your mother's apartment. You pressed the mailbox nameplate back into place.**

At the time, the line had been personal above all else.

Now something else bothered him.

Because of Weber, he had gone back to the beginning.

Not systematically.

No large timeline on the wall monitor.

Just the old messages, his notes, and the places where he had later seen meaning that had not existed yet at the time.

The first warning.

The yellow strip.

The pickup.

The mailbox.

Daniel stopped at the time.

8:11 p.m.

He opened his calendar.

The appointment with Jana was there as a private block. No address.

He opened Jana's message from that week.

**I'm already upstairs. Realtor may come by again later.**

Then the documents for the warehouse complex.

Then the internal assignment of the case.

The sequence had been in front of him for days.

He had simply never put the points beside one another.

On the evening with Jana, Warehouse C was not yet his case.

Berg had put the matter on Daniel's desk only afterward, not hours before.

Daniel sat still.

He checked the times again because the first reaction had come too quickly.
