Maybe the matter had already been sitting somewhere in his work area.

No.

It had arrived the following morning.

Maybe Berg had mentioned it verbally beforehand.

Daniel opened his appointment records and his messages with Berg.

Nothing.

Maybe another task had already linked him to the warehouse address.

He searched his earlier cases for Warehouse C.

No hit.

Unit 17 either.

His only earlier contact with the same complex was the closed fraud matter he had found only later through the address search.

That was not enough to predict that this case would land with him.

Daniel also opened the team's handover list from the following morning.

The warehouse complex first appeared there assigned to him at 8:34 a.m.

He searched the version from the night before.

No line.

No advance marker.

No indication Berg had already reserved the matter for Daniel.

That did not rule out Berg or someone else having discussed it earlier.

It only ruled out Daniel's normal work records as the explanation for the observation in the apartment hallway.

Daniel pushed his chair back. The wheels sounded suddenly too loud against the floor.

He stood for a moment.

His right hand rested on the edge of the desk. His fingers were cold.

Then he sat down again.

Someone had captured the scene in the hallway to the minute before the case was even his.

That could be coincidence.

Someone could have been watching Daniel for another reason.

Someone could have been watching many people and later pulled out the one detail that fit.

The source could have received the detail later from whoever had observed it.

Daniel wrote down the three possibilities.

Then a fourth.

**Time reconstructed later.**

Possible. Jana had received a call from the realtor. Emails carried timestamps. Someone could have reconstructed 8:11 later from other data.

Daniel opened the original first source message.

It had arrived before he had widely shared the connection to Warehouse C.

The private reference was already there.

So any later reconstruction would still have had to happen before that message.

Still possible.

Just more complicated.

He called Jana.

She answered on the fourth ring.

"If you're calling to ask whether the mailbox is still straight, I'm hanging up."

Daniel looked at the time.

"What time did the realtor call that night?"

Silence.

"Which call?"

"When we came out of the apartment. You were already on the street."

"Daniel, that was weeks ago."

"You got an email afterward."

"Of course I got an email afterward. That man sends more email than a newsletter."

"Can you check?"

"Why?"

Daniel did not answer immediately.

"Because I need the sequence."

Jana sighed.

He heard her typing.

"Here. 8:13 p.m. Subject: purchase price. He called two minutes before that. Is that enough?"

Daniel looked at the anonymous message.

8:11 p.m.

"Were you already outside then?"

"I was in front of the building. You were heading toward the subway."

"Sure?"

"Because I almost walked into that broken planter while I was on the phone. Yes."

Daniel remembered the planter. Gray concrete, chipped corner beside the entrance.

"And the nameplate?"

"What about it?"

"When did I press it back in?"

"Before we went outside."

"Approximately?"

"Two minutes before? Three? Why?"

So the time did not merely fit an email visible afterward.

It fit a brief event in the hallway the realtor had not seen and that had never been described in any message.

"Did the realtor see me at the mailbox that night?"

"No. He'd already left."

Daniel said nothing.

"What's going on?" Jana asked.

"Nothing I can explain cleanly yet."

"That's one of your worst answers."

"I know."

"Then try a better one."

Daniel looked at the four explanations on his sheet.

"Someone knew something about that evening before I was assigned a case in which the same source later appeared again."

Jana was silent.

"Person?"

"Source."

"And you think someone was already watching you before that."

Daniel did not answer immediately.

"I'm checking exactly that."

"Which means yes when you say it."

"No. It means the sequence no longer fits my previous explanation."

Jana sighed.

"Fine. That's all I know. The call was at 8:11, the email at 8:13, and you pressed that stupid label in shortly before."

"Thanks."

"And?"

"That's enough."

"Daniel."

He almost said again that he would call later.

The sentence was already there.

He did not say it.

"I don't know what it means yet."

"Okay."

"But it's important."

"Also okay."

"I'll call you when I have more."

"Then don't say later this time."

Despite everything, Daniel smiled briefly.

"I won't."

After the call, he put the times side by side.

Evening with Jana.

8:11 p.m. mailbox / realtor call.

Following morning, 8:34 a.m.: first documented assignment of the warehouse complex to Daniel.

Later: minor incident.

After that: first anonymous message containing the private detail.

Later: source knows about his problematic data cross-check.

The old explanation no longer fit. The source had not begun reacting to Daniel only after he became important to the case. The private reference predated the documented assignment.

That did not prove selection. Someone could have been watching him for another reason, reconstructed the time, or kept many people under observation. But Daniel had to allow a second possibility: someone had already had a reason to find his behavior interesting.

He wrote down two questions.

**Who could have assessed me professionally beforehand?**

**Who could have expected me to become relevant before the case was assigned?**

Then only one note:

**Private reference predates Warehouse C assignment. Review possible selection point.**

He opened no personnel files and no lists of names. Later access could not retroactively turn someone into a pre-case candidate.

The new chronology changed no operational fact. Mika remained involved. The wider review group had genuinely worked. Weber had been wrongly burdened. The source had delivered real information.

Only Daniel's place in the sequence had shifted.

Not: who knew the case?

Who knew him?

And why had that mattered before Daniel himself knew there was a case?

## 26

"You're holding back information and calling it source review."

Lena stood in the doorway.

Daniel did not look away from the screen.

"How do you know I'm holding anything back?"

"Because I know you."

"That's not a reassuring argument right now."

She came in and closed the door.

Daniel still had the note on the pre-case chronology open. Not in the wider source file. Only in his narrowest working view.

**Private reference predates Warehouse C assignment. Review possible selection point.**

Nothing more.

Lena did not sit down immediately.

"What did you find?"

Daniel looked at the sentence.

The easy reaction would have been to turn the screen toward her. Lena was the person who had challenged his decisions most aggressively over the past few days. She was also the person who still refused to show him part of her own oversight path.

Both were true.

"The private reference in the first message predates my case assignment."

Now she sat down.

"Sure?"

"Yes. The observation at my mother's building was in the evening. Berg assigned me the warehouse complex the following morning."

Lena looked at the monitor Daniel still had not turned toward her.

"And the time?"

"Cross-checked with Jana. No prior involvement in my records; handover list starts the next morning. Verbal pre-assignment isn't ruled out."

Lena nodded.

"So not: someone definitely knew you'd get the case."

"No."

"Someone had you in view beforehand."

"That's as far as I'll go."

Daniel turned the monitor slightly toward her after all.

Lena read the sentence once.

"And now?"

"Now I find out who could have assessed me professionally before the case."

"That wasn't my question."

Daniel looked at her.

"What was?"

"What do you do with the possibility that you were selected, and the information was only the means?"

Daniel leaned back.

"I control the information paths more tightly."

"You're already doing that."

"Then more tightly."

Lena ran a finger along the edge of the table.

"That's control. Not an answer."

"Right now, control is a pretty useful answer."

"For you."

"For the case."

"Maybe."

Daniel noticed his reaction to the word.

"Say it."

"What?"

"You've got something in mind."

Lena exhaled.

"If someone selected you beforehand, maybe it wasn't because you're reckless."

Daniel was silent.

"Because you're credible," she said. "Because you normally take boundaries seriously. Because your decisions carry weight precisely when you cross one anyway."

"That's a very large theory built on one timestamp in an apartment hallway."

"Yes."

"And you have no evidence for it."

"No."

Daniel stood.

It helped. Not much.

He went to the window. Outside, someone pushed a file cart across the yard. A completely ordinary movement. Daniel watched it longer than necessary and was annoyed with himself for doing so.

"We protected people," he said.

"Yes."

The security guard might not have been the last. The wider review group had exposed a real preparatory step. Daniel disputed neither point.

He turned back to her.

"Then don't talk as if every step is just political material."

"I'm not."

"Sounds like it."

"Daniel, the fact that the steps actually work is exactly what makes them institutionally useful."

He said nothing.

Lena took her tablet from her bag and placed it on the table.

"This has been circulating in the project group since yesterday."

Daniel went back and read it.

An internal overview of accelerated cross-case cooperation. His name was not in the heading. His problematic data cross-check was still in the document. Beside it was the later operational success.

The overview did not tell a hero story. It used the case as an example.

**Time lost through separated information environments. Operationally effective countermeasure. Structural action required.**

Daniel read the line twice.

"Berg?"

"Provided it. Heller was on the distribution."

"You?"

Lena looked at him.

"Me too."

Daniel pushed the tablet back.

"That proves nothing about the source."

"I didn't say it did."

"It doesn't prove anyone needed my decision either."

"No."

"Then what?"

Lena did not take the tablet back.

"That your decision didn't stay with you."

Daniel looked at the overview again.

He had known the case was being used internally as a reference. He had not known this form of it.

Berg had assessed the access. The project structure had grown out of it. Of course the case had become a reference point internally.

It still felt different now that Daniel knew someone had been observing him beforehand.

The mechanism might include not only his information, but his reasonableness.

Daniel sat down.

"So what?"

"Boundary."

"Which one?"

"One that doesn't depend on whether the next access works again."

Daniel looked at her.

"You want us to shut down the project structure."

"No."

"Then what?"

"I want you to define when it ends. Before the next situation arrives."

Daniel opened the project note.

The end date was already there.

"It's time-limited."

"A duration isn't the same as a return boundary."

"Yes, it is."

"Not if one sentence is enough on the final day: threat not fully resolved."

Daniel was silent.

Lena tapped the screen.

"Case-specific. Temporary. Independent review. And when the acute case basis is gone, no automatic transition into the next complex."

"When the threat is over."

"When the acute basis is over."

"Those aren't always the same."

"Exactly why I'm saying it."

Daniel stood again.

"And if we still have fragments at the end?"

"Then we justify it again."

"And lose time again."

"Maybe."

"You saw the security guard."

"Yes."

"You know what waiting can cost."

"Yes."

Lena stayed seated.

"And now you know what success can cost too."

Daniel looked at her.

"Weber."

"Among other things."

"The find was still real."

"Yes."

Again she denied him the easy opposing position.

Daniel returned to the desk, left his private note alone, and opened the formal project record.

He wrote:

**Use exclusively within the defined terrorism case complex.**

Underneath:

**No automatic continuation once the acute case-specific basis ends. Continuation requires renewed documented justification and authorization.**

Then:

**Exceptional decisions and expansions will be independently reviewed after the operation; full logging will be retained.**

Lena read along.

"Who's independent?"

Daniel stopped.

"Not me."

"That's a start."

"Not Berg alone."

"Also a start."

Daniel added the responsible oversight function from the existing project structure without entering a person's name.

"And the data?" Lena asked.

"Returned under the defined rules. No silent permanent pool."

"Write it."

Daniel did.

He did not like the tone she was using with him.

That was not an argument against the sentence.

When he finished, he did not send the change only to Lena. Berg received it. So did the defined oversight channel.

The delivery states changed one after another. Berg: read. Oversight function: delivered. Lena: in the room. The boundary was no longer merely Daniel's intention.

"What if I have good reasons tomorrow?" Daniel asked.

"Then you justify them against your own boundary. You don't pretend the boundary never existed."

Daniel nodded.

"Is that a commitment?" Lena asked.

"Yes."

"Even if the structure works again before then?"

Daniel looked at the note. "When the acute basis is gone, we justify continuation again."

Lena nodded once.

Daniel said, "You're acting like control is only my problem."

"No."

"You still haven't fully explained your own path to me."

Lena stopped at the door.

"True."

"And at the same time I'm supposed to accept that you're protecting my autonomy."

"No. You're supposed to accept that I want your decisions to remain reviewable. Whether you trust me while I do that is a different question."

Daniel felt anger.

"Very reassuring."

"That wasn't the goal."

She opened the door.

"Success is not an argument for why you need less oversight right now."

Daniel looked at her.

"I never said it was."

"Good."

This time she did not add *not yet*.

She left.

Daniel remained alone with the project note.

At that moment, he meant every word.

## 27

The next message came that afternoon.

No greeting.

No reference to what had happened before.

**The next stage runs through tomorrow's setup. A new delivery authorization was added today. Don't check only the name. Check the access around it.**

Underneath was an identifier from the event logistics system.

Then a blank line.

**6:32 p.m. Your sister left the practice through the rear exit.**

Daniel stood before he knew why.

The chair rolled into the cabinet behind him.

Jonas looked up from his desk.

"What?"

Daniel held the phone tightly.

6:32 p.m.

It was 6:47.

Fifteen minutes.

"Check the delivery authorization."

"Which one?"

Daniel gave him the identifier.

"Source?"

"Yes."

Jonas was already typing.

Daniel opened Jana's contact.

His thumb stayed over the call icon.

He did not call.

Not yet.

The fact that someone knew which exit Jana had used proved exactly one thing: someone knew it.

Daniel wrote the sentence on paper.

On a separate sheet, outside the source file and away from the delivery identifier.

Underneath:

**Private knowledge increases pressure, not threat evidence.**

Daniel drew another line beneath it. He assigned no urgency level beside Jana's name.

He could have used it as an argument without effort. Fifteen-minute-old observation. His sister. Unknown source. Everyone in the room would have understood why it made him want to move faster.

That was exactly the problem.

If the delivery authorization was dangerous, it had to be dangerous without Jana. If it was harmless, his fear could not replace the missing second anchor.

He slid the sheet where Jonas could see it.

The separation should not exist only in Daniel's head.

His handwriting remained neat. His body did not cooperate. His neck was rigid, his mouth dry, and while Jonas checked the delivery authorization Daniel had to stop himself twice from calling Jana before he even knew what he could tell her.

"You're faster right now," Jonas said.

Daniel looked up.

"What?"

"With the questions. Normally you let me finish one status before you start the next."

Daniel looked at the sheet.

Jonas was right.

"Then slower," he said.

"Because of Jana?"

"Yes."

Jonas simply nodded.

Daniel liked that he did not turn it into a psychological scene.

"Operationally, she doesn't count," Daniel said.

"Understood."

"If I pull her into a justification anyway, tell me."

"I will."

The pressure did not disappear.

It simply had a witness now, someone who could stop Daniel from quietly turning it into evidence.

"Authorization exists," Jonas said.

Daniel moved to his screen.

"Since when?"

"Created today at 4:08 p.m."

"For whom?"

"New delivery service for setup."

"Created only, or approved?"

Jonas opened the next view.

"Formally approved. 4:41 p.m."

"By whom?"

"Event logistics. Two approval steps."

"Why new?"

"The existing provider handed off part of tomorrow's route. It's in the logistics note."

Daniel read it himself.

Capacity bottleneck. Replacement service provider. Driver lists partly to follow.

A plausible explanation.

Not even unusual.

"Company connection?"

"Pulling it."

Jonas opened the existing project data. The delivery service had not appeared before. No direct connection to Mika. No known link to the storage unit they had most recently found.

"So all that's confirmed right now is that the identifier is real," Jonas said.

"And new."

"Yes."

"Nothing more."

"Nothing more."

Daniel showed him the message down to the blank line.

Jonas read it.

"'Access around it' is pretty convenient."

"How convenient?"

"Could mean drivers. Shift planners. Access badges. People who can see the driver lists. People who approve the delivery area. Or basically anyone working anywhere near it."

"So not a criterion."

"Not yet."

Jonas marked the identifier **confirmed** in the working view and its significance **open**.

Daniel now showed him the final line too.

Jonas read it and said nothing at first.

"That's current?"

"Fifteen minutes old."

"Shit."

"Yes."

"You calling her?"

"In a minute."

"Why in a minute?"

Daniel looked at him.

"Because I want to know what I can actually tell her first."

"And if she's on the road right now?"

The objection was good enough that Daniel disliked it.

He still did not call yet.

"First check whether the delivery authorization appears in any other current strand."

Jonas turned back to the screen.

Lena came in carrying a printout.

"What is it?"

Daniel gave her only the factual part first.

She read the message down to the blank line.

"Identifier confirmed?"

"Yes. Significance open. Company unremarkable so far. Time window only says tomorrow's setup, no exact time."

Lena looked at him.

"What are you leaving out?"

"Why does something always have to be missing?"

"Because you're standing again."

He was still standing. Only then did he notice.

He showed her the final line.

Lena read it once.

Her face barely changed.

"Jana is not evidence for the setup."

"I know."

"Then don't treat her like a hit in the same column."

Daniel pointed to his note.

**Private knowledge does not confirm the threat hypothesis.**

"I'm not."

"Good."

"Does that make it feel better now?"

"No."

Daniel finally sat down.

Jonas said, "I have a second factual point."

They turned toward him.

"The driver lists still aren't complete. Two names are missing even though the access authorizations are supposed to become active tomorrow morning."

"Unusual?" Lena asked.

"Bad practice. But logistics set a deadline for late submission by tonight."

"So a process gap, not yet a threat connection."

"Exactly."

Daniel looked at the time.

6:54 p.m.

The sentence about Jana was now twenty-two minutes old.

Berg arrived with his jacket still over one arm.

"What do we have?"

Daniel explained the delivery authorization first. Identifier genuine. Replacement provider real. Driver lists incomplete. No confirmed offense connection.

Then the line about Jana.

Berg stopped at the door.

"Can you reach her?"

"Haven't called yet."

Berg looked at him.

"Why?"

Daniel felt irritation because two people had now asked the same good question.

"Because I don't want to enter the call with an instruction before I know what we actually have."

Berg nodded once.

"Then what can we properly check before tomorrow morning?"

Lena answered first.

"Delivery service within the existing project context. Existing access roles. Submitted drivers. Known contact overlaps. No new broad collection just because of the source wording."

Jonas said, "And we can break 'access around it' into concrete testable categories. Otherwise we end up checking everything."

Berg looked at Daniel.

"How much time?"

"Setup starts early. We don't have a precise threat time."

"The operational question is what we can rule out tonight without being blind tomorrow," Berg said. "The source's credibility comes second."

Daniel nodded.

That was Berg at his best. No theory about manipulation. No demand for maximum reach. Only the question of which options needed to remain open.

Lena tapped Daniel's project note, the one he had changed that morning.

"And your boundary?"

"Still stands."

"Then it applies now."

"Yes."

"Even with Jana in the message."

Daniel looked at her.

"Jana isn't evidence."

"That wasn't the question."

Daniel was silent briefly.

"Yes. Even now."

Jonas created three columns.

**Delivery authorization. Access context. Private knowledge.**

Under the third he wrote only: **handle separately**.

Daniel looked at it and left it there.

Then they began the narrow review.

The replacement provider was real. Contract genuine. Contact reachable. Two of the already submitted drivers had ordinary prior work at other events. Nothing unusual.

One access role in the logistics system had been expanded that afternoon. The reason was documented: the responsible employee was covering for a sick colleague.

Another hit initially looked like an unusual overnight permission. It was a standard authorization for setup phases.

Everything explainable.

Daniel took a blank sheet and drew a line down the middle.

On the left he wrote:

**Real threat + pressure tactic.**

On the right:

**Real fragments + deliberate steering.**

Jonas looked at it.

"Those are almost the same thing."

"For the first review steps, yes."

"And later?"

"Later, what holds independently decides."

Lena stood beside the desk.

"And what about the version where the delivery authorization is completely harmless and the Jana line exists only to make you stop treating it as harmless?"

Daniel added a third line beneath the right side:

**Harmless factual point + psychological pressure.**

He did not like the line.

It stayed anyway.

Jonas pointed to the first column. "What would make that bigger?"

"A second current anchor. Not from the source."

"And what would make it smaller?"

"A clean explanation for the open access roles and drivers."

Lena said, "Then at least we have criteria before we start inventing them."

Daniel looked at the message again.

The source had named no specific person. No vehicle. No time for an offense. Only a genuine new identifier and wording that could widen the view almost indefinitely.

**Check the access around it.**

The longer Daniel looked at the sentence, the less information it contained.

And the more action could be derived from it.

That was new enough to bother him.

In the working view, he marked both what had been confirmed and what the source had **not** supplied: no concrete threat time, no named person, no independent indication that the delivery authorization had been manipulated.

Berg read the addition and nodded.

"Good. Then investigate the gap, not the tone of the message."

Then came the first small irregularity.

One of the two missing driver names had been added, but the listed mobile number, according to the available records, had not belonged to him for months.

"Could be sloppy admin," Jonas said.

"Yes."

"Check?"

"Narrowly."

The query to the service provider went out.

Berg looked at the time.

"And if we have nothing by midnight?"

No one answered immediately.

Daniel thought of the four paragraphs in his self-imposed return boundary.

No automatic expansion.

New justification.

Documented review.

Then he thought of Jana at the rear exit.

His mind tried to pull both into the same calculation.

He forced them apart again.

"Then we decide with what we have," he said.

"What does that mean concretely?" Berg asked.

"If the narrow checks leave a real gap and an independent anchor appears, we put the broader option back on the table. Not before."

Lena looked at him.

"That's the boundary."

"Yes."

Jonas said, "And Jana?"

Daniel picked up his phone.

"That's a different decision."

He stood.

This time at least he knew he was standing.

He went into the empty corridor and called his sister.

## 28

"What do you know?"

Jana did not say hello.

Daniel stood in the empty corridor.

Beyond the fire door at the far end, he could hear a cart rolling across the floor. Nothing else.

"That someone saw you leave the practice through the rear exit today at 6:32 p.m."

Silence.

"Who?"

"I don't know."

"Why would someone tell you that?"

Daniel leaned one shoulder against the wall.

"It came with a tip connected to my case."

"Is the tip real?"

"Part of it."

"Which part?"

"A new delivery authorization exists. We don't know whether it has anything to do with a threat."

Jana was quiet briefly.

"And what do you think?"

Daniel closed his eyes.

It was exactly the question he asked other people.

"That the sender wants to put pressure on me. And that a real threat could still exist behind it."

"That's not an answer about me."

"No."

"So again: what do you know about me?"

Daniel opened his eyes.

"That someone knew about the rear exit and saw you there today."

"More?"

"No."

"What do you think?"

"That it's connected to my case."

"How sure?"

"I'm not."

"Good."

Daniel felt immediate resistance to the word.

"That doesn't mean you should ignore it."

"I didn't say that."

"Don't go home alone tonight."

"I already did."

"Then don't stay there."

"Why?"

"Jana."

"No. What do you know that makes my apartment a problem?"

Daniel pressed thumb and forefinger against his forehead.

"Nothing specific."

"Then don't tell me to leave my apartment as if that were a fact."

"Someone knows your routes."

"Then I change my routes."

"That might not be enough."

"A hotel might not be enough either."

Daniel said nothing.

Jana was not being reckless.

That would have been easier.

She was making exactly the distinction he had just defended in the conference room: known, assumed, open.

"What were you planning to do tonight?" he asked.

"Go home, eat, first appointment tomorrow at eight."

"Cancel it."

"Already did."

Daniel straightened.

"When?"

"While you were probably still deciding whether to call me."

He was silent.

"Claudia noticed I was bothered," Jana said. "I told her someone might be watching my routines."

"You told her about the case?"

"No. About me."

The correction landed.

"What are you doing?"

"She's picking me up. I'm not driving my car tomorrow. I canceled the first appointment; she's taking the second. I'm not sleeping at home tonight."

"Who knows besides Claudia?" Daniel asked.

"No one who needs to know where I'm staying."

"Staff?"

"They only know I'll be late tomorrow or not in at all."

"Family?"

"No."

"And if Claudia can't drive you?"

"Then I take a cab and change the destination if anything feels wrong."

Daniel noticed himself automatically testing her answers for weaknesses.

They were not bad answers. Daniel kept searching anyway for a place where he could take control again.

"Do you have cash?" he asked.

A short silence.

"What?"

"In case you have to turn off the phone—"

"Daniel."

He stopped.

"Yes."

"You're on escape plan number seven now."

"Three."

"That doesn't make it better."

He exhaled briefly.

"Okay."

Jana said, "I don't need a complete operational plan from you. I need an honest update when you know more."

"You'll get one."

"Even if the update is: we still know nothing more about me."

Daniel was silent for a moment.

"Even then."

Daniel let out some of his breath.

Not enough.

"Where?"

"With someone I know."

"Claudia?"

"Maybe."

"Jana."

"No."

"No what?"

"I'm not giving you the address."

Daniel pushed away from the wall.

"Why?"

"Because you just told me you don't know who is getting information about me."

The sentence stayed between them.

"My phone isn't the source file."

"I didn't say it was."

"Then what?"

"That having fewer people know my location while nobody knows where the information is coming from isn't completely crazy."

Daniel walked three steps down the corridor and back.

"I'm your brother."

"Yes."

"And the person trying to protect you right now."

"Also yes."

"Then I need to know where you are."

"No."

The word was not loud.

It reminded him of their mother's apartment. Jana arranging the realtor appointment while Daniel was still trying to postpone a decision. Back then he had mistaken her speed for impatience.

Now the danger was real.

And still he ran into the same boundary.

"You're doing it again," Jana said.

"What?"

"Responsibility turns into jurisdiction very quickly for you."

Daniel laughed once without humor.

"This time it isn't just an apartment."

"I know."

"Someone watched you today."

"I know that too."

"Then—"

"Stop."

Daniel was silent.

Jana continued more calmly.

"I'm taking it seriously. That's why I'm changing my routine. I'm not alone. Claudia knows. My phone stays on. I'm not going to the practice at the normal time tomorrow."

"And if someone follows you?"

"Then I don't go to the destination and I call the police."

"And if you notice something before that?"

"Same thing."

"I can arrange protection for you."

"If you can tell me I'm specifically a target, we can talk about that."

"By the time I can tell you that, it could be too late."

"And until you can, it could also be wrong."

Daniel stopped.

She was right.

He hated it.

"You could at least give the address to me only."

"And if your environment is exactly the problem?"

Daniel felt his jaw tighten.

"That's not fair."

"No."

Jana sounded tired.

"But you asked me to deal reasonably with limited information. That's what I'm doing."

Daniel wanted to argue.

He could not find a factual point, only his need to know where she was.

That was not enough.

"Okay," he said.

Jana was quiet briefly.

"Okay?"

"I think it's shit."

"I believe you."

"But okay."

He heard a door and voices in the background.

"Claudia's here," Jana said.

"Let her drive."

"That was the plan."

"Phone on."

"Yes."

"No routine routes."

"Yes."

"And text me when you've arrived. No address. Just arrived."

A short pause.

"I will."

Daniel rested his head against the wall.

That was the compromise.

It was her compromise, not his.

Daniel remained there with the phone in his hand and went through the facts as if Jana were a case file, even though that was exactly the wrong way to think about her.

She was not alone.

She was changing route and vehicle.

She had canceled the first appointment.

She was keeping her phone on.

She had a clear response agreed if she noticed someone following her.

That was more protection than Daniel would have been able to justify for many people he had advised professionally without a specific threat indicator.

What was missing?

Only what Daniel himself wanted: the address.

He did not rewrite that in his head as a security gap.

At his mother's building, he had still believed a joint decision meant Jana had to wait for his final check. Now the situation was more serious and the temptation stronger to turn concern into jurisdiction.

Jana had still drawn the same boundary.

Information, yes.

Decision over her daily life, no.

Daniel put the phone away.

He could think her decision was wrong.

He could not reasonably call it unreasonable.

That was the part that took the most control away from him.

"And if I can tell you more later—"

"Then you tell me more."

"Not just: do this."

Jana laughed softly.

"That would be new."

"Don't exaggerate."

"Never."

For a moment they sounded like they used to.

Then the lightness was gone again.

"Daniel?"

"Yes?"

"Don't turn me into evidence for anything just because you're afraid."

He closed his eyes.

"I'm trying not to."

"Good."

She hung up.

Daniel looked at the phone.

Jana had acted reasonably.

She had canceled her appointment, changed her routine, involved another person, taken her own car out of the plan, and agreed on a clear response if anything seemed wrong.

Daniel could barely have justified more control sensibly.

His sense of risk did not fall at all.

Maybe it even rose.

Because now he did not know where she was.

He remained in the corridor for another minute.

Then Jana's message arrived.

**On the way. Claudia's driving.**

No address.

Daniel typed **Okay**.

Deleted it.

Wrote **Let me know when you're there.**

Sent it.

After the call, Daniel stayed in the corridor, looking at the display.

He wanted to text Jana to turn off location services. Then he wanted to know whether Claudia still had the same last name as before, whether her car could be known, whether the route was too predictable.

He wrote none of it.

Instead he opened the working view for the source message and checked whether Jana's line had been carried over anywhere as operational confirmation.

It had not.

**Private knowledge – separate from threat assessment.**

Daniel left the marker in place.

For the first time that evening, not knowing was not something he could close immediately.

Then he went back into the operations room.

His sister was not a variable he had closed.

She was a person who had made her own decision.

That did not make the situation calmer.

Only more honest.

## 29

The narrow review left twenty-seven names.

Too many for certainty.

Too few to ignore the gap.

The number had been on the screen since Jonas removed the last duplicates.

27.

No traffic light. No risk scores. No ranking.

Just people inside the defined project scope who, under the agreed criteria, could even fall within the wider review.

Berg stood in front of the screen.

"We built the project structure for exactly this."

Lena sat at the table with both hands flat beside her laptop.

"To combine existing case-relevant information faster."

"Yes."

"Not to turn twenty-seven people into precautionary suspects."

"No one's doing that."

"Not yet."

Daniel looked at the time.

The overnight review had neither cleared the new delivery authorization nor made it look more suspicious.

The replacement provider was real. The unclear mobile number had turned out to be an old data error. Two drivers had been added and were plausible. Some of the access roles had been explained.

A gap still remained.

Several people could touch relevant information or access during the remaining period, and the narrow review could not fully cover their contact and timing overlaps.

The warning's time window had not been confirmed.

Neither had the possible scale of harm.

The genuine new delivery authorization had.

Daniel had barely slept.

Shortly after midnight, Jana had texted:

**Here. Everything quiet.**

No address.

He had read the message and turned the phone face down.

Now it lay beside his notepad with the screen down.

"Criteria," Daniel said.

Jonas put the list on the large monitor.

"Only people within the defined project scope," he said. "Access to sensitive case information or relevant event access. Plus unexplained contact or timing overlaps inside the defined windows."

"No automatic expansion to their contacts?" Daniel asked.

"No."

"No assessment based on a single time overlap alone?"

"No."

"No action from a hit without human review."

Jonas looked at him. "Obviously."

"Say it anyway."

"No action from a hit without human review."

Lena watched Daniel.

"Broader than yesterday," she said.

"Yes."

"And Weber?"

Daniel looked at her.

The name sat between them even though it was not on the screen.

"Exactly why we need alternative explanations."

"Not a reason to bring in even more people."

Berg said, "And the storage unit?"

Lena turned toward him.

"Exactly why the question is hard."

Berg nodded.

No triumph.

Daniel almost liked him for it in that moment.

"Without the wider review group, we probably wouldn't have connected the temporary driver in time," Berg said. "With the same review group, we weighted Weber wrongly."

"Yes," Lena said.

"Then we don't have a clean example for or against it."

"Correct."

Daniel looked at the 27.

That was worse than a clear failure.

He wrote Weber and the storage unit side by side. Under one: misattribution, employer response, downstream effect. Under the other: real threat strand, probably visible earlier because of the wider review.

No success or failure label.

"Now the decision," Lena said.

Daniel looked at both columns.

Neither was empty.

He did not authorize the twenty-seven-person group because the method had proved itself.

He authorized it despite already knowing that both success and harm to an uninvolved person were part of what the method could produce.

That was a different step.

And he knew it at the moment he took it.

A failure would have pointed in one direction.

Instead, the method had simultaneously exposed a real threat strand and harmed an uninvolved person.

"What remains untouched if we stay narrow?" Daniel asked.

Jonas opened the gap list.

Six people with sensitive information access whose relevant contact times had so far been only partly reviewed. Nine people with event access and at least one unexplained overlap. The rest were roles where data was ambiguous or responses still incomplete.

"And if we review broadly?"

"Then we get more explanation work," Jonas said. "Probably a lot of garbage."

"And maybe a hit," Berg said.

Lena looked at Daniel.

"And maybe another Weber."

Daniel nodded.

"Yes."

She apparently waited for more.

Daniel did not give it to her.

He opened the project note containing his return boundary.

**No automatic continuation or expansion. Continuation only after renewed documented justification and authorization.**

He read the sentence silently.

The acute basis had not disappeared. But this specific wider review was exactly the kind of expansion he did not want to become habitual.

He opened a new decision note.

**Basis:** confirmed new delivery authorization; remaining unexplained access/information gap; narrow setup window; real threat strand in the same complex previously identified only through wider connection review.

Then underneath:

**Countervailing risk:** Weber misattribution; correlation does not establish individual involvement; additional people will be drawn into review.

Lena read along.

"And Jana?" she asked.

Daniel looked at her.

"She's not in there."

"Good."

"She's not evidence."

"I know."

The sentence irritated him less than it had the day before.

Maybe because by now he needed it himself.

Berg sat down.

"Decision?"

Daniel looked at the 27 again.

"We review the defined group."

Lena raised her eyes.

"Completely?"

"Under the criteria. No expansion beyond them."

"And what do we do with hits?"

"Explain first. Decide afterward."

She shook her head once.

"You thought that with Weber too."

"Yes."

Daniel said it without defending himself.

"And I'm authorizing it anyway."

The room was briefly quiet.

Then Jonas released the review.

The first results were boring.

A lawyer had five calls with a state office because jurisdiction had been entered incorrectly.

A technician appeared in nighttime access lists because his account was being used for an automated import.

Two phone numbers now belonged to different people.

One employee had called the same internal office during three relevant time windows. Her father was in the hospital there; the extension had been categorized incorrectly.

One access event looked like a nighttime login and was delayed synchronization.

Jonas swore quietly about duplicates.

"So far, only work," Berg said.

"Good," Lena said.

Daniel looked at the explanations being added beside each hit.

People still made the decisions. They read, called, compared, crossed things out again.

That was exactly why it took time.

After forty minutes, seven hits had been explained.

After an hour, eleven.

Three remained open because responses were missing.

Then Jonas said, "Wait."

One word.

The room changed anyway.

Daniel stepped behind him.

A name appeared in three time windows.

Dr. Lena Vogt.

Her name was there because of repeated contacts with an internal data-protection and oversight unit that were not explained in the project documents, not because of her normal case work.

The times were strikingly close to Daniel's exceptional decisions.

And to two phases in which the source later knew information that had not been public.

Jonas said nothing else.

Berg moved closer.

"What is that?"

Lena looked at the screen.

Her face did not change.

"Contacts."

"I can see that."

"Then you know as much as I can tell you about them right now."

Berg turned toward her.

"Seriously?"

"Yes."

Daniel immediately felt his mind building counterhypotheses.

Oversight questions.

Legal consultation.

Documentation.

A complaint about his first access.

A professional review that had to remain confidential.

Something completely ordinary that only looked wrong because of the time windows.

The possibilities came fast.

Too fast.

He had known them with Weber too.

Shared work phone.

Shift swap.

Wrong access assignment.

He had written them down and then weighted them less.

Now he wanted to test every harmless explanation first.

Daniel noticed the difference in himself before anyone said anything.

Lena noticed that he noticed.

"Say it," she said.

"What?"

"That you're looking for reasons why it's different with me."

Daniel looked at the three time windows.

"I'm looking for alternative explanations."

"Faster than you did with Weber."

"Because I have more context about you."

"Exactly."

The sentence was not mocking.

That made it worse.

Daniel knew her work. Her attitude. Her history in the case. He knew how often she had slowed him down when an easy explanation looked too neat.

All of that was context.

All of it was also personal proximity.

Berg said, "Until we've clarified this, you lose access to the source file and the most sensitive project sections."

Jonas turned halfway toward him.

"Completely?"

"The sensitive parts."

"She has active reviews."

"Then hand them over."

Lena's eyes went to Daniel, not Berg.

Daniel could have objected.

The anomaly was not proof.

The anomaly with Weber had not been proof either.

"Which parts exactly?" Daniel asked.

Berg named the source file, provenance review, and the sensitive project information not widely distributed.

"General operational picture?"

"Only as needed for handover until the review is complete."

Lena said, "If you need the rule, don't make it smaller because you know my name."

Daniel looked at her.

"You could explain what the contacts are."

"Formally."

"That takes time."

"Then it takes time."

"You know what's happening tomorrow and you're still giving me nothing?"

Lena leaned back.

"I'm giving you exactly what I can give you outside the proper channel about a confidential oversight contact: that it exists and can be formally explained."

"That's very little."

"For your personal comfort, yes."

Daniel felt anger.

Jonas looked away.

Berg said, "Daniel."

The decision was already on the table in substance.

Daniel opened the note in which he had documented the criteria for the twenty-seven-person group.

No personal exception.

No clause for people he trusted.

He placed the cursor beneath the hit assessment.

**Temporary restriction of sensitive access pending formal clarification of unexplained contact pattern.**

His fingers rested on the keyboard for a moment.

Then he saved it.

"Temporary," Daniel said.

Lena nodded slowly.

"Of course."

Jonas began opening her active tasks.

"The delivery-role review is with you," he said.

"Goes to you," Lena said.

"The question from the oversight office?"

"Don't open it while my access is suspended. Have it reassigned formally."

"Takes time."

"Yes."

Daniel heard the decision create work immediately.

Lena did not simply close her laptop. She went through each open item one by one.

One question to Jonas.

One document to Berg.

One timeline she was no longer allowed to maintain herself.

No dramatic scene.

Only a professional exclusion becoming real through small hand movements.

Then the access notice appeared on her screen.

**Authorization for sensitive project area temporarily suspended.**

Lena read it.

Her face stayed calm.

"Official enough now?" Daniel asked.

She looked at him.

"For the exclusion? Yes."

"I meant—"

"I know what you meant."

Daniel was silent.

Lena stood.

At the door, she stopped briefly.

"You didn't apply the rule incorrectly."

Daniel looked at her.

The sentence helped less than he expected.

"But?"

"No but."

She opened the door.

"If you need the rule, at least actually apply it."

Then she left.

Jonas was still sitting in front of the screen.

"What do we do with her hit?"

Daniel looked at Lena's name.

"Review it formally."

"And the others?"

"Continue as decided."

Berg nodded and returned to his place.

The room went back to work.

Daniel remained standing for a moment.

An hour earlier there had been twenty-seven names.

Now there were twenty-six names and Lena.

That was factually wrong. Of course she was still one of the twenty-seven.

That was exactly why Daniel noticed the thought.

He did not delete her hit.

He did not change the rule.

He simply could no longer pretend it felt the same when applied to every person.

The rule did.

## 30

Lena would explain the contacts neither in the corridor nor to Daniel alone.

"Formally," she said. "With a record and the responsible office."

Daniel stood opposite her.

"That takes time."

"Then it takes time."

"You know what's happening right now. You still accept being out until then?"

Lena looked at him.

"I accept that you applied the rule. I don't accept that the oversight path becomes worthless the moment it's inconvenient."

Daniel felt anger at the fact that she was using up his time. Her position was not the problem.

Or maybe the problem was that he experienced it that way.

Jonas came out of the analysis room.

"The delivery-role review is stuck."

Lena turned toward him.

"Why?"

"A question is sitting in your restricted area. I can only see that it's there. I started the formal reassignment."

"How long?"

Jonas lifted his shoulders.

"No idea."

Daniel looked at the time.

The exclusion was no longer an abstract marker. It was producing friction immediately.

"Can you tell me what the question is about?" he asked Lena.

"Not without opening the restricted material."

"You might remember it."

"Maybe."

"Lena."

"That's exactly the point."

She walked past him.

An hour later they were sitting in a small room Daniel normally used for quick coordination.

This time a formal record lay on the table. With it sat someone from the internal data-protection and oversight unit. No name Daniel needed to remember. The function was enough.

The door stayed closed.

The oversight officer did not begin with Lena's contacts.

They began with Daniel's first problematic data access.

Date.

Time.

Data environments used.

Rationale.

Documented follow-up.

Then a second matter.

Then a third.

Daniel recognized his own decisions, only written in language that contained neither justification nor accusation.

"Who prepared this compilation?" he asked.

"Ms. Vogt documented the individual decisions. The oversight assessment comes from our unit."

Daniel looked at Lena.

She said nothing.

The officer turned the page.

"After the first access, Ms. Vogt requested a subject-matter assessment. Not operational authorization. Independent documentation and later reviewability."

"How often?"

"Several times."

"When?"

The times were placed on the table.

Daniel knew them.

They were exactly the windows that had appeared unusual in the broad review.

After his first rule violation.

After an expansion.

After an operational decision that later became part of the project documentation.

The pattern had been real.

Only its meaning had been different.

"Why wasn't that explained in the project file?" Daniel asked.

This time Lena answered.

"Because a confidential oversight contact doesn't become more independent by being entered into the operational project it's supposed to review."

"You could have told me an oversight contact existed."

"I did."

Daniel remembered.

*I know more about the oversight path than you need to know right now.*

At the time, it had sounded evasive.

It had still been evasive.

Only with a reason.

"Did anything from this contact leak into the source channel?" Daniel asked the oversight officer.

"Based on everything we've checked: no."

"Access to the early source contents?"

"Not in a form that would explain your first messages."

"The number of hits from my legacy-record search?"

"This contact doesn't explain it."

"Heller's area?"

The officer shook their head.

"This review doesn't allow me to say anything about that."

Daniel nodded.

No new solution.

One fewer old hypothesis.

Lena was not the leak.

Daniel did not put that sentence into the normal source file. He corrected only what belonged there: the unusual contact path had been formally explained and provided no indication of information leakage.

Then he opened the security note that had triggered her temporary restriction.

The old entry remained in the history.

Beside it came the correction.

Not deleted. Refuted.

Lena stood behind him.

"Better than Weber," Daniel said.

"What?"

"We're correcting the origin everywhere we control."

"Yes."

"And you were still out for four hours."

"Also yes."

Daniel looked at the two timestamps.

Exclusion.

Restoration.

Between them were reassigned tasks, a delayed query, and an interim assessment that had needed correction because her independent check was missing.

"I can't reset that," he said.

Lena took her coffee.

"That's the point of temporary."

"That it isn't temporary?"

"The decision can be temporary. The effect can't."

Daniel nodded.

That did not settle the apology.

It only made the apology smaller than what he wanted to apologize for.

Her timing pattern had been real.

His interpretation had not.

Daniel looked at the list of contacts.

He noticed he felt relieved.

He did not like that.

With Weber, he had felt no relief. Only correction.

With Lena, he had been looking for a harmless explanation from the beginning.

And it had been right.

That did not make his reflex cleaner.

Daniel opened Weber's old hit assessment beside it.

He did not want to equate Lena with Weber in any way. Precisely that would have been wrong.

He was only looking at the places where he had documented alternative explanations.

With Weber, they had been there early:

**Work phone shared. Shift swap possible. Badge assignment open.**

With Lena, Daniel had produced almost the same kind of sentences verbally in the first few minutes, only faster and with more conviction.

**Oversight contact. Confidential path. Legitimate documentation.**

The second list had been right.

That did not make the difference in how he had weighted them objective.

Daniel placed the same sentence beneath both cases:

**Personal proximity does not change the evidentiary weight of a counterhypothesis.**

Lena saw it later.

"Nice sentence."

"Too nice?"

"No. Just harder than it looks."

Daniel nodded.

At least now he knew where personal knowledge could be relevant professional context and where it could become preference.

The boundary between the two remained messy.

But it was no longer invisible.

"Can the restriction be lifted?" Berg asked, having joined them for the final part.

"From the perspective of this oversight review, there's no reason it can't," the officer said.

"Then let's do that."

"Not immediately," Lena said.

Berg looked at her.

"Why not?"

"Because the formal restoration should be documented as carefully as the exclusion was."

"You want to stay out longer?"

"No. I don't want you to become less careful with the correction than you were with the suspicion."

Jonas, standing at the door, lowered his eyes.

Daniel could not tell whether he was hiding a smile.

Berg exhaled.

"Then carefully."

Restoring access took another forty minutes.

Forty minutes in which Lena could not reach the sensitive project material.

Jonas had to take over two of her tasks. One question was passed to another role. An interim assessment went into the operational picture without Lena's independent check and came back ten minutes later with a correction she probably would have caught immediately.

No drama.

Only cost.

When the authorization reappeared, Lena was sitting by the coffee machine.

She pressed the button and did not remove the cup.

Daniel stopped beside her.

"You're back in. The internal assessment against you is being corrected."

"I know."

"I'm sorry."

Lena looked at the machine.

"For what?"

"That you were out."

"That was the consequence of your rule."

"The anomaly was real."

"Yes."

"I couldn't exempt you because I know you."

"I didn't ask you to."

Daniel looked at her.

"Then what?"

Lena finally took the cup out.

"Don't pretend *temporary* is a neutral word for the person it hits."

Daniel was silent.

"I was out of a case I'd been working for days for four hours," she said. "Other people had to take over my work. My name was in a security note. That will be corrected. It still happened."

"I know."

"No. Now you know."

She walked toward the elevator.

Daniel did not follow.

He returned to the analysis room and opened the task history covering the four hours Lena had been out.

He called it follow-up, not guilt.

Three items had been reassigned. On one, Jonas had to ask the same question twice because an old oversight note was missing. A second sat open for ten minutes until Berg reassigned responsibility. On the third, the situation picture needed a correction after Lena regained access.

None of it had endangered anyone.

None of it was dramatic.

It was simply work created because, for a few hours, her name had sat in the same category as the other unexplained hits.

Daniel opened the security note.

Beside the original restriction was now the correction:

**Contact pattern explained by legitimate oversight activity. No confirmed information leakage. Restriction lifted.**

The old entry had not disappeared.

It remained above it, with time and author.

Daniel knew why. A corrected case could not look as though the first decision had never existed.

That was exactly what he had wanted with Weber: traceable correction instead of quiet deletion.

With Lena, the same cleanliness suddenly felt like a mark that would remain readable forever.

He closed the note again.

When Lena reappeared in the room, it went quieter for a moment than necessary.

Barely noticeable; only two conversations ended a second too early.

Jonas pushed the tasks he had taken over back toward her.

"This one's done. This one's open. And I probably sorted this one differently than you would have."

Lena sat down.

"Then I'll look at it."

No comment about being back.

No small welcome gesture.

Daniel was grateful for that and found even the gratitude uncomfortable.

The correction had restored her access. It could not restore the fact that everyone in the room had spent four hours seeing her name beside an unexplained contact pattern.

Jonas looked up from his screen.

"Everything okay?"

Daniel thought of the question Jonas now asked too often.

"No."

"Because of Lena?"

"Because of the rule."

Jonas waited.

Daniel corrected himself.

"No. Because of what the rule does when it lands on someone."

"Isn't that the same thing?"

"Not quite."

The rule had not lied. The contact pattern had been real.

It simply had not known what the pattern meant.

People had supplied that meaning.

Daniel included.

New hits from the same broad review were waiting on his phone.

He opened them.

Lena's objection had not been wrong. Her innocence said nothing about whether the other hits were wrong too.

That separation was beginning to feel dangerously reasonable.

## 31

The least conspicuous hit was the one that remained.

A delivery address.

No name Daniel knew.

No contact with Mika.

No unusual role in the event system.

Only two brief overlaps between a service-provider account and an address that had appeared months earlier in a different threat review.

Jonas had almost closed it.

"Why didn't you?" Daniel asked.

"Because the second overlap was yesterday."

"That alone isn't enough."

"I know."

Lena was back in the room. Her access had been restored; the atmosphere had not.

She stepped behind Jonas.

"What's the harmless explanation?"

Jonas opened his notes.

"The service provider works there. The old address is coincidence. The earlier threat connection is closed or belongs to another user."

"And what's independent of that?"

"Nothing yet."

Daniel looked at the address.

Two days earlier, he might have left it open and pulled the next checks in parallel.

After Lena, he wanted more.

"Then we don't close it as harmless," he said. "But we don't make anything out of it until we get a second anchor."

Jonas opened a new line.

"What counts as a second anchor this time?"

Daniel did not answer with *another hit*.

"Something that doesn't just represent the same project information in another form."

"Such as?"

"A current stop from another case. A separately documented presence. A corporate connection that exists independently of the first vehicle. Something whose provenance we can trace without ending up back at our own broad review."

Lena said, "No echo."

"Exactly."

Jonas wrote **independent provenance** beside the open address.

"And if the second office got the same data from us?"

"Then it isn't a second anchor."

"Costs time."

Daniel looked at him.

"Lena spent four hours out of the case today because of a real pattern that was interpreted wrongly."

Jonas nodded.

Daniel pointed at the address.

"So this one earns its second anchor."

That did not mean the broad rule had been wrong.

Only that speed did not become independent merely because two screens said the same thing.

Berg stood at the far end of the room.

"Time?"

"Running."

"It always is."

Daniel looked at him.

"Then let it run this time."

Twenty minutes later, the first response came in.

A Berlin office had stopped someone from exactly this service-provider network that morning.

Routine.

No arrest.

No special situation.

The person had been driving a vehicle that had appeared at another location in an earlier support-network investigation.

Jonas put the photo on the screen.

"Plate, registered keeper, person?"

"Plate matches. Keeper is the service provider. Person isn't the one from our hit."

Lena said, "Then that still isn't a second anchor for the address."

"No," Daniel said.

Jonas nodded and continued calling.

The next response made the matter smaller at first.

The vehicle was used by several employees. The stopped driver had a legitimate assignment in the same city.

"Almost closed," Jonas said.

Daniel looked at the time.

"Almost is open."

Lena gave him a look.

"You can use that in either direction now."

"That's why we need something else."

They waited.

The waiting was not quiet. Other hits ran in parallel. Two were closed. One went to the next shift because a response was missing. The delivery address stayed on Daniel's screen even though nothing new happened.

Then a photo arrived from the traffic stop.

It showed the vehicle location; the driver was not in the frame.

Jonas did not enlarge the image. He pulled the location data from the original stop.

The delivery address was two streets away.

"Still not enough," Daniel said.

Jonas confirmed from the original case record that the time and location had not later been copied in from Daniel's project. Daniel left the point open.

That was slower work than simply believing a clean-looking connection on a screen.

But not slow enough to erase the advantage of the new structure.

That combination worried Daniel more now than a clearly bad process: faster than before, more controllable than his first rule violation, and still capable of drawing people into a false connection.

"No."

"Why was the vehicle there?"

The Berlin colleagues checked.

The driver had said he was picking up material before his shift.

From whom?

He did not remember a name. Only a warehouse bay.

The company confirmed no assignment at that address.

Now it was more than before.

Still no threat connection.

Daniel had the company behind the delivery address checked.

Registry information.

Lease.

Current user.

The company itself was formally unremarkable. But the adjoining storage area was leased by the same company that connected, two steps away, to a previously known support-network contact.

"Old or current?" Daniel asked.

"The support-network contact is known. The corporate link still exists."

"Not enough."

"No."

Lena looked at the vehicle photo.

"But now we have two separate things: a current vehicle there and an existing corporate connection."

"Yes."

Daniel noticed how strongly he reacted to the word *separate*.

What mattered were two separate paths instead of source plus source or correlation plus interpretation.

Jonas opened a current case from another office.

A second person from the service-provider network had been recorded at the same address the previous day. Not because of Daniel's project. A routine stop in a different context.

"Photo?"

"Yes. 5:26 p.m., confirmed."

Daniel looked at Lena.

She nodded.

"That's the second anchor."

Berg came closer.

"Then move."

"Only this strand," Daniel said.

"What about the others?"

"Stay open. No action from correlation alone."

The further review moved quickly.

It moved quickly because the contacts were already established, responsibilities were clear, and the same lawfully available information no longer had to travel sequentially through several offices inside the project. It had nothing to do with additional authority.

The actual user of the storage area was confirmed.

The vehicle had been there the previous day.

A second person from the known network had been there as well.

Still no proof of a specific offense.

But enough to prepare the targeted next step through the responsible office.

During this, Daniel received a message from Jana.

**Everything okay. I'm not at the practice. Call later.**

He read it.

His first impulse was to call back.

Then he looked at the confirmed strand.

Jana was not the evidence.

He put the phone face down.

"Continue," he said.

Before the targeted measure began, Daniel asked to see the path back once more.

"Show me what we'd have without the broad hit."

Jonas opened a second working view.

The current vehicle stop would still have been there. So would the legitimate assignment. The old corporate connection sat in another case. The second current presence at the same address would have been noticed only once someone already had a reason to search for that address.

"So no proof we'd never have found it," Daniel said.

"No."

"Only later."

"Probably."

Lena stood beside him.

"And *probably later* still isn't a blank check for everything that came before."

"I know."

Daniel did not want the sentence to sound defensive.

It did anyway.

The colleagues on site were waiting for formal authorization for their next step. Not long. Still, the minutes were enough for everyone in the room to start watching the same clock again.

Berg asked, "Anything missing?"

Jonas named two confirmations already in hand.

Daniel added the alternative explanation: legitimate service provider, shared vehicle, corporate connection alone not current enough.

"And the second current presence?" Berg asked.

"Independently confirmed."

"Then?"

Daniel looked at Lena.

She said nothing.

She would not take the decision away from him; that was not her job.

"Targeted on this strand," Daniel said. "Not the rest of the group."

The authorization moved forward.

Jonas marked the time.

"Why?" Berg asked.

"So afterward we don't tell ourselves it was all obvious immediately."

No one objected.

Over the next few minutes, the benefit of the project structure showed itself in an ordinary way: questions no longer moved one after another through three distribution chains. The already confirmed vehicle location, corporate assignment, and second current observation were available side by side, each with provenance and timestamp.

No new authority.

No new fact.

Only less friction between facts that already existed.

Then the response came from the field.

**Concrete threat. Further preparatory step interrupted in time.**

No one in the room said anything.

Daniel read the sentence again.

He had understood it. He read it again because he knew what came next.

Berg said it aloud.

"Without the broad review, we don't have the address."

Lena did not answer immediately.

Daniel looked at her.

"Probably not in time," she said finally.

Berg nodded.

No victory.

Lena added, "That doesn't make my hit any more correct."

"No," Daniel said.

"And it doesn't make the find any less real."

"No."

Jonas looked from one to the other.

"Can anyone have one simple conclusion today?"

No one answered.

Daniel sat still.

Only a few hours earlier, the same logic had pushed Lena out of the case.

Now it had exposed a real threat strand the narrow review probably would not have connected in time.

Both were on the same table.

They could not cancel each other out.

That was exactly why Daniel knew the next discussion had become harder.

Harder, not easier.

Jonas sat down again.

"What do I put down as the result of the broad review?"

Daniel looked at the open and closed hits.

"Not: it worked."

"Then what?"

"That it exposed the strand. And that the measure came only after independent confirmation."

Lena looked at him.

"And the misattributions?"

"They belong in the same after-action report."

Jonas nodded and began writing.

Daniel knew why the wording mattered to him. If later all that remained from this evening was the prevented harm, the decision would look cleaner in retrospect than it had been.

The success could remain true.

So could the cost.

Jonas opened the after-action report while they were still in the room.

Under **operational impact** was the storage strand they had found.

Daniel pointed to the next empty line.

"Now the costs."

Jonas looked at Lena, then back at the screen.

"Weber?"

"Yes."

He wrote:

**Weber misattribution; employment consequence despite correction.**

"Lena?"

A short pause.

Then:

**Temporary exclusion of Vogt; operational friction and later clearance.**

Daniel added:

**Additional closed or benign hits: documented, no action.**

Jonas leaned back.

"That reads pretty badly beside the find."

"Is it supposed to read well?"

"No."

Lena said nothing.

Daniel pointed at the heading.

"If later only the storage unit remains in the report, the method will look as though it found exactly the right person out of twenty-seven."

Jonas nodded.

"It didn't."

"Exactly."

The real success remained in the first line.

Daniel did not delete it, qualify it, or put a *but* in front of it.

He only made sure the hits that had led nowhere or wrongly implicated people did not disappear from the story once the benefit was known.

Lena read the paragraph.

"I can work with that."

Daniel did not know whether she meant the method or the report.

Maybe the distinction did not matter right now.

Jonas added the total number of people reviewed under the list.

The number served only as a denominator, not as a success rate.

Daniel looked at it.

One find without the rest of the group told a different story from the same find beside the people for whom nothing relevant remained.

"Leave the number in," he said.

"Even if it makes it look like a lot of wasted effort?"

"Especially then."

The after-action report should not look more efficient than the process had been.

## 32

Mara answered on the second ring.

"Hi."

Daniel was sitting in the car in the parking lot. He had not started the engine.

For the moment, the situation was stable enough that no one needed him.

That had been the definition of free time for days.

"Got a minute?"

"Yes."

He heard dishes and a cabinet door in the background.

"We're done for today."

"Good."

"Not done. But the acute part."

"Understood."

Daniel rubbed a hand over his face.

On the way to the car, he had told himself not to begin with the case.

That was exactly what he had done.

"How was your day?" he asked.

Mara was silent for a second.

"Interesting order."

"I'm practicing."

"Mediocre."

He laughed briefly.

Then it went quiet again.

"When do you have to give Hamburg your final answer?"

Mara did not answer immediately. The pause was enough.

"Today."

Daniel looked through the windshield at the dark windows of the building.

"Today by when?"

"Three hours ago."

"And?"

"I accepted."

Daniel rested his head against the seat.

There was no shock.

More like a brief emptiness in which his mind immediately started calculating.

"Start date still November first?"

"Yes."

"Apartment?"

"Two viewings next week."

"Furnished at first?"

"One of them."

"Commuting initially would still—"

Mara said nothing.

Daniel heard himself.

He stopped.

"Okay."

"You're allowed to ask."

"I know."

"I just wanted to let you finish calculating first."

"Was I that bad?"

"Start date, apartment, commuting in twelve seconds."

"Solid."

"For a freight company, maybe."

He laughed again, shorter this time.

Then he asked, "Are you happy?"

Mara exhaled.

"Yes."

The word did more than Daniel expected.

"Very?"

"Enough that I accepted."

"What does enough mean?"

"That I want the role. The team. The responsibility. And that I don't need a perfect solution to know I want to try it."

"What happens your first week?" Daniel asked.

Mara laughed softly.

"Is that a trick question?"

"No. I'm trying to understand what you're looking forward to."

This time the answer came faster.

"Two days of handover. Then a meeting with the new team. After that I go with my future boss to see a client. And somewhere in between I try to find out which apartment isn't directly on a six-lane road."

Daniel pictured her opening boxes in a strange kitchen while he sat in Berlin.

The image was surprisingly concrete.

"The furnished apartment?"

"Is practical."

"But?"

"It looks like a hotel that forgot people sometimes stay longer than three nights."

"Then the other one."

"More expensive."

Daniel was already opening his mouth.

Mara said, "No calculation."

He closed it again.

"I only wanted—"

"I know."

Daniel smiled despite everything.

"Difficult."

"You're learning."

"Slowly."

Mara told him about the second apartment. Small balcony. Longer commute to the office. But a room where she could actually fit a desk. She had not decided anything yet.

Daniel listened. Not all of it was about him.

He did not ask about square meters or lease duration.

"What's the first thing you're really looking forward to in Hamburg?"

She was quiet briefly.

"Is that a trick question?"

"No."

"Then probably the team. Building the structure myself and deciding how we work instead of walking into something finished and only fixing it."

Daniel heard the word *deciding* and this time let it stay where it was.

"And outside work?"

"Being able to walk out in the morning and get coffee, assuming the second apartment isn't completely lying."

"That's your big Hamburg picture?"

"Right now, yes."

He smiled.

It was ordinary enough that he understood how far Mara's decision had already grown beyond the abstract question of *Hamburg or Berlin*.

She was thinking about a team, a first client, a kitchen, a walk for coffee.

Until today, Daniel had mainly been thinking about distance.

"Sounds good," he said.

"It does."

This time he did not immediately turn it into a weekend schedule.

"And your team here?" he asked.

"I tell my boss tomorrow. Friday the others. My deputy gets more responsibility if everything works out."

"She happy about that?"

"First she'll tell me I'm crazy. Then she'll be happy."

Daniel knew the colleague only from two stories. He still noticed that Mara had already been building transitions while he was still thinking about when the two of them would find time to make a decision.

"I could come to Hamburg with you next weekend," he said.

Mara was quiet.

Daniel heard himself and added, "Not as a solution. Only if you want me to."

"That would be nice."

She made nothing larger out of it.

"I don't know how often I'll come back to Berlin at first," Mara said.

A calendar opened immediately in his head.

"Not at all the first two weeks?"

"No idea."

"After that every other—"

He stopped himself.

Mara said nothing.

"Never mind," Daniel said.

"What?"

"I'm trying to turn a relationship into a timetable."

"You're trying to find out whether it works in practice."

"That's not entirely wrong."

"No. Just a little early."

Daniel looked through the windshield.

"Then no weekend rule."

"Thank you."

"That sounded very relieved."

"It was."

He smiled.

Mara said, "I don't want to move to Hamburg and immediately have to prove every week that Hamburg isn't changing anything between us."

Daniel answered after a moment. "Something is changing."

"Yes."

"Doesn't mean it's over."

"No."

They did not have more certainty than that.

This time Daniel did not try to replace it with an appointment.

This time the calendar stayed closed. The pause was allowed to exist.

Mara heard it too.

"You're not calculating?"

"Trying not to."

"How does that feel?"

"Unprofessional."

She laughed softly.

Daniel asked, "What do you need from me in the first few weeks? Not organizationally."

Mara needed a moment.

"To be allowed to arrive without proving every week that Hamburg works for us."

Daniel looked at the dark windshield.

"So no review after two weeks."

"Please not."

"And if it's difficult?"

"Then it's difficult. That's not a decision against us yet."

Daniel nodded.

Normally he would have forced the uncertainty into a rhythm immediately: Friday evening train, Sunday back, every other weekend. A plan that looked reassuring simply because it existed.

Mara wanted something else.

Time in which the new reality was allowed to become real first.

"Okay," Daniel said.

This time she did not ask whether he meant it.

"And you?" Mara asked.

Daniel needed a moment.

"Me what?"

"What does your next week look like?"

He looked at the dark building in front of him.

"I don't know."

"At all?"

"Seven-thirty tomorrow morning. After that it depends on the situation."

Mara was quiet briefly.

The explanation was already prepared. He did not give it.

"Then you don't know," she said.

"Yes."

"That's information too."

Daniel smiled weakly.

"We're remarkably good today at honest bad answers."

"Maybe we need those more right now than good invented ones."

Daniel said nothing to that.

It was not a promise for Hamburg.

But it was a conversation in which Mara did not have to be the only one explaining her new life.

Nor did Daniel have to invent his.

Daniel looked at his hands.

She had told him that in the first conversation already, only differently. Back then he had asked about work from home.

"When are you telling your current team?"

"My boss tomorrow. Then the team."

"Resignation?"

"Prepared."

"Both apartment viewings next week?"

Mara was silent.

Daniel grimaced.

"Again."

"A little."

"Sorry."

"You don't have to apologize for thinking practically."

"Only if that's all I do."

"That would be a useful rule."

Daniel rested his head back.

"You could have texted me."

"I considered it."

"Why didn't you?"

"Because there was nothing you could have done except hang between two things."

"Maybe I could have said something."

"What?"

He thought about it.

Too long.

Mara said, "Exactly."

"That's unfair."

"Yes."

The answer came so quickly he almost laughed again.

"I didn't accept because you weren't there," she said. "I accepted because I want the job and because I had to decide today."

"Would you have decided differently if we'd had the appointment?"

Mara was silent for a moment.

"Maybe we would have understood better what it means for us."

"That wasn't the question."

"That's all I can honestly tell you."

Daniel nodded even though she could not see it.

No accusation.

No easy alternative past.

"And us?" he asked.

"We're not automatically gone."

"But?"

"But the question is different now."

"Not whether you're going anymore."

"Exactly."

"What matters is how we deal with it."

"Yes."

Daniel thought about train connections, weekends, two apartments, calendars.

He noticed the list and this time left it in his head.

"I don't know how right now," he said.

Mara was quiet briefly.

"At least that's an answer."

"Not a good one."

"It doesn't have to be today."

"You needed one today."

"For my job. Not for everything."

Daniel looked at the time and immediately turned his wrist away again.

"I'm happy for you," he said.

"I believe you."

"Doesn't sound like it."

"It does."

"Then what sounds like it doesn't?"

"That I'm tired anyway."

"Because of me?"

"Among other things. Hamburg too. And because I've spent days preparing decisions."

Daniel accepted that.

"I don't want you thinking I just let you go."

"I don't."

"Then what?"

"That you had good reasons to want to decide later."

"And?"

"My deadline still had a date."

Daniel was silent.

Mara did not have to explain the sentence.

"We'll talk tomorrow," he said.

Mara laughed softly once.

The laugh sounded tired, without malice.

Daniel grimaced.

"Wrong sentence."

"A little."

"I'll contact you tomorrow. No promise of a time."

"Better."

"And if it doesn't work again, I'll tell you beforehand."

"Even better."

This time they said goodbye.

Daniel put the phone on the passenger seat.

For a moment he felt something almost like relief.

A decision had been made.

It belonged to someone else.

Then he disliked himself for the relief.

He started the engine.

Mara was going to Hamburg.

What happened to the two of them remained open.

But the old version of their joint decision was not coming back.

## 33

"You said we'd scale it back."

Lena stood on the other side of the table.

Berg sat between them without making the arrangement feel like mediation.

Jonas had placed three documents side by side.

The source message.

The project record for the new delivery authorization.

Daniel's own note defining the return boundary.

Daniel knew every document.

Together they looked worse.

Lena tapped the source message.

**Don't check only the name. Check the access around it.**

Underneath was the identifier for the new delivery authorization.
