Daniel had repeated it.

"Area not confirmed. Twenty seconds. We pull the people out or change the access route."

Heller had been standing beside him.

Not as operational commander.

Not armed.

Only with the same secured access to situation communications he had had for days as coordinator.

"We don't have that time," he had said.

Daniel had looked at him.

"That's not your decision."

"No."

Heller had glanced at his terminal.

"But the previous assessment is solid."

"It was."

"The new information doesn't change the core threat."

"It changes who we may encounter there."

Heller had said nothing to that.

In Daniel's earpiece, operational command asked again for status.

Daniel answered, "Not confirmed. Hold."

Heller raised the terminal in his left hand.

Daniel saw the prepared message on the display.

**Target attribution from investigative strand confirmed. Tactical plan unchanged.**

Not an order.

But Daniel knew what the sentence would mean inside a sequence already running on the old confirmation.

Operational command would receive two contradictory professional assessments. In an operation already aligned to the earlier one.

"Leave it," Daniel had said.

Heller had looked at him.

Calm.

Almost tired.

"If we stop now, we lose them."

"Don't send it."

"Reuter."

"Don't."

Heller's thumb rested over the send field.

Daniel saw more than Heller's hand.

He saw the distance to him.

Three meters of wet concrete.

One step beneath the canopy, two out into the rain.

If Heller stayed still, the distance was ridiculously small.

If he completed the action with one movement, it was too large.

Daniel could move.

He could grab Heller by the forearm.

He could knock the terminal away.

At the same time he could tell the channel that any message from Heller on this matter was not part of the current investigative assessment.

None of those options was absurd.

That was exactly why they would matter later.

Daniel took one step.

"Markus. Keep the device down."

Heller looked at him.

"You're stopping the operation because of a personnel change."

"I'm stopping an old assessment."

"Same result."

"No."

"For the people outside, yes."

Daniel heard a query from operational command in his ear. One of the eight people had been located. Not all of them yet.

He pressed the talk button.

"The withdrawal stands."

Heller waited until Daniel released it.

"And if the vehicle is gone by then?"

Daniel did not answer immediately.

The question was legitimate.

That made it more dangerous.

"Then that's the cost of correcting the picture."

"Eight possibly uninvolved people against confirmed preparation."

"You're turning them into an equation."

"So are you."

Daniel felt the sentence land.

Not as revelation.

As fact.

Every decision that night had involved weighting something.

The only difference was which uncertainty a person was willing to carry.

Heller lowered his eyes to the terminal again.

Daniel took the second step.

Now he could have lunged.

The rain made the concrete slick. Not so slick that a lunge was impossible. Only enough that Daniel could not treat the first step as a guaranteed closing of distance.

Heller was not in a fighting stance.

He was not attacking Daniel.

His body was almost still.

Only the thumb was positioned where one movement would be enough.

Daniel saw the edge of the terminal in Heller's hand, the distance between them, and the open space in between.

Three meters.

Maybe less.

A fast step, a hand on the forearm, a strike against the device.

All possible.

None certain to be fast enough.

It was equally possible that Daniel's withdrawal on the channel would override Heller's prepared message even if Heller sent it.

Daniel did not know how operational command would handle two contradictory professional messages in exactly those seconds.

He knew only that his withdrawal was already out there.

Which meant the option of doing nothing physical also existed.

Heller glanced at the display.

Daniel heard his own breathing louder than the radio traffic.

Heller's thumb moved.

Daniel drew his weapon.

The shot.

Now Heller was on the ground in front of him.

Rain struck Daniel's face.

The terminal had slipped from Heller's hand and lay a meter away.

Daniel walked toward it without lowering the pistol.

The prepared message was still open on the display.

It had not been sent.

The open field on the terminal stayed in Daniel's vision.

Not sent.

He needed a moment to understand that those two words now mattered more than anything else about the device.

Operational command asked for status on the channel.

Daniel forced his voice into the same tone it had had before the shot.

"The previous assessment remains withdrawn. The new personnel information is sufficiently confirmed to require the correction. Other offender anchors unchanged."

"Understood."

"Heller?"

Daniel looked at him.

"Not part of the situation assessment."

He said nothing more.

The question of Heller now belonged to someone else.

Daniel took two steps back to give the teams coming from the building room.

Only then did he notice how tightly his right hand was clenched.

He opened it.

The channel continued in his ear.

Another uninvolved person located.

New route under review.

Vehicle status unchanged.

The case had not cared that something irreversible had just happened.

Daniel could not afford to either. Not yet.

Heller made a sound.

Daniel looked at him.

Blood ran dark through the water beneath his jacket.

Daniel did not holster the pistol.

"Medical to West Coordination," he said into the channel. "Gunshot wound. One person."

Heller turned his head slightly.

His mouth moved.

Daniel did not move closer to hear whether he was saying anything.

A door behind the strip of glass flew open. Two officers came outside.

Daniel stepped back.

Now he lowered the pistol.

He took another step back.

The world was full of ordinary procedures again.

Someone spoke with operational command.

Someone secured the terminal.

Someone asked Daniel's name even though everyone here knew who he was.

He answered automatically.

His eyes stayed on the wet concrete between himself and Heller.

Three meters.

Maybe less.

The distance had not become greater after the shot. It had only become measurable.

Daniel knew that later he would talk about exactly that.

Whether he could have run at Heller.

Whether he could have reached the terminal in time.

Whether his withdrawal on the channel would have been enough.

None of those possibilities had disappeared because of the shot.

They had only become impossible to test.

The next operational report came through his earpiece.

Daniel forced himself to listen.

The area with the eight employees was being reorganized.

Several people had now been located.

The alternative route had not yet been established.

The operation had not been won.

Nothing about Heller lying on the ground had solved the situation outside.

Daniel looked up.

"Who's consolidating the new situation assessment now?"

A voice named the responsible lead.

"My last confirmed basis is there in full—including the withdrawal?"

"Yes."

Daniel had the answer repeated.

He had heard it perfectly the first time. He needed the repetition as a clean point at which responsibility for the ongoing operation was back where it belonged.

One of the officers knelt beside Heller.

The other looked at Daniel.

"Your weapon."

Daniel knew what normally had to happen next.

Secure.

Hand over.

Separate.

Document.

Daniel knew the procedure after a shooting from regulations, exercises, and other people's reports.

Now it did not feel like a procedure.

The officer in front of him waited for the weapon.

At the same time, a situation was still running in Daniel's ear for which he held information no one else knew in exactly the same sequence.

He could not mix the two.

"My weapon goes to you," he said. "I'll give the final investigative assessment on the channel first. Then I'm out as soon as operational command takes over."

The officer gave a short nod.

Daniel realized he needed to explain the sentence to himself.

The shot gave him no additional authority.

If anything, it took authority away.

What was still needed from him was the last clean state of his investigative strand. He did not command the teams outside.

Daniel passed on the situation one sentence at a time.

He noticed he was speaking more slowly than usual.

Not less clearly. More slowly because every word now had two functions: the live operation had to be able to work with it, and later it had to remain reconstructable what Daniel actually knew at this moment.

"Confirmed offender anchors unchanged," he said. "Area assessment withdrawn because of new uninvolved-personnel information. Heller's counter-message is not part of my assessment."

Operational command confirmed each point separately.

Daniel waited for the final "understood."

Only then did he know the shooting would not compound itself through communication chaos.

He repeated the confirmed offender anchors and the withdrawn area assessment once more.

No interpretation of Heller.

No justification.

Only the status.

In his ear, Jonas said, "Daniel, we have the second location."

Daniel looked at the officer in front of him.

"Take the weapon."

He held it out by the grip with two fingers.

Then he pressed the earpiece more firmly into place.

"Go."

## 41

"Not the source," Daniel said. "What do we have ourselves?"

Jonas was out of breath.

"The vehicle from the current authorization has been stopped. The two people inside match our independently confirmed strand. Second location is a service entrance west of the event grounds."

Daniel stood beneath the canopy, now without a weapon.

Behind him, paramedics were working on Heller.

He did not look.

"How did you get the location?"

"From the vehicle. Regular documents. Delivery slot and access point. The organizer confirms the slot."

"Heller?"

"Not needed."

"What about his second service chain?"

"Partly overlaps."

"That wasn't my question."

A short pause.

"Part of it isn't confirmed."

"Then it stays out."

"Understood."

Daniel did not let Jonas leave the second location standing as a finished connection.

"Show me the path."

"From the stopped vehicle?"

"From the first independent point to here."

Jonas switched views.

First, the regular documents in the vehicle.

A delivery slot.

A service entrance.

Then confirmation from the organizer that the slot actually existed and had not been copied afterward from some source tip.

Then the identifier for the access point.

It matched the area they already knew from the authorization with the moved-up window.

"And which part of that comes from the anonymous message?"

"Nothing we need for this location."

Daniel pointed to a second marker.

"This one?"

Jonas hesitated.

"Would be faster. Heller mentioned a connection to another entrance."

"Independent?"

"Not yet."

"Then no."

"Even though it's closer?"

"Especially then."

Jonas removed the marker from the working window.

Daniel felt the lost seconds like physical resistance.

A shortcut lay visibly in front of them.

Maybe even the right one.

It stayed out anyway.

Lena said quietly, "This is the part you asked for earlier."

Daniel looked at her.

"Which part?"

"Making the information earn its justification."

He only nodded.

Lena came through the door.

Her hair was wet against her forehead.

She looked past Daniel toward Heller.

Then back.

"You're not hurt?"

"No."

"Heller?"

Daniel shook his head. "I don't know."

Neither said more.

On the channel, operational command reported the changed route.

The area with the eight employees was not entered through the old access point. A second team took over clearing them. The operational step shifted.

The changed route did not emerge from a single command.

First they confirmed which of the eight employees had already left the critical area.

Then the access route was reassessed.

One team held, another shifted.

Daniel heard only the outcomes, not every tactical intermediate step.

That was enough.

"Which part of this depends on our investigative picture?" he asked.

Jonas answered, "Service entrance, vehicle, access material. Not the exact route."

"Good."

Daniel did not want his professional role to become an operational command function in retrospect merely because he had triggered the withdrawal.

His task remained narrower.

Which information carried weight.

Which did not.

Which connection was strong enough to justify the next review point.

The first person at the second location could be connected through current access and the stopped vehicle.

The second could not immediately.

"Then treat them separately," Daniel said.

Jonas confirmed.

Berg asked over the channel, "Even now?"

"Especially now."

A few seconds later the next response came in.

The second person had no solid independent link to the older support strand. He was there through the same service provider.

Daniel felt something almost like relief.

The situation had not become smaller at all. What relieved him was that a counterhypothesis had survived.

The shared structure had made the name visible quickly.

Human review had kept visibility from becoming guilt immediately.

"Take him out of the suspect category," Daniel said.

Jonas did.

Shortly afterward, the specialists confirmed the other part.

Material, access, and preparation fit together.

Not completely.

But enough.

Daniel looked at the time and realized he had not looked at Heller for several minutes.

He did not know whether that was concentration or escape.

It did not matter yet.

The situation was still live.

When it was finally established that the planned next step could no longer take place, Daniel stayed on the channel.

He had the open points read back to him.

People remained unclear, connections open, parts of Heller's warning unconfirmed.

The core was enough anyway.

Major harm had very likely been prevented.

And the unconfirmed parts remained unconfirmed.

Both had to go into the same file later.

Daniel had Jonas add one more thing.

"Timestamp every discarded lead."

"Why?"

"So nobody later pretends we saw only the right hits."

Jonas understood immediately.

The report would become longer and more uncomfortable because of it.

A false duplicate badge.

A plausible driver.

A person whose contact initially looked suspicious and did not hold up.

Beside them, the few connections that had actually supported measures.

Daniel watched the list form.

The working method had functioned.

But only because people had repeatedly decided **not** to act on what the system made visible.

That too belonged to the success.

It took only seconds, but Daniel heard every one of them.

"Vehicle at the second location," Jonas said.

"Confirmed?"

"Yes. Two at the vehicle, at least one more in the service area. Still no complete picture."

Daniel looked through the rain toward the lights of the grounds.

"Access documents?"

"Match the material from the warehouse strand."

"Independent?"

"Yes."

Lena stood beside him.

"You're asking that after every sentence now."

"Yes."

"Good."

Daniel listened to the next reports.

No images.

No heroic shouting.

Only short sentences.

Person checked.

Access secured.

Vehicle opened.

Specialists taking over material assessment.

Event operations on the affected service axis suspended.

Then nothing for almost a minute.

The minute without an update stretched because no one filled it.

Daniel heard only the rain, radio traffic, and somewhere behind the door the wheels of a stretcher.

He did not look.

A technical query came over the channel first, then silence again.

Jonas said, "Connection holds."

"Which one?"

"Vehicle to service entrance. Access documents to material from the warehouse strand. Person one to the independently confirmed support-network contact. Person two still open."

"Then leave him open."

Berg cut in.

"Through the shared structure we have a name with contact to both vehicles."

Daniel closed his eyes briefly.

"Direct?"

"A current contact point."

"So not direct. Counterhypothesis?"

"Work-related contact through the service provider."

"Then no step based on that alone."

Berg exhaled audibly.

"Understood."

Daniel opened his eyes again.

That was exactly why they had limited the structure.

It could see faster.

It could not judge faster.

Daniel therefore had Jonas lead every new report with its origin.

Heller's message no longer decided which lead stayed on top. The order came from independent responses. If one of them collapsed, the situation picture had to shrink too—even now, even immediately before intervention. No hit could weigh more simply because it fit the final minutes.

"Service entrance, person one, person two?"

"Service entrance: documents from the stopped vehicle plus organizer confirmation. Person one: current access and independently confirmed support-network contact. Person two: shared service provider only."

"Then open."

Jonas began constructing the sentences that way himself before Daniel asked.

He phrased the next one more narrowly: *We have a current contact point; counterhypothesis: work-related relationship.*

Daniel read the same line twice. The letters were sharp; only his mind was back beneath the canopy for two seconds: Heller's hand, the shot, wet concrete. Then he forced himself back to the screen.

Daniel pressed his left hand beneath the edge of the table until the shaking diminished.

"Continue."

Lena saw it.

She said nothing.

That helped.

The shooting could not suddenly make Heller's tips less credible or Daniel's own strands more credible.

Every report had to earn the same path it had three minutes earlier.

Precisely because Daniel himself was no longer the same person he had been three minutes earlier.

Seconds later, the first solid report arrived from the service area.

The first crack appeared in Heller's picture already.

One expected person was not there.

Part of the assumed connection was missing.

But the secured material, real access, and altered time window were enough to support the threat core.

Relief did not come. Almost worse was that reality was once again not clean enough to morally rescue either side.

Daniel's left hand trembled slightly beneath the table.

Berg came onto the channel.

"Daniel?"

"Here."

"Another hit from the project structure. Person in the defined access group, current contact to the stopped vehicle."

"What's the counterhypothesis?"

Berg was silent.

Daniel closed his eyes.

"Thomas."

"Work-related contact through the same service provider."

"Then don't touch him until you have more."

"Time?"

"We have other anchors."

Berg said nothing more.

A few minutes later, operational command confirmed that both vehicles and the service area were secured.

The specialists gave their first assessment.

The secured material belonged to the same preparatory complex they had been finding piece by piece over the previous days. Combined with the access rights and the moved-up time window, it created a specific threat to the event.

The planned next step could no longer take place.

Daniel asked, "Major harm prevented?"

The answer did not come immediately.

"Based on what we know now: yes."

This time Daniel removed the earpiece completely.

Only for three seconds.

The world did not become quiet.

Rain on metal.

Footsteps behind him.

A door.

Then he put it back in.

Major harm had, on the current information, been prevented. The decisive contribution had come from the working method Daniel himself had continued and limited. Heller's final message did not explain that outcome any more than simple luck excused the problematic decisions.

Daniel had no formula for it.

Only the fact.

It had worked.

And none of that made the ground behind him any cleaner.

He took the earpiece out again.

Only from one ear.

Behind him, the stretcher was lifted.

Daniel looked.

Heller's face was white beneath the canopy light. An oxygen mask covered it. His eyes were closed.

No conversation.

No look.

The stretcher disappeared through the door.

Lena leaned back against the wall.

"It worked," Berg said from the speaker on the table.

No one answered.

Daniel heard Berg's sentence again in his head.

**It worked.**

He wanted to object immediately.

The sentence was not false. Daniel objected because it was too large.

What exactly had worked?

The independent confirmation before intervention.

The parallel review.

The limitation to the current access group.

The rejection of several false and ordinary hits.

The changed route after the new information.

Those parts did not all belong in the same moral equation.

Daniel said into the channel, "Document separately in the final status: which hits came from the shared structure, which measure rested on what, and which leads were discarded."

Jonas answered, "Will do."

"Heller's unconfirmed parts too."

"Separate."

Only then did Daniel allow the sentence.

Major harm had been prevented.

That remained true.

Daniel looked at the place where Heller had been lying.

Rain was slowly washing the blood toward the drain.

Then he put the earpiece properly back in.

"What's still open?"

## 42

Jana opened the door herself.

Daniel had not expected that.

"You're here?"

"Obviously."

She stood in the doorway of her practice. Jeans, sweater, hair tied back. The hall behind her was lit, but the treatment rooms were dark.

"I thought you were still with Claudia."

"I was."

"And now?"

"I'm here."

Daniel automatically looked down the street.

Jana noticed.

"There isn't a man with binoculars standing in the doorway across the street."

"Very reassuring."

"Wasn't meant to be."

She let him in.

Two cups stood on the table in the small break room. One clean, one not.

"Claudia?" Daniel asked.

"Just left."

"Alone?"

Jana looked at him.

Daniel took off his jacket.

"Okay."

She sat down.

"Is it over?"

Daniel took the chair opposite her.

"The acute threat is."

"What does acute mean?"

"That the concrete strand I told you to change your routines because of has been stopped."

"And the rest?"

"Still under investigation."

Jana nodded.

"Was I a target?"

This time Daniel answered immediately.

"We can't establish that."

Jana waited.

"And the observation?"

"That was real. It probably didn't come from the perpetrator network."

"Say that again and listen to what it sounds like."

Daniel looked at the dirty cup.

"I know."

He explained as much as he could.

Someone from inside their own institutional environment had probably arranged for Daniel to be observed before the case. Later, knowledge about Jana had been used to increase the pressure on him. The external threat had still been real.

Jana watched him for a long time.

"Probably used," she said finally. "What does that mean?"

Daniel put his hands on the table.

"That the information about you was real. Someone knew when you left the building and which routes you took."

"I knew that. What's new?"

"That we have no solid indication the people behind the external threat selected you as a target in your own right."

Jana waited.

Daniel had to build the next sentence more carefully.

"The private reference mainly did something to me. It narrowed my decision space."

"So I was a means to an end."

"Probably in that part."

"Probably."

"I can't say more cleanly than that."

She stood, went to the counter, then came back without taking anything.

"That's almost worse than a clear answer."

"I know."

"No. You like unclear answers professionally. I have to live with them."

Daniel said nothing.

She was right enough that any defense would have sounded wrong.

Jana sat again.

"What part of your warning back then would you give me again today?"

Daniel thought.

"That there was a real reason for caution."

"And what wouldn't you say again?"

"That maximum caution is automatically the right decision."

Jana nodded slowly.

"I can do more with that."

She listened without interrupting him.

"So you didn't know whether I was in danger."

"No."

"And to this day you don't know whether I needed to leave my apartment."

Daniel looked at the dirty cup.

"No."

Jana leaned back.

No triumph.

Only fatigue.

"I still wouldn't have simply carried on like before," she said.

"I know."

"I didn't know that then either."

Daniel looked at her.

"I thought you were about to say I overreacted."

"You probably did."

"Thanks."

"And I probably made it smaller because I didn't want you taking over everything again."

Daniel nodded slowly.

They sat in silence for a moment.

Then he said, "For the next few days I'd still—"

He stopped.

Jana raised her eyebrows.

"What?"

Daniel took out his phone and opened the short information he was allowed to give her.

"There is no current concrete threat information about you. A few connections are still open. Changing routine routes reduces a residual risk. That's all I can honestly tell you."

Jana did not take the phone.

"I can work with that."

Daniel put it away again.

"What do you need from me?" he asked.

Jana thought longer than he expected.

"A rule that applies to information. Not to me."

Daniel waited.

"If you get something new that increases my concrete risk, you tell me exactly what changed."

"As far as I'm allowed to."

"Yes."

"And if all we have is another theory?"

"Then you don't automatically tell me to make my life even narrower."

Daniel nodded.

"If the observation happens again?"

"Then that's new information."

"If someone internal just says it might?"

"Then it's a somebody-says-it-might."

Daniel had to smile briefly.

"Technical term?"

"You should introduce it."

Jana became serious again.

"I don't want less information. I just don't want every uncertainty to arrive as an instruction to me."

Daniel looked at her phone, the calendar, the practice around her.

"Understood."

This time, giving information did not mean giving away responsibility.

It meant giving Jana enough of it for the decision to genuinely remain hers.

Jana looked at him as though she had not expected the question.

"Right now?"

"Yes."

"No additional rules."

Daniel nodded.

"Information if something changes."

"Yes."

"And if you don't know something, don't automatically give me the more dangerous version just because it sounds safer."

Daniel had to look away briefly.

"That'll be harder."

"Then practice."

"Very constructive."

"You're welcome."

Jana took a sheet of paper from the table and wrote down three points.

"So you don't ask again immediately."

Daniel looked.

**Claudia at closing. Vary route. Changes only with new information.**

"What does the last one mean?"

"That I don't invent a new protective measure every day just because theoretically we could do more."

Daniel nodded.

"And if I tell you the situation has worsened?"

"Then tell me why."

"And if all I can say is that it's less clear?"

Jana looked at him.

"Then I decide with unclear information."

Daniel had to smile briefly.

"Sounds familiar."

"It should."

She slid the paper toward him, then immediately pulled it back.

"This is not clearance for your file, by the way."

"Shame."

"My rules."

"Understood."

"For real this time?"

Daniel nodded.

The difference looked small and yet reached into every decision she would make next: no checklist to work through anymore, only the current state. What Jana did with it remained hers.

She stood and opened the small calendar on the wall.

Monday was full.

Two appointments in the morning, three in the afternoon, an administrative block in between.

Daniel had spent so many days thinking in time windows that the ordinary calendar almost irritated him.

"You're really planning to just work."

"Yes."

"Good."

Jana looked at him.

"That was almost permission."

"It wasn't."

"Close."

Daniel let it stand.

"Good."

"I'm still staying closed tomorrow."

Jana reached for her own phone.

"I told Claudia I won't close alone for the next three evenings."

Daniel started to say something.

She raised one finger.

"My decision."

"I understood."

"And I won't take the same route every time."

"You already said that."

"I'm repeating it so you don't add three more things."

Daniel leaned back.

"Fair."

"Monday I open normally. If something changes before then, you tell me exactly what. Not just that I should be careful."

"Yes."

"And if nothing changes, I keep deciding for myself."

Daniel nodded.

This time she said nothing about it.

Daniel almost nodded again, then stopped himself.

"And Monday?"

"I work."

"Alone?"

"Daniel."

"Information, not instruction."

She had to laugh briefly.

"Claudia's there. And for the next few days I won't drive exactly the same route every evening."

"Okay."

Jana stood and picked up the dirty cup.

"Oh. The apartment."

Daniel looked at her.

"What about it?"

"The realtor put in the new price. Photographer was there yesterday. No notary appointment yet."

"Without me?"

She stopped by the sink.

"You sent me the power of attorney."

Daniel remembered.

At some point between two situation briefings.

"Right."

"I used it."

"Apparently."

"The photographer was a disaster, by the way," Jana said.

Daniel blinked.

"What?"

"Apartment. Photos. He wanted the kitchen to look bigger and took the door off its hinges."

Daniel laughed, though he had not expected to.

"Did he put it back?"

"After discussion."

"Then you were adequately protected."

"Very funny."

Jana rinsed the cup.

Their mother's apartment had continued to exist while Daniel had believed the case was consuming every available reality: realtor, photos, power of attorney, another appointment. None of it was dramatic. It was simply life that did not need clearance from the situation he was managing.

Jana put the cup in the sink.

"You can come to the next appointment if you want."

"Send me the time."

She turned toward him.

Daniel raised a hand.

"I didn't say you should wait for me."

"Progress."

"Don't exaggerate."

This time she really laughed.

Daniel stayed another ten minutes.

Jana told him about two patients she absolutely wanted to see Monday.

She did not discuss their reasons, only that she had moved appointments and one of them had already called twice.

Daniel listened.

It was nothing he could solve.

That was exactly why it felt good.

When his phone vibrated once, he checked the display.

No acute report.

He put it away again.

Jana noticed but did not comment.

Then he left.

Jana closed the door behind him.

Not because he had told her to.

## 43

Heller died the next morning at 5:18 a.m.

Daniel got the news in the corridor outside the conference room.

Berg said only, "He's dead."

Daniel looked at him.

"When?"

"Twenty minutes ago."

"Who knows?"

"Enough people."

Daniel put the phone away.

Nothing more came.

Nothing more came from Heller either. No message. No explanation.

Three folders lay on the table in the conference room.

The head of the internal review pointed to them.

"We separate this."

Daniel sat beside Lena.

Berg sat opposite them.

"First: Mr. Heller's information steering, accesses, project activity, and the preliminary review of Mr. Reuter."

Her hand rested on the first folder.

"Second: Mr. Reuter's own procedural violations and yesterday's shooting."

Second folder.

"Third: the operational impact of the working structure created during the case."

Third folder.

The review lead left all three closed for the moment.

"Before we mix the contents, I need a simple discipline from everyone in this room," she said. "If you use a point from folder one to answer a question from folder two, say so explicitly."

Berg frowned.

"That's artificial."

"No. Artificial would be pretending the operational success settles the question of the shooting. Or that Heller's manipulation settles the question of Mr. Reuter's decisions."

Daniel looked at the edge of the table.

He did not like the separation.

That was precisely why it was necessary.

In his own head, the pieces had long since begun explaining one another.

Heller had created pressure.

Daniel had made decisions under pressure.

People had been protected.

Someone had died.

It would have been easy to turn that into one story.

The three folders prevented it.

Berg folded his arms.

"You can't separate it completely."

"You don't have to," the woman said. "But no answer automatically closes the other two."

Daniel looked at the second folder.

His name was not written on the cover.

Only a case number.

"Let's start with Heller," Lena said.

They laid out the chain.

The early professional assessment and the improper preliminary check on Daniel.

The 8:11 p.m. trace and the anonymous messages.

The plate processed in Heller's working environment before the first real harm and later omitted from the warning.

The project records in which Daniel's successes were subsequently used institutionally.

None of it was complete by itself.

Together, it was difficult to construct another explanation requiring fewer additional assumptions.

"No direct technical attribution of the message channel?" the review lead asked.

"No," Daniel said.

"No confession?"

Daniel looked at her.

"No."

She nodded and wrote something down.

"Then that's how we phrase it."

Then came Daniel's folder.

The first protected cross-case data check.

The later broader review.

The decision not to scale back.

The shooting.

"Before yesterday's expanded review, you knew Mr. Heller had probably helped construct the pressure situation deliberately—and you used the structure anyway?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

Daniel looked at the question as though it were lying on the table.

"Because the final threat was independently confirmed, and in my assessment the narrower path carried a higher time risk."

"Did you have another option?"

"Yes."

"At the shooting too?"

Daniel was silent for a second.

"Yes."

Berg shifted.

Lena did not.

The review lead asked, "Which ones?"

"Close the distance. Stop him physically. Rely on operational command to hold my withdrawal against his message in time."

She wrote the three possibilities down separately.

"At the time, did you consider any of them certain to fail?"

"No."

"Any certain to succeed?"

Daniel looked at the list.

"No."

"Then you were not choosing between possible and impossible."

"No."

"You were choosing between risks under time pressure."

Daniel nodded.

Berg moved in his chair.

The review lead did not look at him.

"For the review, it matters that we do not manufacture certainty backward from the outcome. The fact that the message was not sent does not prove you could certainly have stopped Heller another way. But it also does not prove the shot was the only possible option."

"Yes."

Daniel did not like the sentence.

It was still precise.

"And the project structure?" she asked.

"Same question," Daniel said. "The narrower path existed. I chose not to use it because of the time risk."

"Even though you already knew about Heller's manipulation."

"Yes."

The two decisions were not in the same folder, but they shared the same uncomfortable core.

Daniel had seen alternatives.

He had weighted them.

And he could not reduce responsibility simply because some of the later outcomes favored him.

"Why didn't you use the non-shooting option?"

"Because at that moment I wasn't sure it would work in time."

"Not sure is not the same as ruled out."

"No."

The review lead let the answer stand.

"Let me ask it another way. Did you believe the non-shooting alternative was impossible?"

Daniel looked at the second folder.

"No."

"Practically too uncertain?"

"At that moment: yes."

"That's your assessment from then, not ours from today."

"Correct."

She wrote.

Daniel sensed Berg shift beside the third folder but say nothing.

There was no wording with which Daniel could make the moment smaller without also making it less honest.

He could say Heller had been about to transmit an outdated assessment.

He could say the operational effect would have been immediate.

He could say a physical alternative had existed and its timely success had been uncertain.

After that, the decision was still his.

She wrote again.

No one helped Daniel.

He did not want them to.

Then came the third folder.

Berg opened it.

"The structure delivered the decisive time savings in the final phase."

Lena looked at him. "And we had Nils Weber. Me. Several correlations weighted incorrectly. That belongs in the same balance."

"I'm not disputing that."

The review lead went through the documents.

"The question for the current case will be moot soon anyway. The project structure is time-limited."

Daniel looked up.

"And afterward?"

"That's no longer the same decision."

"What decision is it then?"

"Which elements were operationally useful. Which can be made legally sustainable. Which control thresholds are necessary. What feeds into the ongoing reform effort."

Daniel looked at Lena.

She had heard it too.

Berg said, "That's reasonable."

"Maybe," Lena said.

The review lead continued.

"When the temporary structure expires, its current legal and working basis expires too."

Berg nodded.

"But not the finding that parts of it worked."

"Correct."

Lena said, "And not the finding that parts of it wrongly implicated people."

"Also correct."

The review lead took a blank page from the third folder.

"So the next question will not be whether we extend this exact project. It will be which elements, if any, can be transferred into a regular framework."

Daniel looked up.

"That already answers whether."

"No."

"Partly."

She thought for a moment.

"For individual capabilities, perhaps. Not for every reach, every data source, or every threshold."

It was reasonable.

That was exactly what made the sentence harder.

No one in the room said Heller had been right.

No one said Daniel had been right.

And yet they were no longer discussing whether the working method created in the case should disappear entirely.

They were discussing limits, roles, and controls.

The vocabulary had shifted before a final decision had been made.

Daniel looked at her.

She returned the look.

The new question was already sitting in the room as though the old one had been settled.

Daniel pulled the first folder toward himself again.

"The preliminary check goes in in full."

The review lead nodded.

"Even if that later changes how my own access is evaluated."

"That's the point of the review."

Daniel also pushed forward the documents on the shooting.

"Then everything."

Daniel remained seated as the review lead gathered the documents.

"When will there be an assessment of my shooting?"

"Not today."

"And the other violations?"

"Not today either."

Berg looked at him.

Daniel ignored the look.

"Until then?"

"The usual interim measures apply. You'll be informed separately."

Daniel nodded.

No dramatic suspension.

No immediate exoneration.

Only a process that would last longer than the situation in which everything had happened.

Lena closed her folder.

"And Heller?" she asked.

The review lead looked at the first folder.

"There will be open points there too that his death can no longer close."

Daniel looked at the stack of files.

That was the most uncomfortable form of ending.

No final statement. No clean motive formula. No way to assign Heller everything in retrospect that Daniel himself had decided.

He stood.

The third folder remained on the table.

Operational impact.

It was the only one everyone already discussed as though there would inevitably be another version afterward.

Berg said nothing.

Neither did Lena.

Daniel did not place his hand on any of the three folders.

For the first time, he did not have to solve any of them immediately.

Heller's actions would be reviewed.

Daniel's own decisions would be reviewed too.

And independently, other people would decide what remained of the working structure.

The three processes could reach different conclusions.

That was not disorder.

It was probably the cleanest form still possible after this case.

Before Daniel left the room, the review lead asked, "Do you want to know whether the assessment of the structure affects your own proceeding?"

Daniel stopped.

Of course he wanted to know.

If the working structure was ultimately judged useful, controllable, and suitable for a future regular framework, every decision in the case would read differently in retrospect.

Maybe not legally.

But humanly.

"No," he said.

The review lead raised her eyebrows slightly.

"Why not?"

Daniel looked at the three folders.

"Because that would pull the questions back together again."

Heller could have manipulated him.

Daniel could have violated boundaries.

The working structure could still contain elements other people wanted to retain in the future.

None of those statements justified the next one.

Lena was already standing by the door.

"Good answer," she said.

Daniel looked at her.

"Wasn't a test."

"Those are the worst ones."

The review lead closed Daniel's folder.

"Then you'll receive the assessments separately."

"Good."

Daniel walked toward the door.

He knew separate proceedings did not mean the consequences would remain separate.

Only that no one was allowed to take a good outcome from one folder and use it to close another.

In the corridor, Daniel stopped briefly at the window.

Three folders. Three timelines.

Heller's possible manipulation began before Daniel was assigned the case.

Daniel's own rule violation began later.

The operational impact of the new structure later still.

The sequence alone prevented them from being turned into a single clean cause-and-effect sentence.

Daniel wrote nothing down.

For the first time, it was enough that the separation was visible in the actual chronology and therefore existed as more than a methodological demand.

Anyone who later wanted to make one story out of everything would have to actively ignore those differences.

He kept walking.

Outside the window, people crossed the yard carrying coffee cups.

The rain had stopped.

## 44

Eleven weeks later, it was no longer called a project structure.

It was called an integrated review.

Daniel stopped on the term when he reached page two of the new guidance.

It did not sound threatening at all. Quite the opposite.

It sounded like something you might use to audit a travel-expense claim.

Lena sat two seats away and had already marked five passages.

Berg was turning pages faster.

"This is narrower than what we had in the case."

"In several places," Lena said.

"So that's good."

"I didn't say that."

The presenter put a comparison table on the screen.

It began with concrete changes rather than principles.

**Case nexus:** more narrowly defined.

**Review group:** must be justified in advance.

**Expansion:** second approval.

**Hits:** no automatic action.

**Post-review:** sampling plus mandatory review where individuals faced adverse consequences.

Daniel read the lines twice.

Only when he turned the page did he notice how tightly he was holding the pen. He loosened his fingers and kept reading.

"Who gives the second approval?"

"Depending on the data category, the responsible supervisory authority or the designated oversight office."

"Not the same operational chain?"

"No."

Lena put a check mark beside it.

Berg noticed.

"Another compliment?"

"Not yet."

The presenter continued.

Where hits arose only from old contacts or geographic proximity, an independent check now had to be explicitly documented before any adverse measure could follow.

Daniel thought of Nils Weber.

Then Lena.

He said nothing.

"And under time pressure?" Berg asked.

"The review can run in parallel. But any deviation is logged and must be reviewed afterward."

"That costs time. At the wrong moment, that can matter."

"Yes."

The presenter said it so matter-of-factly that Daniel looked up briefly.

No rule without a price.

Not even the new, cleaner version.

That made it more credible.

Berg exhaled.

Daniel kept reading.

Defined threat nexus.

Documented review group.

Two-stage approval for certain cross-case linkages.

Logging.

Time-limited use.

Oversight review.

Some things Daniel had once decided himself could no longer be decided by one person alone.

Other things that had once been imaginable only under heavy justification pressure now stood in the procedure as a regular option.

"The project access has been shut down completely?" Daniel asked.

A presenter at the other end of the table nodded.

"For three weeks. The data sets were returned or blocked according to the rules. This is separate from that."

Daniel nodded.

That mattered.

It did not make the rest any smaller.

"The case is one of several practical examples," the presenter said. "Not the sole basis."

Berg leaned back.

"But the clearest."

She did not answer.

She did not need to.

Daniel turned to the appendix.

There were examples.

Neither his name nor Heller's appeared. Instead, an abstracted case involving delayed linkage of existing information, later time savings through joint analysis, and prevention of major harm.

He recognized every line.

Lena said, "The independent oversight review is better than before."

Berg smiled. "I'm framing that sentence."

"Then read the next one."

He did.

"The threshold for bringing in an expanded review group is too open for me in two places."

"Of course."

"You wanted praise."

"Not from you."

Daniel listened to them.

Three months earlier, this conversation would have started differently.

Three months earlier, it would have begun with the basic question of whether a linkage like this belonged on the table at all, not with how far it could reach.

Now they were discussing who could authorize it.

How long it could run.

How hits were reviewed.

Those were real improvements.

Daniel knew that.

He had argued for some of them himself.

Later, they reached a passage Daniel had not marked.

**An integrated review may be proposed as a regular review option where a sufficient threat nexus exists.**

Not ordered.

Proposed.

Daniel held the pen over the sentence.

"Before the case, this would have said who may request an exception."

The presenter looked at the passage.

"Possibly."

"Now the review itself is the regular option. Only its reach needs additional approval."

"That's the consequence of the reform process."

"I'm not saying it's wrong."

"I didn't take it that way."

Berg asked, "Then what exactly bothers you?"

Daniel thought briefly.

"That the important shift isn't written in a spectacular sentence."

Lena looked at him.

The presenter was already turning the page.

That was exactly the answer.

No one had to celebrate the new capability.

It was enough that no one had to explain anymore why it was on the table at all.

The presenter slid another page toward him.

"We'd like you to remain in the operational advisory group."

Daniel looked up.

"Why?"

Berg laughed briefly.

"Seriously?"

Daniel ignored him.

The presenter said, "Because you know both sides of the instrument."

"That's a kind formulation."

"It's a factual one."

"And if the review of my shooting reaches a different conclusion?"

"Then your role will be reassessed. The proceedings remain separate."

Again the same separation: Heller's manipulation, Daniel's shooting, operational benefit. None canceled the others.

As the meeting was almost over, the presenter returned to the advisory group.

"This isn't an honorary role," she said. "You'll read cases, edge cases, and cases of wrongful implication. You do not give operational approval."

Daniel nodded.

"How long?"

"Six months initially. New decision after that."

He could have declined.

The shooting was still being reviewed. His own violations had not disappeared. No one needed him in order for the new structure to exist.

That was exactly why withdrawal suddenly seemed too easy.

If he left, he could later say that whatever the instrument became had nothing more to do with him.

That would not be true.

"Six months," Daniel said.

The presenter put a check mark beside his name.

That was all.

Daniel looked at Lena.

"You're staying?"

"For now."

"Why?"

"So Berg doesn't read everything alone."

"Very trust-building," Berg said.

Lena turned the page.

Daniel picked up the pen.

"Me too."

No one made a moment out of it.

The presenter only put a check beside his name.

Then she opened another anonymized edge case from the trial phase.

Not a terrorism case.

A suspicious access to a sensitive technical facility, two conflicting log records, and an employee with a plausible explanation.

An integrated review had been requested and then narrowed again after the first independent check.

"Outcome?" Daniel asked.

"No substantiated threat nexus. Proceeding closed."

Berg looked at the two pages.

"And why are you showing us this one?"

"Because an instrument can't be evaluated only by its hits."

Lena leaned back.

"How many reviews have been closed this way?"

The presenter gave a number.

The number was unspectacular but high enough that Daniel immediately understood how different the later statistics would look if presentations showed only successful linkages.

"Those belong in every evaluation," he said.

"They are included."

Daniel put down his first check mark of his own.

His check was for the requirement to keep even the **decision not to use it** measurable. It was not an endorsement of the integrated review itself.

Then they moved on to the next open formulation.

## 45

Mara no longer had a coffee machine.

Daniel stood in her kitchen in Hamburg looking at a small porcelain pour-over dripper.

"You're serious?"

"For six weeks now."

"Voluntarily?"

"I've survived things here you know nothing about."

She switched on the kettle.

The apartment was smaller than the one they had shared in Berlin, but it did not feel temporary. That had surprised Daniel on his first visit.

Pictures on the walls. Books on the shelves. Shoes in the hallway. A plant stand by the window that Mara would never have bought in Berlin because there had been no room for it.

Nothing was waiting for her to go back.

That morning Mara had already shown him her route to work without meaning to explain it. She knew the bakery downstairs, greeted the man at the kiosk, and stopped at a traffic light even though Daniel had already taken two steps into the street.

"They'll actually run over your feet here," she said.

"In Berlin too."

"You just don't notice there."

Outside her office, a colleague was waiting with two paper cups.

"You must be Daniel."

Mara introduced them briefly.

The two of them immediately began discussing an appointment. Daniel understood only that someone could not make Wednesday and that this had been a problem since yesterday.

Mara did not unpack it for him.

She said goodbye to the colleague and continued walking with Daniel.

"Nice," he said.

"Yes."

"How long have you been working together?"

"Since my second day."

Daniel automatically counted backward, then stopped himself.

There was no meaningful point at which he could catch up on everything that had become important without him.

"I'm having dinner with the team at seven tonight," she said.

Daniel looked at her.

"Tonight?"

"It's been in the calendar since Tuesday."

He took out his phone.

It was.

"I thought that was next week."

"Of course."

"Can I come?"

"If you want."

"Do you want me to?"

Mara thought for a moment.

"Yes. But not if you get up at seven-thirty and leave because Berlin texts you."

"Fair."

"Wasn't a condition."

"Sounded like one."

"Then I learned from you."

Daniel made a face.

His phone vibrated.

A work message.

He read the first line.

A question about the integrated review. No operation. No acute case.

He would once have answered because it would take two minutes.

Mara put the coffee in front of him.

"Do you have to?"

"No."

"Then do what you want."

She sat down.

Daniel tapped the message anyway.

**Seen. Monday is fine.**

Then he put the phone face down.

Mara did not comment.

That afternoon, the plant stand was crooked.

Mara had noticed for days and ignored it.

Daniel knelt beside it and tightened a screw.

"You don't have to rescue that."

"It wobbles."

"For three weeks."

"That doesn't make it better."

"It didn't bother me."

Daniel stopped.

"Want me to stop?"

Mara looked up from the sofa.

"No. You're already doing it now."

He kept tightening the screw.

It was absurdly small.

He still noticed that he had asked.

He had started asking first whether something was his problem at all, rather than whether he was solving it correctly.

Mara said nothing about that either.

When the stand was straight, Daniel put the screwdriver back in the drawer where Mara now kept it. By his third visit, he already knew where that was.

Not home yet. But long past being a guest in a stranger's apartment.

"How's the apartment search for your team?" he asked.

"What apartment search?"

"You said two people are still moving here."

"Oh. One found something in Altona. The other is commuting for now."

Daniel nodded.

There were now many people in Mara's daily life whom he knew only from half-sentences. She was not hiding them from him. He simply had not been there when they became important.

"Next week I'll come Saturday," he said.

"I may be working Saturday morning."

"Then Friday."

Mara looked at him.

"On Friday I don't know yet whether Friday works."

Daniel laughed.

"That's my answer."

"It was."

"Unpleasant."

"A little."

They drank coffee.

Later, they walked to the subway because Mara wanted to show him a store where she had seen a table.

Just before the team dinner, Mara's phone rang.

She looked at the display.

"I have to take this."

Daniel nodded.

Mara stepped aside and spent five minutes talking about a presentation, a rescheduled appointment, and someone who had been waiting for a decision since that morning.

Daniel understood enough to realize she was no longer simply new in Hamburg.

She was part of something there now.

When she hung up, he said, "Problem solved?"

"For today."

"Do you still have to send something?"

"Maybe later."

Daniel waited for the old movement inside himself: change the plan, reorder the time, turn a maybe into a fixed task.

It came.

He let it pass.

"Then let's go eat."

Mara looked at him briefly.

"Yes."

Daniel did not have to make more out of it.

Daniel thought the table was too big.

Mara did too.

They did not buy anything.

Later, on the way to dinner, Daniel asked, "What do we do about Christmas?"

Mara looked at him from the side.

"It's September."

"That's why I'm asking early."

"You're asking early so you can check a decision off the list."

"Possible."

They kept walking.

"I don't know," Mara said. "My sister will probably go to Cologne. I don't know what my work schedule will be yet."

Daniel nodded.

"Then we don't know."

"That was easy."

"Don't get used to it."

Mara smiled.

It was no solution for the next few years.

Only a weekend, a team dinner, a crooked plant stand, and a question that was allowed to stay open.

That evening Daniel went with her to the team dinner.

His phone vibrated twice in his jacket pocket.

The first time he did not look.

The second time he did.

Nothing urgent.

He put it away again.

The next morning, he drove back to Berlin.

Mara stayed in Hamburg.

That was how it was now.

## 46

The case was small enough that Berg had not wanted to give it to Daniel at first.

"Two nighttime accesses, one contact, and a bad gut feeling."

Jonas put the documents on the table.

Daniel looked at him.

"Whose gut feeling?"

"Mine."

"Then we can cross that out first."

Jonas grinned.

"Missed you too."

The case involved a technical subcontractor with access to a federal government building. One of its employees had used a side entrance twice within a week outside his normal shift. There was also an old, weak contact with a person from an extremist support network.

No concrete attack warning.

No acute alert.

A matter that once would have received a few open checks and then either grown or been closed.

Daniel read the times.

"Work reason for the nighttime access?"

"Still open. Technically valid authorization. Only the time is unusual. The contact is two years old."

"Then for now it's two years old."

Jonas nodded.

Lena came in, sat down, and took the short version.

Berg followed.

"What's the question?"

Jonas said, "Whether we start an integrated review early."

Daniel looked at him. What bothered him was precisely how normal the proposal sounded. It did not sound wrong at all.

Berg asked, "Scope?"

"Employees with technical access to the affected section of the building plus known current contact overlaps."

Lena turned to the last page.

"Threshold?"

"Two unusual access events and the old contact aren't enough for the whole group yet."

"Not for me either," Daniel said.

Jonas nodded.

"Then smaller. First the employee and direct functional access around the two time windows."

Lena said, "That holds up better. Time-limited, existing data only, no automatic consequence from hits."

Berg looked at the clock.

"How fast?"

"An hour," Jonas said.

The guidance was not even open on the table.

Jonas knew the thresholds well enough to recite them.

"Current case nexus, defined group, limited period, no automatic consequence from hits."

Lena asked, "Who approves the expansion if you want to go beyond the direct person?"

"A second office."

"Which one?"

Jonas named it.

Lena nodded.

The conversation lasted barely twenty seconds.

Daniel remembered how much friction even the idea of his first access had created.

The friction had not disappeared. It had acquired forms, roles, thresholds, and responsibilities. That made the work better than improvisation and showed at the same time that the instrument was no longer an improvisation at all.

Only when Lena marked one of the nighttime access events did Daniel notice that no one in the room was still asking whether this kind of linkage was fundamentally permissible. They were discussing only the scope.

"There's a maintenance window in the building here," she said.

Jonas looked.

"Officially not until one."

"The access was at 12:44."

"Sixteen minutes."

Berg shrugged.

"Could be preparation."

"Could also be someone arriving early," Daniel said.

Jonas nodded.

"I'll check."

Jonas called the technical operator.

The first nighttime access was explained faster than expected.

A maintenance job had been moved forward by phone at 12:31 a.m. The employee had been in the building at 12:44. The written change had not been entered into the system until later.

"Who confirms that?" Daniel asked.

"Shift supervisor and external client."

"Independently?"

"Yes."

"Then the access isn't suspicious. The documentation is just bad."

Jonas removed the marker. No one objected.

Daniel liked that.

The second access remained open.

There was nothing dramatic about it. It simply did not yet have a clean explanation.

Berg said, "Then the small integrated review on the person and direct functional access."

Lena asked for the time period and data categories.

Jonas answered.

Daniel listened.

Again, no fundamental dispute.

Only a professional choice between tools that were now available.

The meeting moved on.

Just like that.

Daniel looked at the guidance beside Lena's laptop.

**Integrated review.**

No one had to explain the term.

It simply lay on the table.

A coordinator slid a draft form across the table.

"I only need the threat nexus in one sentence."

Jonas dictated it.

She crossed out two words.

"Too broad."

Jonas read the revised version.

"Works."

Daniel looked at the sentence.

Possible misuse of technical access combined with a real but weak contact link.

No major situation.

No state of emergency.

This was exactly the kind of case the new routine had now been built for.

Jonas collected the first work assignments.

Half an hour later, the first results from the small integrated review came back.

They arrived as a list, not as a red alert.

Four access contacts inside the relevant time window.

Three were explained within minutes: shared equipment cart, documented handover, one late shift change.

The fourth remained open.

Jonas showed it on the screen.

"Current contact with our employee, but professionally plausible. They both work on the same system."

"Then the contact alone means nothing," Daniel said.

"Yes."

Lena asked, "What's left independently of that?"

"The second nighttime access."

"So exactly the same open point as before."

Jonas nodded.

The integrated review had surfaced more information in thirty minutes.

It had not automatically made the case bigger.

Not this time.

Berg said, "Then we clarify the access and close it if nothing new comes in."

The instrument did not have to find something every time in order to become normal.

"I'd prepare another proposal in parallel," Jonas said.

Berg asked, "What proposal?"

Jonas looked at Daniel.

"In case the nighttime access can't be explained cleanly."

Daniel leaned back.

"Do it."

Jonas opened a new page.

## 47

"I wouldn't stop with the employee."

Jonas had put the page on the large screen.

Daniel read the first paragraph.

The nighttime access had now been partly explained.

The maintenance window had in fact been moved forward. The employee had had a legitimate reason to be in the building early.

What remained unresolved was the second access three nights earlier. And the old contact remained what it had been: old, but real.

Jonas had not turned that into an alarm.

His proposal was broader anyway.

Daniel did not let Jonas add the open points together.

"The second nighttime access first."

Jonas opened the logs.

11:18 p.m.

Side entrance.

Eight minutes inside the building.

No maintenance order in the regular system.

"Badge unambiguous?"

"Personalized card. Technically possible to hand it over, but prohibited. No video from the corridor. The exterior camera has a maintenance gap during that period."

Berg raised his eyebrows.

Jonas immediately said, "Scheduled camera maintenance. Documented for weeks."

Daniel nodded.

No sign of manipulation.

Only an open access event that was harder to explain because of it.

"Shift schedule?"

"Person officially off. Swap not yet clarified."

Daniel pointed to the old contact.

"And that?"

Jonas opened the old note.

No meeting in some back room.

A shared club event two years earlier. Several dozen people. Later, two digital contacts whose content was no longer available.

"So real," Daniel said.

"Yes."

"And thin."

"Also yes."

The two traces did not become stronger merely because they stood beside each other.

But they did not disappear either.

"Define the scope," Lena said.

"Not just the person and his direct functional access. All employees of the three subcontractors with active access to that section of the building for fourteen days."

Berg looked at the screen.

"How many?"

"Just under one hundred twenty."

Daniel looked up.

"And what do you want to do with them?"

"Check existing contact, time, and access relationships against the relevant records. Only for that period."

Lena asked, "Individual basis for each person?"

"Their shared access to the area at risk."

"That isn't an individual basis."

"No."

Jonas said it without defending himself.

As if that were exactly the point they had to decide.

Lena took Jonas's list of nearly one hundred twenty employees.

"What happens in practice if we approve your proposal?"

Jonas answered without drama.

"Existing access, time, and relevant contact relationships are checked against the relevant records for the defined period. Hits are reviewed manually. If a potentially incriminating hit can't be explained, I'd temporarily suspend that specific access and continue checking."

"For someone against whom there was no individual basis before."

"Then there would be the hit."

"A hit produced by a review they entered only because of proximity to their workplace."

Jonas nodded.

"Yes."

Berg said, "That's exactly the question the new safeguards are there for."

Lena looked at him.

"Safeguards don't answer whether the initial group is too large."

"No. But they make the proposal reviewable."

Daniel heard both sentences.

Both could be true at the same time.

That was the danger.

Jonas had not forgotten that misattributions existed.

He had built them into his procedure.

That had not made the measure harmless.

Only more professional.

Berg asked, "And when there are hits?"

"If a hit isn't immediately plausibly explainable, temporarily suspend access until we've clarified it."

A technical-coordination employee in the room asked, "How quickly could you pull the group?"

Not: are we allowed to do that?

How quickly.

Jonas answered, "With the existing structure, a few hours."

Daniel looked at him.

Jonas noticed.

"What?"

"Why that broad?"

"Because otherwise we end up checking only the person who catches our attention first again."

Daniel said nothing.

Jonas moved to the next point.

"We know the harm is asymmetric. If one hundred nineteen people are unremarkable, nothing happens to them. If we miss the one person abusing the access, we can't undo that later."

Daniel did not know the sentence.

But he knew every part of it.

Lost time.

Incomplete certainty.

The harm of not acting.

An instrument that now existed.

Jonas did not sound like Heller.

He sounded like a good investigator who had learned from a real case.

From Daniel.

Lena asked, "What argues against it?"

Jonas answered immediately.

"Misattributions. Old contacts. Shift overlaps without significance. And we'd be bringing people into a review group solely because of their functional proximity."

"So you have the counterarguments."

"Of course."

"And you still make the proposal."

"Yes."

Berg looked at Daniel.

"What do you think?"

Daniel looked at the two nighttime access events.

One explained.

One open.

One old contact.

No acute warning.

No security guard in the hospital.

No final time window moved forward.

But also no proof there was nothing behind it.

Jonas's threat scenario was not absurd.

That made it harder.

Daniel thought of the first nighttime access.

12:44 a.m.

Sixteen minutes before the written maintenance window.

An hour earlier, it would have appeared on a list of unusual access events.

Now it was explained.

If they had broadly reviewed the one hundred twenty people before that explanation arrived, perhaps more hits like it would have appeared.

The hits would have been real, only without the context that made them ordinary.

Daniel pointed to the line they had already clarified.

"If we'd seen this access yesterday and didn't yet know about the maintenance call—how would your procedure have treated it?"

Jonas thought briefly.

"As a high-priority hit whose alternative explanation needs to be checked immediately."

"Suspend access?"

"Not automatically."

"Why not?"

"Because the plausible work explanation would be the first obvious one."

Daniel nodded.

Jonas could test the alternative explanation.

The problem was not that he had forgotten the old way of working.

The problem was where it now began.

The technical-coordination employee asked again, "So do we check the open explanation first, or run the large group in parallel?"

No one in the room behaved as though the question itself might be fundamentally out of bounds.

Daniel looked at Jonas's proposal.

Then at the clarified nighttime access.

The new starting line was visible between them.

Daniel looked again at the list of nearly one hundred twenty people.

Then at Jonas.

"What's your strongest counterhypothesis?"

Jonas did not hesitate.

"That the second access was work-related too, just documented badly. And that the old contact has no significance whatsoever for today's case."

Daniel nodded.

"What would support that hypothesis?"

Jonas glanced briefly at Lena.

Then back at the screen.

"Shift changes. Maintenance order. Shared device or badge use. And an explanation for why that contact appeared at all back then."

The technical-coordination employee asked, "Do we check that first, or in parallel?"

Daniel looked at the clock.

Then moved it out of his line of sight.

He did not say, "First."

He did not say, "In parallel."

He looked at Jonas.

"How strong is your counterhypothesis?"
